1. Location and neutrality
Carrascon, A. (2026). Location and neutrality. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02604-z
Abstract (original): Abstract I characterize two different senses in which a formal theory of location can be said to be neutral: neutrality with respect to some locational principles, and neutrality with respect to some controversial cases that have been presented as counterexamples to some principles. Theories neutral in the second sense are often weaker than theories neutral in the first. To illustrate this, I consider some popular principles and some of the counterexamples that have been used to argue against them in the literature, including inexact objects, cases of Nested Multilocation, and failures of Additivity. I then show that no currently existing theory of location built on just one primitive is able to account for all the cases that I have discussed. Lastly, I present a theory of location based on just one primitive which can account for all the discussed cases.

3. Redeeming Relative Value: Indexicality and Consequentializing Agent‐Centered Norms
Howard, N.R. (2026). Redeeming Relative Value: Indexicality and Consequentializing Agent‐Centered Norms. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70065
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Robert Nozick doubted forms of consequentialism that employ so‐called “agent‐relative value” because he found them “gimmicky.” This doubt has been sharpened by Don Regan, Mark Schroeder, and others into the objection that agent‐relative value lacks the practical importance we associate with good simpliciter . This sharpened objection has prompted agent‐relative consequentialists to replace the agent‐relative value at the foundation of their view with agent‐relative reasons for desire. This paper argues for two claims. First, the turn to agent‐relative reasons is less promising than it seems. Second, concerns about gimmickiness can be allayed directly by showing how standard decision‐theoretic conceptions of outcomes already relativize what agents evaluate without relativizing value itself.

4. Revenge Russell‐Myhill and the Ramified Hierarchy
Berneri, J. (2026). Revenge Russell‐Myhill and the Ramified Hierarchy. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70119
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The Russell‐Myhill paradox targeting the structuralist view of proposition in classical higher‐order logic is blocked by implementing restrictions associated with an ordinary account of predicativity. However, it has been shown that these restrictions are not safe: “revenge” versions of the paradox show the inconsistency of the structuralist view in what is ordinarily regarded as a predicative setting. This result has been interpreted as suggesting an expressive limitation of the structuralist view. In this paper, I argue otherwise. There is a metaphysical sense in which structured propositions presuppose properties appearing as their constituents. I use this idea to elaborate a more thoroughgoing understanding of predicativity. In this way, the structuralist has the resources to interpret revenge paradoxes as a byproduct of impredicativity and coherently block them by means of a more subtle regimentation on how properties are defined.

5. Collective Self-Rule and Comprehensive Participation: A Critique of Council Democracy
Ree, E.v. (2026). Collective Self-Rule and Comprehensive Participation: A Critique of Council Democracy. Philosophy & Social Criticism.
https://doi.org/10.1177/01914537261481246
Abstract (original): This article critically explores council democracy as a utopian state order, on the basis of anarchist and Marxist works from western Europe and the United States, post-World War 2. As a system of national government, council democracy cannot possibly deliver on its utopian promises. Collective self-rule cannot put an end to domination/hierarchy/rule but will engender another variety of ‘democratic hierarchy’. Comprehensive citizen participation locks citizens in an overregulated ‘democratic bureaucracy’, and delivers no feasible, working formula of government. In a paradox, council democracy is likely greatly to reduce the share of the population participating in the country’s political process, thus carrying less rather than more democratic legitimacy than representative democracy.

8. Equivalence and Determinism in Light of Topologically-Induced Structure
Grimmer, D. & Read, J. (2026). Equivalence and Determinism in Light of Topologically-Induced Structure. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01153-5
Abstract (original): Abstract Contemporary philosophy of spacetime has thus far focused predominantly on the metrical properties of spacetime, with substantially less attention paid to its topological aspects. In this article, we go some way to rectifying this situation, by introducing philosophers to a class of ‘(almost) asymmetric’ manifolds which possess flat geodesically complete Riemannian metrics yet admit only finite (or trivial) isometry groups. The resulting spacetimes exhibit a unique dual nature: they are locally homogeneous (indistinguishable from standard flat space) but globally inhomogeneous, such that every spatial point is individuated from its neighbours. We argue that this topology-induced structure has at least two significant philosophical upshots. First, we show that there are surprising upshots for existing popular notions of theoretical equivalence when one considers theories set on these manifolds: many theories which might appear prima facie inequivalent in fact become categorically equivalent on such topological settings. Second, we demonstrate that theories set on these manifolds satisfy what Manchak et al. (2026) dub “de re* determinism” and “rigidity”, thereby avoiding (e.g.) the indeterminism which some associate with Leibnizian spacetimes.

17. A Constructive Condorcet Jury Theorem
Cresto, E. (2026). A Constructive Condorcet Jury Theorem. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10133
Abstract (original): Abstract There is a well-established research program that relies on the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) to justify democracy and majority voting from an epistemic perspective. This paper explores how the CJT can be extended beyond its standard epistemic interpretation. I propose a version of the CJT that does not presuppose the existence of an objective fact of the matter (or moral fact) on which group members must vote; I call it the “Constructive CJT.” This constructive version of the CJT is designed to show that a close relative of majority voting (here modeled as majority polling followed by individual action) can help bring about outcomes that are desirable independently of the procedure itself. In particular, it helps agents build situations in which cooperative behavior is achieved, as long as certain conditions are met. This provides a middle ground between epistemic and procedural justifications; under certain conditions, majority voting contributes to generating the environment under which desirable outcomes arise.

20. A note on mereological extensionality principles
Morais, H.F. (2026). A note on mereological extensionality principles. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag063
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper is a small contribution to the study of extensionality principles in mereology. We argue that the extensionality of composition logically implies the extensionality of parthood, and that this entailment persists in the presence of weak supplementation even if we restrict the former principle to cases of proper composition.

21. Principles across distant worlds? A reply to Yoshimura
Salein, V. (2026). Principles across distant worlds? A reply to Yoshimura. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag064
Abstract (original): Abstract This discussion note challenges Yoshiki Yoshimura’s recent attempt to defend Scanlonian contractualism against the distant world objection. While Yoshimura’s amended contractualism initially appears capable of avoiding the objection, I argue that his proposal ultimately falls short in certain relevant cases. Moreover, I suggest that attempts to revise the view reveal a deeper problem for Yoshimura’s overall strategy that has important implications for principle-based views more generally.

22. Does the Rule of Law Require the Separation of Powers?
Kyritsis, D. & Lewans, M. (2026). Does the Rule of Law Require the Separation of Powers?. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70061
Abstract (original): The separation of powers is an enigmatic political ideal, particularly in the context of the modern administrative state, in which administrative officials routinely perform a combination of legislative, judicial, and executive functions. In this article we reexamine this ideal by interrogating Adrian Vermeule's provocative claim that the separation of powers is neither necessary nor desirable for the rule of law. We begin by comparing Vermeule's view with the constitutional models of two theorists who share a similar skepticism of the separation of powers – Thomas Hobbes and Carl Schmitt. We argue that Vermeule's view is best understood as a variation of Schmitt's apology for unitary executive power. By contrast, Hobbes emphasises what we call law's systematicity. We then proceed to elaborate this idea. We claim that at the conceptual level law's systematicity issues into a ‘proto‐separation’ of state institutions that together realise the rule of law. Because law is systematic, it also makes normative sense to combine the rule of law and the separation of powers. The separation of powers allows us to articulate the demands of the rule of law through a process of public deliberation and scrutiny. By contrast, a political order that relinquishes its commitment to the separation of powers for the sake of unleashing the power of the executive degrades its commitment to the rule of law as well.

23. Post‐Trial Responsibilities for Participants Implanted With Neurosurgical Devices: A Qualitative Study
Higgins, N. et al. (2026). Post‐Trial Responsibilities for Participants Implanted With Neurosurgical Devices: A Qualitative Study. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70186
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The issue of post‐trial responsibility is about what is owed to research participants after a clinical trial ends, who is responsible, and how long this responsibility must endure. Despite two decades of international guidance on this issue, research involving novel high‐risk interventions—such as implantable neurotechnologies—raises underexplored ethical tensions. Trials of implantable devices carry surgical risks and require access to multidisciplinary teams and specialised clinical infrastructures. These factors can foster participant dependence on investigator teams and trial institutions for post‐trial care and support. With research and development in this area accelerating, actionable ethical guidance on the post‐trial responsibilities of trial stakeholders is urgently needed. Recent normative commentaries have examined the post‐trial responsibilities of clinical trial investigators, who play a central role in study design, trial coordination, and follow‐up. A persistent challenge is demarcating investigator responsibilities of research integrity from those of therapeutic care when developing ethical guidance on post‐trial responsibilities. Recognising the value of empirical engagement for informing further ethical reflection, we conducted qualitative semi‐structured interviews with n = 21 investigators involved in neurosurgical device trials. We contend that investigators frequently experience institutional and regulatory pressures that shape how they interpret and enact responsibilities for participants' post‐trial wellbeing, often in ways that extend beyond standard expectations of clinical research practice.

24. Trapped in the Cave: Toward a Platonic Theory of Ideology
Proios, J.D. (2026). Trapped in the Cave: Toward a Platonic Theory of Ideology. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70118
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper is about showing that Plato has a theory of ideology in the broadly Marxist sense of distorted self‐consciousness of social life. Focusing on the Allegory of the Cave and related passages in the Republic , the paper argues that in the Cave, Plato illustrates the social mediation of human psychology, which contributes to the soul's orientation to sensible reality. Orientation to sensible reality is by itself a condition of psychological bondage, but it becomes specifically ideological when its social mediation meets certain conditions. I argue that social structures are ideological when they distort social reality so as to enclose the soul in its orientation to sensible reality with little ability to escape. The paper argues that Plato develops this framework to critique Athenian cultural and political institutions. Seeing this allows us to separate Plato's critical from his constructive political thought, and we learn about the nature of both by seeing where ideology ends and Plato's ideas about beneficial social arrangements begin. By contrast, previous interpretations have tended to crowd out Plato's theory of ideology by focusing either on the role of ontology or desire in Plato's conception of psychological bondage or on his positive political ideas.

25. Reclaiming Fear in the Age of Climate Breakdown: From the Biopolitics of Catastrophe to Affective Self-Defense
Silberzahn, L. (2026). Reclaiming Fear in the Age of Climate Breakdown: From the Biopolitics of Catastrophe to Affective Self-Defense. Philosophy & Social Criticism.
https://doi.org/10.1177/01914537261481248
Abstract (original): Contemporary diagnoses of resurgent authoritarianism frequently attribute it to the political mobilization of fear, drawing on a long tradition that casts fear as the antithesis of freedom. This article argues that such a framework is conceptually inadequate in the face of climate breakdown. It critically engages scholarship that cautions against environmental fear and catastrophist discourse, identifying three key assumptions underpinning most of these critiques: that environmental fears are politically homologous to other historically theorized forms of fear; that fear directly fosters authoritarian tendencies at the individual level; and that fear consolidates reactionary power while weakening emancipatory movements at the collective level. Against this affective determinism and threat functionalism, the article draws on ecofeminist and anti-militarist thought to reconceptualize fear not as a regressive affect opposed to democratic agency, but as an ambivalent, critical orientation toward shared vulnerability. In this context, affective self-defense emerges as key: a mode of political praxis grounded in the open, collective acknowledgement and articulation of fear - rather than its suppression or instrumentalization - as a first step in the organization of resistance and care.

26. The Ambivalence of Recognition in Sexual Subjectivity
Wijngaarden, V.v. (2026). The Ambivalence of Recognition in Sexual Subjectivity. Philosophy & Social Criticism.
https://doi.org/10.1177/01914537261484989
Abstract (original): This paper addresses the problem of sexual misrecognition: some groups are excluded from being seen as sexually desirable or are hypersexualised. This shapes their sexual subjectivity – how they understand themselves as sexual agents. Despite widespread initiatives calling for recognition in the domain of sexuality, the need for sexual recognition has remained undertheorised in recognition theory. Building on debates on the ambivalence of recognition, I argue that sexuality has a uniquely complex relationship to recognition. While sexual recognition is necessary for subjectivity, it also reinforces subjection to power structures. Moreover, sexuality continually transgresses sociality and subjectivity itself. Using Jeanette Chedda’s erotic story in Damn Horny as a case study, I demonstrate how sexual recognition transgresses ideals of mutuality and equality, and theorise transformative possibilities for cultivating sexual subjectivity differently.

27. Commercial Ties with Authoritarian Regimes in a Changing Global Order: The Need for a Refined Ethical Approach
Kriebitz, A. (2026). Commercial Ties with Authoritarian Regimes in a Changing Global Order: The Need for a Refined Ethical Approach. Moral Philosophy and Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1515/mopp-2025-0039
Abstract (original): Abstract In an era of profound geopolitical fragmentation, rising authoritarianism and an increasingly multipolar global order, political and economic decision-makers face a growing moral dilemma: how to reconcile economic interests with commitments to international law, human rights, and democratic values. In this paper I examine the ethical dilemma raised by commercial ties with authoritarian regimes in the context of a rapidly changing economic and political landscape. Drawing on moral philosophy, international political theory, and business ethics, I analyze how existing ethical approaches evaluate such relationships. I argue that prevailing binary frameworks, such as democratic versus authoritarian regimes or legal versus political norms, increasingly fail to capture the normative and strategic complexity of today’s multipolar world. Instead, I propose a convergence approach, emphasizing the prioritization of norm violations that challenge the very foundations of an already eroding global normative order, including wars of aggression and gross violations of international humanitarian law.

28. Steelmanning in the Shadow of Iron Swords
Erez, L. (2026). Steelmanning in the Shadow of Iron Swords. Moral Philosophy and Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1515/mopp-2025-0040
Abstract (original): Abstract This article argues that the approach to political theory articulated by Shmuel Nili’s Philosophizing the Indefensible needs to be modified to address non-ideal political circumstances in illiberal political societies in deep conflict. Building on Nili’s analysis of Israeli politics as a paradigmatic example, and with the moral catastrophe of October 7 and the Gaza war in mind, I argue that the burdens of politics under these circumstances are likely to make repugnant views commonplace; that worldviewing – the attempt to engage with the strongest version of our interlocutor’s arguments – is an overly charitable exercise, allowing repugnant views to appear non-repugnant; and that, counter-intuitively, the imperative to engage with repugnant views is stronger, not weaker, under these non-ideal circumstances.

33. Obscure engine: China’s Typical Case system for intellectual property law – empirical and doctrinal insights
Li, G. (2026). Obscure engine: China’s Typical Case system for intellectual property law – empirical and doctrinal insights. Legal Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lst.2026.10133
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper integrates empirical and doctrinal approaches to demystify the intellectual property law Typical Case system in China. IP Typical Cases have proliferated nationwide, yet their institutional meaning – particularly what makes them ‘typical’ and what functions they are intended to serve – remains obscure. Normative documents suggest a dual purpose: doctrinally, to unify the application of law across similar cases; and socially, to promote law popularisation and support broader social governance. This dual intention aligns with the findings from a semantic network analysis based on a large-N Weibo dataset, which reveals that IP Typical Cases embody economic, political, and legal dimensions. However, policymakers have overlooked the inherent tension between these functions: the legal function requires the doctrinal complexity of hard cases, whereas the societal function depends on simple, communicable cases suitable for public education. Given the predominance of non-hard cases and the pre-existing ambiguity surrounding the system’s intended purpose, the current IP Typical Case system struggles to realise either objective effectively. A clear recognition of the hard–simple division could enable the system to function more coherently, contributing both to the development of China’s case guidance system and, more narrowly, to the formation of IP legal dogmatics.

34. Living in a material world? Regulatory challenges of software as a medical device
Quigley, M. et al. (2026). Living in a material world? Regulatory challenges of software as a medical device. Legal Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lst.2026.10139
Abstract (original): Abstract Software as a medical device (SaMD) offer significant promise for care and have become near ubiquitous within healthcare and medicine. Yet, the regulation of SaMD in the UK and Europe has not kept up with the technological state-of-the-art. In this paper we argue that there is a conceptual mismatch between regulatory frameworks designed for physical devices and the intangible nature of software. To demonstrate this, we identify three key gaps in the application of medical devices regulations to SaMD: (1) how the ‘intended use’ of digital devices impacts on whether they are captured by the relevant Regulations; (2) the problems created by the boundaryless nature of software for determining the device ‘manufacturer’; and (3) the challenges of managing changes to SaMD, especially where these are updated rapidly and frequently. Whilst recognising that recently published guidance and planned proposals to update medical devices regulation in the UK and EU attempt to address these challenges, we argue that they do not go far enough. Namely, they fail to properly attend to the foundational mismatch at the heart of regulating SaMD. In response, we propose three potential solutions to ensure that any forthcoming regulatory changes appropriately capture the nuances and risks specific to software.

38. Satisficing, maximizing, and bounded rationality
Thorstad, D. (2026). Satisficing, maximizing, and bounded rationality. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag059
Abstract (original): Abstract It is often argued that maximizing normative theories are inappropriate for bounded agents. I argue that leading motivations for theories of bounded rationality are substantially compatible with some traditional maximizing normative theories. This finding expands the audience for theories of bounded rationality by making room for advocates of maximizing normative theories, and has implications for knowledge-action principles, the relationship between descriptive and normative theorizing, and the definitions of central tenets of bounded rationality.

39. VSED Should
                    Not
                    Be a Pathway to MAiD (Though Both Are Ethically Permissible)
Davison, D.B. (2026). VSED Should
                    Not
                    Be a Pathway to MAiD (Though Both Are Ethically Permissible). Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70144
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Recent changes in aid in dying laws have led some to argue for a pathway from Voluntarily Stopping Eating and Drinking (VSED) to Medical Aid in Dying (MAiD). Proponents point to the Sontag case as demonstrating the efficacy of the practice. This article first provides an overview of the development of the practice of VSED and MAiD and provides the ethical arguments which support both. It then moves to discussing and critiquing Pope and Brodoff's defense of VSED‐as‐pathway. Pope and Brodoff argue it is maleficent and inconsistent to exclude patients diagnosed with dementia from accessing aid in dying. The main crux of their argument relies on the notion of “terminal self election.” In this article terminal self election is critiqued, and alternate proposals are put forward to allow incapacitated patients with dementia access to aid in dying without first having to access VSED.

40. On Our Cosmic (In)significance
Sivula, O. (2026). On Our Cosmic (In)significance. Ratio.
https://doi.org/10.1111/rati.70043
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article discusses three interpretations of cosmic significance, all of which reveal something important about the notion of significance. Along the way, it proposes a unified view of significance by combining elements of each of the views. It then moves on to analyse space endeavours, considering these views to investigate whether there are any grounds for thinking that grandiose space projects could be significant on the cosmic scale. Of the three space endeavours examined, planetary seeding fares best for cosmic significance. Nonetheless, this does not mean that we ought to seed planets, since the value of such activity remains uncertain, and in general significance is not something that we should have pro‐attitudes towards, as also malign things can be important.

41. The Architecture of Docility: Digital Platforms, Post-Revolutionary North Africa, and the Foucault–Bauman Dispositif
Chebika, M.D. & Kahli, R. (2026). The Architecture of Docility: Digital Platforms, Post-Revolutionary North Africa, and the Foucault–Bauman Dispositif. Philosophy & Social Criticism.
https://doi.org/10.1177/01914537261481244
Abstract (original): This article traces a single, three-stage argument. It begins by documenting, with empirical precision, how digital platforms functioned as genuine instruments of horizontal mobilization during the North African uprisings of 2010–2012. It then analyses how those same platforms were rapidly incorporated into new and frequently more sophisticated architectures of social control, transforming what had been emancipatory tools into what Foucault would call dispositifs of docility. Finally, it asks what theoretical apparatus is adequate to comprehend this transition—and argues that the most productive framework is a deliberate synthesis of Foucault’s analytics of biopower and disciplinary normalization with Bauman’s diagnosis of liquid modernity. The contribution of Bauman is not merely decorative: his account of how solid disciplinary confinement mutates under liquid conditions into consensual, self-administered surveillance explains precisely why the digital panopticon is so much more difficult to resist than its institutional predecessors. The article also examines the epistemological dimensions of this subjection, attending to the structural marginalization of non-Western knowledge traditions through the normative alignment filters of Large Language Models. The argument is organized as a continuous analytical thread, not as a review of theoretical positions, and concludes with implications for decolonial critical theory.

43. Agent-Centred Nuclear Safety Principles for Small Modular Reactors
Widyatmanto, J.N. & Hillerbrand, R. (2026). Agent-Centred Nuclear Safety Principles for Small Modular Reactors. Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00629-5
Abstract (original): Abstract Small Modular Reactors (SMRs) are among the new designs that promise more compact nuclear power plants (NPPs) with scalable generation capacity and shorter construction time, while retaining emission-free electricity in exchange for small amounts of manageable radioactive waste per kWh of generated electricity. Their size and modular design contribute to the growing popularity of SMRs. However, these features also present risk scenarios that do not apply to large, conventional NPPs, such as when a buyer and SMR manufacturer are located in different nations with different regulations and standardisations. Such potential new scenarios require explicit ethical considerations. Unfortunately, such considerations are absent from the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) safety principles, particularly for SMRs. Existing nuclear safety principles are too broad to keep up with potential new scenarios introduced by the novel features of SMRs. Meanwhile, existing approaches in nuclear energy ethics are focused on specific nuclear-related issues across reactors, such as radioactive waste management and nuclear fuel processing. Drawing on Hermansson and Hansson’s agent-centred ethical Risk Assessment (eRA), this paper aims to enhance the IAEA’s nuclear safety principles in order to clarify the distribution of responsibilities for nuclear actors, especially in SMR cases. We articulate what we call the agent-centred presupposition in eRA and apply it to nuclear safety principles using the case of SMRs. We then propose a critique of this approach and suggest further conceptual analysis, which could be suitable for considering SMRs or new reactor designs more generally.

44. Unjustified Defeaters and the Role of Incoherence in Epistemic Defeat
Fett, J.R. (2026). Unjustified Defeaters and the Role of Incoherence in Epistemic Defeat. Acta Analytica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12136-026-00704-3
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper examines the possibility of unjustified defeaters and the explanatory role of incoherence in epistemic defeat. I reconstruct Peter Graham and Jack Lyons’s objections to Jennifer Lackey’s psychological defeaters, paying special attention to their rejection of unjustified defeaters and to their claim that incoherence plays no indispensable explanatory role in defeat. First, I argue that denying the possibility of unjustified defeaters leads to implausible verdicts about rationality in cases involving incoherent belief sets. Second, I argue that incoherence is essential for explaining the epistemic damage generated by such defeaters, even when a subject’s total evidence remains unchanged. Finally, I examine different ways of accommodating this phenomenon and argue that, regardless of whether it is understood in terms of full loss of justification, structural irrationality, or some combination of the two, incoherence remains an indispensable component of its explanation. I conclude that, although evidentialist accounts plausibly explain many ordinary cases of defeat, they cannot fully account for defeat involving unjustified defeaters, where incoherence itself becomes the source of the epistemic downgrade.

45. Paediatric Preference Prediction: the Future of Decision-Making for Children?
Wilkinson, D.J.C. et al. (2026). Paediatric Preference Prediction: the Future of Decision-Making for Children?. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09669-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Adolescents sometimes express firm views about high-stakes medical decisions that conflict with those of their caregivers or clinicians, raising difficult questions about how much weight their wishes should carry. A recurring concern is whether such decisions will later be regretted, although the normative significance of future potential regret remains hotly debated. Recent work in adult bioethics has proposed using artificial intelligence–based Personalized Patient Preference Predictors (P4s) to estimate what current treatment incapacitated adults would have chosen based on their known or inferred preferences when last autonomous. This paper explores whether, and with what ethical significance, a parallel ‘Paediatric Personalized Patient Preference Predictor’ (P5) could be applied to children and young people. We analyse how predicted future preferences might bear on questions of substituted judgment or proxy consent, best-interests assessments, resistance to treatment, and adolescents’ own deliberation. We distinguish different groups of paediatric patients based on current and future decision-making capacity, and identify both where a P5 might have greatest promise and where it is least defensible. We identify challenges for a P5 including that of self-reinforcing or self-negating preferences as well as of deciding which future preferences are relevant to current decisions. We argue that, in paediatrics, predicted future preferences cannot ground substituted judgment in the adult sense, but may nonetheless play a supportive role in best-interests reasoning and shared decision-making, subject to significant epistemic and ethical constraints.

46. What does the correlation between the anthropomorphisation of technologies and the automation of humans mean?
Chiodo, S. (2026). What does the correlation between the anthropomorphisation of technologies and the automation of humans mean?. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01346-2
Abstract (original): Abstract In recent years, the phenomenon of anthropomorphisation of technologies, especially artificial intelligence, has been extensively studied. The reciprocal phenomenon, which may be defined as the automation of humans, has been studied less extensively. And the correlation between the two phenomena has been studied even less. The objective of the article is to contribute to filling the gap by addressing the following question: if we realise that there is a direct proportionality between the anthropomorphisation of technologies and the automation of humans, what may their direct proportionality mean from a philosophical perspective? More precisely, what may their direct proportionality mean for both the way we think of technologies and the way we think of humans, i.e. ourselves? The argument I will propose is that, even though, more superficially, the coexistence of the two phenomena seems to be symptomatic of the radicalisation of typically neoliberal values, starting with humans thought of as optimised and productive as technologies, less superficially, it may be symptomatic of their crisis, because, as paradoxical as it may seem, radicalising the automation of humans means sabotaging their optimisation and productivity and, thus, escaping typically neoliberal values.

47. Challenges, paradoxes and lessons for inclusive AI governance: an interdisciplinary analysis of corporate initiatives

Abstract (original): Abstract Current AI governance strategies and guardrails ensure that AI systems adhere to the ethical principles, legal requirements, and safety standards. This research aims to address these weaknesses by employing a multi-case analysis involving a sector-level critical synthesis to examine corporate governance and social responsibility (CG/SR) initiatives across the oil and pharmaceutical industries to provide interdisciplinary understanding for the ongoing global dialogue on inclusive and sustainable AI governance. We also offer practical pathways for putting our innovative AI governance framework to work in the real-world, which could turn AI principles into operational strategies for AI/technology governance.

https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag029
Abstract (original): Abstract The Corporate Insolvency and Governance Act 2020 introduced a statutory ‘cross-class cramdown’ provision in UK corporate restructuring law, enabling the court to impose a restructuring plan on a class of creditor or shareholder that has voted against it. This has dramatic consequences for the law and practice of reorganising financially distressed companies. The statutory provision is, however, extremely thin, offering no solid basis for the power and posing real challenges for judges who are trying to work out the contours of its operation. 53. Acting on the Voicelessness Challenge to Democratic Deliberation: A Six‐Actor Framework
Jamison, J.C. (2026). Acting on the Voicelessness Challenge to Democratic Deliberation: A Six‐Actor Framework. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70180
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Political philosophers have long recognized that key stakeholders in policy decisions—children, future generations, nonhuman animals, and individuals with severe cognitive impairments—are structurally unable to participate in the deliberative processes meant to legitimate those decisions. This paper argues that such “voicelessness” is not a marginal complication to be accommodated at the edges of deliberative democracy (DD), but a first‐order problem for exactly the large‐scale, high‐stakes domains—health, biotechnology, and environmental policy chief among them—where the voiceless often constitute the majority of those affected. This exclusion risks systematically neglecting crucial interests and suggests that some degree of surrogacy or representation by proxy is functionally unavoidable, whether or not DD's more demanding commitment to reasoned, preference‐transforming deliberation is in play. Building on this recognition, and after critiquing the feasibility of idealized deliberative democracy as well as partial solutions implied by existing work, this paper's central contribution is a more pragmatic, ethically robust Six‐Actor Framework. The framework delineates specific roles for policymakers, target populations, general laypeople, technical domain experts, and two novel categories: “benevolent partisans” (e.g., caregivers) and “agent‐specific experts” tasked with interpreting or forecasting the interests of the voiceless. The utility of this structured approach is illustrated through three high‐stakes policy domains: constructing global health metrics (DALYs) for cost‐effectiveness analysis, animal welfare regulation, and the challenges involved when impacting the very existence of future people. This paper offers a path toward a policy process that is both practicable and ethically defensible, specifically for large‐scale, high‐priority global challenges.

54. Protecting Patient Information in Healthcare
Szabat, M. (2026). Protecting Patient Information in Healthcare. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70188
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper considers the connection between fairness and unfairness from a theoretical standpoint, highlighting the importance of critically evaluating the potential impact of artificial intelligence (AI) tools on patients' perception of healthcare justice. The arguments and analysis are framed within the context of Hannah Arendt's concept of the ‘banality of evil’ and Miranda Fricker's concept of ‘epistemic injustice’. The article suggests that the greatest threat to fairness and transparency when it comes to AI may not be people deliberately trying to cause harm, but rather a widespread culture of apathy and complacency that prevents people from taking action. The paper concludes that renewing patients' commitment to critical thinking and encouraging them to take greater personal responsibility are key. More active and engaged citizenship is also required. By cultivating critical reflection, prioritising data privacy, promoting collective support, building emotional resilience, and advocating for ethical AI, individuals can transform their healthcare into a formidable bulwark against those who seek to curtail their autonomy and stifle their voices. This comprehensive healthcare strategy is about more than just the individual's welfare. It is also about creating a fair, self‐governing society in which the potential of each person as a smart, active participant is recognised and protected.

58. The Hague as Archive: Duterte’s War on Drugs, Instrumental Sovereignty, and the Architecture of Global Impunity
Regilme, S.S. (2026). The Hague as Archive: Duterte’s War on Drugs, Instrumental Sovereignty, and the Architecture of Global Impunity. Law Crit.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10978-026-09435-1
Abstract (original): Abstract The March 2025 detention of former Philippine president Rodrigo Duterte under an ICC arrest warrant marks an unprecedented legal rupture, yet its significance extends far beyond individual prosecution. The Duterte case exposes how global security infrastructures, instrumental sovereignty, and oligarchic legality co-produce authoritarian impunity. Using Duterte’s drug war as a paradigmatic case, the article develops four key arguments. First, violent narcotic governance in the Philippines was sustained by transnational counterterrorism alliances, legal security frameworks, and strategic silence from key partners. Second, Duterte’s sovereignty rhetoric functioned as political stagecraft that concealed dependence while resisting external scrutiny, preserving U.S. military ties and courting Chinese finance. Third, the ICC should be read not only as a tribunal but as a moral archive : a site that names atrocity, preserves counter-memory, and affirms victims’ and survivors’ dignity when domestic institutions failed to do so. Fourth, the article advances “radical accountability,” a survivor-centered approach that links truth-telling and recognition to reparations, institutional redesign, and redistribution. Acknowledging the Court’s selectivity, including the importance of consistent treatment in the case of Palestine, the article shows how archival naming can still be mobilized to disrupt impunity and to strengthen local justice claims. Framed at the intersection of critical legal theory, international relations, and postcolonial studies, the article reconceives accountability as an ongoing, emancipatory struggle against the systemic and transnational architecture that makes mass disposability appear lawful.

64. Aptness‐centered Disjunctivism about Practical Knowledge
Chen, S. (2026). Aptness‐centered Disjunctivism about Practical Knowledge. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70119
Abstract (original): Abstract Practical knowledge, as Anscombe understands, is agent's knowledge of what they are doing intentionally. McDowell (2010) contends that if Anscombe insists that one can possess the same practical knowledge even when failing to execute their intention, this view readily leads to a “highest common factor” conception of practical knowledge. As an alternative, McDowell then suggests a disjunctive conception of practical knowledge. In this paper, I first reconstruct McDowell's criticism as a skeptical argument against practical knowledge, which parallels that against perceptual knowledge based on a similar highest common factor conception. I then contend that although disjunctivism is the promising framework to solve this skeptical challenge, McDowell's approach leads it into the wrong way. His success‐centered disjunctivism fails to accommodate two salient features of Anscombean practical knowledge. Finally, I propose an aptness‐centered disjunctivism about practical knowledge based on a what I call Triple‐C ( Conducive to success , Capacity , Circumstance ) model of aptness as a better response to the skeptical argument. The crucial point of this new version is that it takes aptness , rather than success , as the criterion for distinguishing the disjuncts.

65. The Scope of Dishonesty
Um, S. (2026). The Scope of Dishonesty. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10197
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper addresses the unification question for dishonesty: what do paradigmatic instances—such as lying, misleading, and bullshitting—have in common, and what explains when they genuinely manifest dishonesty rather than merely appearing to do so? I argue that what unifies these cases is disrespect for the right not to be deceived (RND). To handle the full range of cases precisely, the paper introduces a tripartite framework distinguishing acts that manifest the vice of dishonesty, acts that wrongfully infringe RND without expressing such a vice, and acts that are merely apparently dishonest while being either permissible or wrong for other reasons. Unlike Christian Miller’s account, which identifies honesty with a cluster of behavioral dispositions, my view grounds dishonesty in a single evaluative failure and thereby offers a more unified and context-sensitive explanation of the scope of dishonesty.

66. Introduction: The Impact of AI on Economies: Challenges and Opportunities
Cetindamar, D. (2026). Introduction: The Impact of AI on Economies: Challenges and Opportunities. Science, Technology and Society.
https://doi.org/10.1177/09717218261480246
Abstract (original): This editorial introduces a special issue examining the impact of artificial intelligence (AI) on economies across seven countries at different stages of development: Australia, China, Greece, India, Korea, Türkiye, and Vietnam. Drawing on perspectives from labour economics, innovation systems theory, sociotechnical analysis, and development studies, the assembled contributions move beyond technologically deterministic accounts of automation to reveal heterogeneous outcomes shaped by occupational structures, workforce demographics, governance arrangements, and institutional capacity. Five cross-cutting themes emerge: the age and experience asymmetry of AI’s labour-market effects, with early-career workers disproportionately affected through narrowed hiring margins; significant sectoral heterogeneity in adoption trajectories; a diverse spectrum of governance approaches, none clearly superior and all context-dependent; a critical gap between technology deployment and the institutional conversion of AI capabilities into inclusive growth; and differentiated skill policies. The issue argues that skills policy must be differentiated and context-sensitive, and that the central challenge of AI-driven transformation lies not in access to technology but in building the complementary institutional, organisational, and human-capital foundations needed to translate technological potential into broadly shared prosperity.

67. On Williams on Plato
Kirkland, S.D. (2026). On Williams on Plato. Eur J Polit Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14748851261481500
Abstract (original): This essay is a response to David Lay Williams’ reading of Plato’s political thought, in his book The Greatest of All Plagues: How Economic Inequality Shaped Political Thought from Plato to Marx . The review essay asks three questions: Does Williams see among practitioners of contemporary political thinking any sign of a return to the deeper moral and philosophical aspects of political theory and any sign of a move toward expanding on the safer statistical approaches he himself diagnoses as insufficient? Of the two modes of responding to radical inequality, the imposition of practical impediments to acquiring massive wealth versus the re-education of the population toward an attitude that does not value the excessive accumulation of wealth, does Williams see across the canon of thinkers he treats here any general preference for one over the other? And lastly, in response to Plato’s apparent observation of a tendency in human life to lose sight of the whole of the political community one exists in and to focus on smaller horizons of concern, are there any strategies in Plato or in the other thinkers he treats, that Williams would suggest are valuable in combatting this tendency?

70. Organization-Level Inclusion: Evidence of Positive Effects for Both LGBT and Non-LGBT Employees
Figueroa, A. et al. (2026). Organization-Level Inclusion: Evidence of Positive Effects for Both LGBT and Non-LGBT Employees. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06458-7
Abstract (original): Abstract This work examines organization-level inclusion signals and their subsequent effects on a broad range of employees. Answering the call for more research on concealable stigmatized identities, we consider the effect of such organization-level inclusion initiatives for sexual and gender minorities (lesbian, gay, bisexual, and transgender employees) while also considering their reverberating effect on cisgendered, heterosexual (non-LGBT) employees. In a compiled archival data study ( N = 849), a preregistered, time-lagged observational study ( N = 1,165), and a preregistered, randomized experiment ( N = 398), we show that organization-level inclusion efforts lead to more positive perceptions of the organization’s psychological climate for sexual and gender minorities, closing the psychological safety gap between non-LGBT and LGBT employees. Notably, intentional inclusion efforts also positively affect the psychological safety of non-LGBT employees, which challenges theoretical assertions that targeted inclusion policies can harm majority groups, or that organizational inclusion is zero-sum. In turn, these signals shape positive behavioral intentions for all employees, leading non-LGBT employees to engage in more organizational ally work and leading LGBT employees to be more likely to disclose their identities. Unexpectedly, inclusion signals also made non-LGBT employees less likely to conceal sensitive aspects of their own identities (e.g., religion, parental status). In sum, we show that inclusion signaling both increases psychological safety broadly, and reduces disparities between target and non-target employees. This finding suggests that organization-level inclusion initiatives may have wider-reaching benefits than previously assumed.

75. Giving color reductionism its due
Sharp, W.A. (2026). Giving color reductionism its due. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02608-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Pautz (2003, 2013a) argues that to secure for colors’ reducers structural match with the colors-as-perceived, the reductionist turns colors (inappropriately) into mere relations to numbers, those used to denote quantities of long-, medium-, and short-wavelength light. In this paper, I argue Pautz’s objection stems from an accidental, easily shed, feature of the reducers, namely, their description in terms of long-, medium-, and short-wavelength light. The reducers should instead be given in terms of (relevant) postreceptoral sensitivity functions given directly in terms of wavelength. This results in reducers that should be fully kosher by Pautz’s lights. The upshot, if this works, for debates about perception is externalism is alive and well.

76. Beyond Innovation: The Burdens and Harms of Maintenance in Deep Brain Stimulation
Pateraki, M. & Gilbert, F. (2026). Beyond Innovation: The Burdens and Harms of Maintenance in Deep Brain Stimulation. Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00628-6
Abstract (original): Abstract In this contribution, we explore iatrogenic harms associated with the maintenance of deep brain stimulation (DBS) systems. By conceptualizing patients with neural implants as infrastructures, we examine them as ongoing sites of maintenance that require continual care and adjustments. This analysis reveals the tradeoffs inherent in implantable neurotechnologies. Using first-hand accounts collected during a qualitative study of individuals with Parkinson’s disease treated with DBS, we subsequently develop a typology of harms associated with maintenance, highlighting the ethical issues arising from the integration of technology in the body. Importantly, by focusing on older DBS devices within a healthcare system constrained by financial resources, we address a gap in the literature, as current debates tend to prioritize innovation while neglecting the difficulties of maintaining older implanted systems. Our findings show how inadequate or faulty maintenance compromises the successful incorporation and integration of technological components within the body, underscoring the ethical significance of maintenance in the context of DBS. Finally, we emphasize the inherent limitations of implanted technologies in managing chronic illness and the enduring importance of caregivers. This study calls for a more comprehensive examination of the ethical implications of the maintenance in relation to neural implants.

78. Who watches the machines? Institutional design and the ethics of national AI enforcement: lessons from Germany’s KI-MIG
Teichmann, F. (2026). Who watches the machines? Institutional design and the ethics of national AI enforcement: lessons from Germany’s KI-MIG. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01358-y
Abstract (original): Abstract The effectiveness and the moral credibility of the European Union’s Artificial Intelligence Act will be decided less by its substantive provisions than by the national institutions charged with enforcing them. This article examines Germany’s KI-Marktüberwachungs- und Innovationsförderungsgesetz (KI-MIG), adopted by the Bundestag on 11 June 2026 and in force since 29 July 2026, as one of the first comprehensive national enforcement frameworks for the AI Act and as a test case for the claim that institutional design is itself an ethical variable. The article reconstructs the KI-MIG’s architecture—the centralisation of market surveillance at the Federal Network Agency, a coordination and competence centre (KoKIVO), an independent market surveillance chamber for fundamental-rights-sensitive systems, a central complaints body, and a hybrid model that preserves sectoral competences—and evaluates it against five criteria of legitimate enforcement derived from regulatory theory: independence and mandate fidelity, competence and capacity, accessibility and voice, coherence, and regulatory candour. The assessment yields verdicts rather than a mere inventory. The design earns genuine credit on coherence and on accessibility, but the article concludes that the independence construction for fundamental-rights-sensitive supervision—a chamber staffed, in personal union, by the leadership of a hierarchically embedded economic regulator—falls short of the AI Act’s heightened requirements. Further concerns—dispersed notification responsibilities, undersecured Länder capacity, and an official rhetoric of ‘lean oversight’ that risks normalising under-enforcement—are sharpened by the Digital Omnibus’s deferral of high-risk obligations to December 2027. The article concludes with transferable design principles for jurisdictions that are now building AI enforcement institutions.

80. Towards a practice of knowledge commoning: academics’ ethical duties to defend Wikipedia in the age of generative AI
Choi, H. (2026). Towards a practice of knowledge commoning: academics’ ethical duties to defend Wikipedia in the age of generative AI. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01373-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Wikipedia is the most widely used reference work in history and one of the most valuable training resources available to the generative AI (genAI) industry; and yet that same industry is systematically undermining the conditions that make Wikipedia possible. GenAI companies extract Wikipedia’s corpus to train their products while redirecting users away from the encyclopedia. The result is declining readership, a shrinking volunteer base, and a growing threat to the encyclopedia’s long-term sustainability as a knowledge commons. This article examines that threat through the lens of commons theory, arguing that Wikipedia functions as a knowledge commons that is collaboratively constructed, transparently produced, and freely accessible, and that genAI-based tools operate as a force against it. Against this backdrop, the article makes the case that academics have a responsibility to defend Wikipedia and address the epistemic injustice caused by the genAI industry’s attempts to consolidate control over knowledge production.

84. How Bioethicists Can Contribute to Democratic Deliberation
Saunders, B. (2026). How Bioethicists Can Contribute to Democratic Deliberation. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70168
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT It is sometimes suggested that policymaking on bioethical questions should be more democratic, either through direct popular participation (as in a referendum) or through improved forms of representation (such as random mini‐publics or elected officials). Such proposals seem to leave no special place for input from bioethicists. This paper argues that it is not contrary to democracy to give bioethicists additional influence over relevant policy areas. First, while referenda, mini‐publics, or electoral representation may be more democratic in some respects, these processes also have limitations, in particular excluding the interests of disenfranchised groups (such as children or future generations). Second, I suggest that it is wrong to think of democracy only in terms of something like the will of the citizens. Democracies usually involve mechanisms to divide power, potentially blocking a majority's will to protect minority rights. Bioethicists may serve this function, particularly when they raise objections on behalf of minorities or disenfranchised groups.

85. Shifting sands? The impact of conflict displacement on voting
Bitton, G. (2026). Shifting sands? The impact of conflict displacement on voting. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261485652
Abstract (original): Does exposure to conflict displacement amplify support for far-right parties? This study investigates the political consequences of conflict-induced displacement in northern Israel by exploiting a natural experiment arising from government-ordered evacuations during military conflicts. Using a pre-registered survey and a regression discontinuity design based on an exogenous 5 km threshold, I assess changes in retrospective and prospective voting behavior as well as self-reported political ideology. The analysis reveals no evidence that displacement increases far-right support or induces partisan realignment. Rather, evacuees demonstrate a modest rightward shift in ideological self-placement without corresponding changes in voting behavior. This divergence suggests that displacement influences symbolic political positioning without prompting corresponding changes in voting behavior. These patterns unfold against a broader backdrop of political frustration and electoral disengagement that affects both displaced and non-displaced populations. The findings reveal troubling implications for democratic accountability during external conflict: leaders may be shielded from electoral consequences of security failures even as civic engagement deteriorates, challenging models of wartime accountability.

88. Conscience, Conflict, and Health Care: A Critical Examination of the Conflict-of-Interest Argument against Conscientious Objection
Belousek, D.W.S. (2026). Conscience, Conflict, and Health Care: A Critical Examination of the Conflict-of-Interest Argument against Conscientious Objection. The Journal of Medicine and Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1093/jmp/jhag031
Abstract (original): Abstract I critically examine a conflict-of-interest argument against conscientious objection in health care. This argument claims that conscientious objection is analogous to conflict of interest in which objecting professionals place their personal interest above patients’ health, which violates the healthcare professional’s primary ethical obligation. The argument concludes that conscientious objection in health care is impermissible. I argue that this argument proceeds from false or dubious premises. First, it categorizes conscientious objection with conflict of interest. I argue that conscientious objection is not analogous to conflict of interest: conscientious objection lacks key features that make conflict of interest ethically problematic; and conscientious objection exhibits a key feature that makes it ethically incompatible with conflict of interest. Second, the argument assumes that conscientious objection is likely to harm patient health. I argue that this assumption lacks strong supporting evidence. I conclude that the argument fails to demonstrate its conclusion.

91. Adorno and the Kantian Sources of Ideology Critique
Athanassiadis, A. (2026). Adorno and the Kantian Sources of Ideology Critique. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70072
Abstract (original): Abstract Social theorists in the Marxian tradition often interpret philosophical problems as reflections of social problems. This approach is central to Adorno's interpretation of Kant's epistemology. Adorno claims that the Critique of Pure Reason develops a concept of subjectivity that mirrors the “functionalization” of experience in capitalist societies. He retains that concept contradictory just as the social functionalism it ideologically reflects. In this paper I argue that Kant's concept of subjectivity is not contradictory and that Adorno must rely on it to articulate his critique of functionalism. Recognizing this Kantian debt implies not just acknowledging the Kantian sources of Adorno's model of ideology critique, but also revising central aspects of his social theory.

93. The Changing Application of Vulnerability Across the Different Phases of Clinical Research
Moore, K.T. (2026). The Changing Application of Vulnerability Across the Different Phases of Clinical Research. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70187
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The United States Food and Drug Omnibus Reform Act (FDORA) of 2022 established a legislative mandate requiring sponsors of pharmaceutical research to actively enhance the inclusion of historically underrepresented populations in clinical trials. This policy reflects the growing recognition that diversity in research is not only a scientific and medical necessity, but an ethical imperative rooted in the principle of justice. However, as efforts to increase diversity expand, the inclusion of individuals traditionally viewed as “vulnerable” introduces complex ethical considerations that necessitate a reassessment of current research practices. Historically, clinical research has often adopted an overly protective stance toward individuals classified as vulnerable, such as children, pregnant women, older adults, or those with cognitive or socioeconomic disadvantages, leading to their exclusion. This exclusion, while intended to prevent harm, paradoxically perpetuates inequalities restricting access to the potential benefits of research participation and evidence generation to support the broader use of the product. Moreover, this cautious approach is applied inconsistently across the phases of clinical development, suggesting a lack of ethical coherence in how vulnerability is defined, interpreted, and operationalized. A reexamination of the concept and treatment of vulnerability across all phases of clinical research is therefore essential. The consistent and ethically justified inclusion of vulnerable populations would advance the generalizability of scientific findings, promote health equity, and uphold the bioethical principles of autonomy, beneficence, nonmaleficence, and most critically, justice. Ensuring equitable inclusion is not merely a regulatory expectation but a moral obligation that underpins the legitimacy and integrity of clinical research.

95. Right up my alley: Local radical right success, contextual alignment, and immigration attitudes in Sweden
Dahlberg, S. et al. (2026). Right up my alley: Local radical right success, contextual alignment, and immigration attitudes in Sweden. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261483794
Abstract (original): Electoral success of radical right parties is often argued to normalize previously stigmatized anti-immigration attitudes. Yet, it remains unclear whether such normalization reflects genuine within-individual persuasion or shifts in the local climate of political expression. We address this question by linking highly disaggregated district-level vote shares to individual-level panel survey data from Sweden (2018–2022), a period marked by sustained growth and partial normalization of the Sweden Democrats. Cross-sectional analyses reveal pronounced contextual patterns: Restrictive immigration attitudes are more prevalent in districts with higher local radical right support, particularly among voters who do not support the party, while core supporters remain comparatively stable. However, first-difference panel analyses provide little evidence that changes in local radical right support are associated with attitudinal change. The findings suggest that what appears as normalization in ecological analyses is more consistent with contextual alignment than with rapid persuasion, clarifying the micro-foundations of radical right normalization.

96. Indigo orientalism: The fabric art of Eve Kosofsky Sedgwick and Candice Lin
Kim, S. (2026). Indigo orientalism: The fabric art of Eve Kosofsky Sedgwick and Candice Lin. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261476991
Abstract (original): This article comparatively reads the Buddhism-inspired cyanotype work of the US queer-theory scholar Eve Kosofsky Sedgwick and the indigo-dyed fabric work of the LA-based multimedia artist Candice Lin. It examines how Jason Edwards, one of the very few scholars to analyze Sedgwick's visual art, mistakenly identifies her cyanotype blue as “indigo” rather than Prussian blue, tracing this error to the troubled link between the visual blur of the two blues and their opposing geohistorical associations—Prussian with the West and indigo with the East. I read this charged mistake as an Orientalist refusal. Lin, on the other hand, enacts traditional indigo-dyeing techniques to critique the colonial history of the indigo trade. Despite her elaborate distancing of her ethnicity from her artistic practice, the two are often conflated in its reception. Underlining the tendency in their work perceptions where their ethnicity is either confounded (Sedgwick) or overdetermined (Lin), this article argues that indigo blue is treated similarly. At the same time, it foregrounds the deep blue that permeates across the two artists’ work that helps dismantle such a binary. By elucidating the previously unstated distinction between the underexamined and oversaturated readings of color and ethnicity in their work, this article seeks to transform this absent discussion into an impetus for envisioning an expansive queer relationality across the Pacific.

98. AI Adoption, Structural Constraints and Inclusive Growth: Evidence from Greece
Bartzokas, A. & Kostis, P.C. (2026). AI Adoption, Structural Constraints and Inclusive Growth: Evidence from Greece. Science, Technology and Society.
https://doi.org/10.1177/09717218261480257
Abstract (original): Artificial intelligence (AI), as a General-Purpose Technology, is reshaping both work and productivity. However, its implementation is contingent not solely on its technical capabilities, but also on sectoral capacity, institutional quality, and the state of human capital. This article, focusing on Greece—a peripheral EU economy with substantial projected AI benefits but considerable structural limitations—develops a structural conversion framework linking three dimensions: capacity for AI-driven sectoral upgrading; institutional readiness beyond digital infrastructure; and the regeneration and retention of skilled labour. Employing micro-level data, it formulates indices for institutional trust, digital confidence, and service usability. The analysis uncovers a structural mismatch: while usability improves with education, digital confidence is primarily influenced by performance rather than legitimacy. Moreover, Attica demonstrates a conflict between elevated institutional trust and inadequate user-centric quality. These findings underscore a fragmented transition characterised by social and regional imbalances, implying that inclusive growth necessitates a policy shift away from simple deployment and towards the institutional conversion of technological resources.

99. Reshaping China’s Labour Market: AI’s Dual Impacts on Employee Adaptation and Employer Demand
Tan, C. & Ran, A. (2026). Reshaping China’s Labour Market: AI’s Dual Impacts on Employee Adaptation and Employer Demand. Science, Technology and Society.
https://doi.org/10.1177/09717218261480258
Abstract (original): The rapid advancement of artificial intelligence (AI) is reshaping labour markets globally, with profound implications for China amid structural fragility, youth unemployment and demographic ageing. This study examines AI’s impacts from a dual-perspective framework: employees’ adaptation, skills and perceived risks and employers’ demand reshaping, emphasising supply-demand interactions and mismatches. Using nationally representative survey data from the 2023 China Social Survey and 2023 AI recruitment big data, we find that platform employment sustains opportunities but erodes stability and heightens substitution anxiety, with occupational heterogeneities driving polarisation—routine roles face greater risks, while high-skill positions adapt better. Employers exhibit stratified demand, premiuming algorithm-intensive skills in urban clusters, exacerbating regional and demographic divides. These dynamics reveal perceptual and skill mismatches, advancing task-based models by incorporating bidirectional feedbacks with ethical implications calling for transparent regulations to address vulnerabilities, with policy recommendations for upskilling and equity-focused governance.

100. Labour Market Implications of Generative AI in an Emerging Economy: The Case of Türkiye
Gürci̇han, B. et al. (2026). Labour Market Implications of Generative AI in an Emerging Economy: The Case of Türkiye. Science, Technology and Society.
https://doi.org/10.1177/09717218261480259
Abstract (original): Generative artificial intelligence (AI) is increasingly viewed as a general-purpose technology with far-reaching labour-market implications. While existing evidence is largely drawn from advanced economies, much less is known about the impact of generative AI on employment in emerging economies. This article examines the labour-market impact of generative AI in Türkiye using individual-level administrative records from the Social Security Institution combined with occupation-level exposure measures from the International Labour Organisation. We find that the share of AI-exposed employment within the top 20% of the exposure distribution (fifth quintile) amounts to 17% of total employment. About 2.3 million registered jobs in Türkiye are highly exposed to generative AI. Employment effects are strongly age-asymmetric: since 2023, job growth has weakened mainly for early-career workers in highly exposed occupations, while no comparable losses are observed for workers over thirty. These patterns survive controls for sectoral reallocation, suggesting that generative AI operates primarily through hiring margins, displacing inexperienced workers while complementing experienced ones.

Abstract (original): Artificial intelligence (AI) is reshaping global labour markets, yet adoption remains uneven across sectors. This article employs a sociotechnical systems perspective to examine how national policy and institutional barriers shape AI implementation in advanced economies by examining the case of Australia. 103. Originality in art and other artifacts: an etiological-ecological approach
Eaton, A.W. (2026). Originality in art and other artifacts: an etiological-ecological approach. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05702-x
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper proposes an account of artifact function that applies equally to artworks, everyday artifacts, and highly specialized technical artifacts. This unified account of artifact function centers on the notion of proper function and combines reproduction history (etiology) with careful attention to an artifact’s cultural niche (ecology). 105. Explaining evidential discordance
Sequeiros, S.B. (2026). Explaining evidential discordance. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05761-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Evidential discordance, or the situation where scientific evidence supports contrary hypotheses, claims or theories about a given phenomenon, is common in scientific inquiry (Franklin, 2002; Boyd, 2018; Stegenga, 2009, 2012). Although in many ways useful for scientific progress, discordance also poses distinct epistemic and methodological challenges for science, as it hinders building consensus and causes problems for decision making (Collins, 1985; Hey, 2015a; Maziarz, 2024; Reijula and Blanco Sequeiros, 2024). To address these challenges, philosophers of science have discussed episodes of disagreement in scientific evidence and presented strategies for dealing with discordance (Bokulich, 2020; Ohnesorge, 2021; Reijula & Blanco Sequeiros, 2024). Despite this discussion, the concept of evidential discordance itself has remained overlooked. In this paper, I clarify the concept of discordant evidence, and propose a novel explanation for the emergence of evidential discordance into scientific evidence. I argue that evidential discordance can be explained by disagreement between inferences from data to phenomena between various lines of evidence. The data-to-phenomenon inferences conflict because of how they conceptualize the causal tracks of the phenomenon of interest within data. This explanation for discordance is useful because it unifies a variety of empirical explanations for discordance, including error, noise and bias. I use examples from the social sciences to showcase my arguments but the lessons extend to other areas of philosophy of science as well.

106. Making oneself known and the ethics of epistemic reparations
Nicola, N. (2026). Making oneself known and the ethics of epistemic reparations. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05794-5
Abstract (original): Abstract Jennifer Lackey has argued that victims of gross violations and injustices are owed epistemic reparations and have the right to be known. This is roughly the right for these victims to have their stories heard and to be givers of knowledge. Recent work in the field has focused on ways we fail to listen to the stories of victims. Less attention has been paid to the ways in which oppressive systems, like colonialism, distort victims’ self-understandings and the narratives they convey. I argue that genuine epistemic reparations within oppressive contexts require conditions that enable victims to come to know themselves on their own terms . I call the process of individual and collective self-recognition made possible by those conditions making oneself known . Drawing on critiques of the colonial politics of recognition, I show why this self-knowledge must precede being known by others. Only by establishing this epistemic foundation can genuine reparative work begin, rather than reproducing the very epistemic violence such reparations are meant to repair.

107. Conspiracy theories and the hidden evidence penalty
McIntyre, J.H. (2026). Conspiracy theories and the hidden evidence penalty. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05797-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Many regard conspiracy theories as intrinsically epistemically suspicious. Generalists defend this view, holding that there is something epistemically wrong with conspiracy theories as a class. Particularists deny it, contending that conspiracy theories must be evaluated on a case-by-case basis. This paper invokes a norm of theory choice from the philosophy of science and metaphysics to defend a weak form of generalism. According to the norm, we should prima facie disbelieve theories that posit undetectable entities. I develop a graded notion of undetectability, such that the more undetectable the posited structure, the greater the epistemic penalty a theory incurs. Conspiracy theorists, then, face a dilemma: either (1) posit a highly detectable (but undetected) conspiracy that should be rejected on evidential grounds or (2) posit a highly undetectable conspiracy that should be rejected on anti-undetectability grounds. Most conspiracy theories posit a high degree of secrecy, running into the second horn. This is illustrated through a range of examples, from “lizard people” to 9/11 “truthers.”

108. On the analytic tethering of group phenomena
Lee, K.Y. & Kang, H. (2026). On the analytic tethering of group phenomena. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05798-1
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper examines “the analytic tethering thesis”, according to which adopting a summative or non-summative approach to one group-level phenomenon rationally requires adopting the same approach to others. We argue that the strong version of this thesis is untenable, given the heterogeneity of group phenomena, and that the weak version defended by Jessica Brown fails as well. Through a formal reconstruction of Brown’s arguments, we show that they instantiate a unified argumentative strategy that faces a dilemma: the derivations are either deductively invalid or question-begging. We then identify the source of this failure and argue that analytic tethering can succeed only under highly restrictive conditions. This result preserves analytic tethering in a principled but significantly constrained form.

Abstract (original): Abstract Following Dewar (2019), ‘reduction’ and ‘sophistication’ are supposed to be two different ways of articulating the shared content of symmetry-related models of physical theories. And yet: there does not exist in the literature a maximally clear, maximally general statement as to what the distinction between reduction and sophistication amounts to.
