113. No strong necessities from radically opaque concepts
Wu, J. (2026). No strong necessities from radically opaque concepts. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05813-5
Abstract (original): Abstract Philip Goff raises an ingenious objection to David Chalmers’s (2009) two-dimensional zombie argument in his 2014 paper (co-authored by David Papineau) ‘What’s wrong with strong necessities?’ The objection aims to defeat the conceivability-possibility thesis by arguing that strong necessities, necessary truths that can be conceived to be false, can be constructed by putting an identity sign between two co-referring radically opaque concepts, concepts that refer but reveal nothing about their referents. If Goff’s strategy succeeds, not only is the two-dimensional zombie argument defeated, but Chalmers’s modal monism must also be rejected. This paper aims to defend Chalmers’s zombie argument and modal monism against Goff’s objection. If internalism about reference fixing is true, I argue that no radically opaque concept can exist. If externalism about reference fixing is true, I argue that although radically opaque concepts can exist, strong necessities cannot be constructed by putting an identity sign between two such concepts. In conclusion, Goff’s attack fails to provide any further evidence for the existence of strong necessities, and, as a result, fails to pose any genuine threat to the two-dimensional zombie argument and modal monism.

114. Sustaining the open society: resources from Popperian science
Kim, N.J. (2026). Sustaining the open society: resources from Popperian science. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05815-3
Abstract (original): Abstract Open society liberalism has experienced a revival in the 21st century, prominently led by Gerald Gaus. Despite its growing popularity, however, the boundaries of whose moral and political perspectives ought to be included remain underspecified. Its most famous early defender, Popper, thought that unreasonable individuals, such as those who hold to beliefs dogmatically, should be excluded from the open society. A belief not unusual among other liberal theorists. However, what these open society theorists miss is a possible underutilized resource that comes from Popper’s philosophy of science. Within science, Popper believed that dogmatic individuals were necessary and important for scientific progress. Using this resource, I argue that Popper himself and other open society theorists in general should be more inclusive and tolerant of closed society individuals, including dogmatic religious individuals, because they allow for liberal progress and the sustainment of the open society.

115. The Negative and Positive Criticism of the Metaphysical Idea of Progress
Kopciuch, L. (2026). The Negative and Positive Criticism of the Metaphysical Idea of Progress. Jus Cogens.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s42439-026-00132-2
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines the main features of the metaphysical understanding of progress. Drawing on Georg Simmel’s concept, the article identifies five core components of this perspective: history is purposive; this purpose is valuable; it emerges immanently from historical development; it presupposes a universal subject of history; and it assumes continuity between historical epochs. The article then discusses several major critiques of the idea of progress as formulated by Jacob Burckhardt, Oswald Spengler, Arnold J. Toynbee, and Feliks Koneczny. Subsequently, it analyses the ontological and axiological criticism of the idea of progress developed within the philosophy of Nicolai Hartmann. Finally, the paper highlights the characteristics and advantages of a non-metaphysical conception of progress, encapsulated in the formula: There is no progress “of” history, but there is progress “in” history. The main aim of this article is to present this interpretation. Historical positions are analysed solely to identify arguments within them that support this systematic thesis.

116. Co-design of a trustworthy AI-based prognostic tool for predicting patient outcome in acute stroke
Hofvenschioeld, E. et al. (2026). Co-design of a trustworthy AI-based prognostic tool for predicting patient outcome in acute stroke. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01337-3
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper reports a descriptive, process-oriented case study of the application of Z-Inspection®, an ethically aligned co-design methodology, in the early design phase of an artificial intelligence (AI) system for healthcare. The methodology was applied within the Horizon Europe project VALIDATE [1], which develops and validates a prognostic clinical decision support system (CDSS) to support treatment decisions in acute ischaemic stroke. An interdisciplinary team of Z-Inspection® experts, AI developers, and clinical stakeholders jointly identified ethical, legal, and technical issues relevant to the planned system. The assessment resulted in 22 ethical issues, 12 dilemmas, 18 risks, and 48 derived requirements, each mapped to the European Commission’s trustworthy AI principles. The identified issues, dilemmas, risks, and derived requirements constitute the primary findings of this descriptive case study and document how general trustworthy-AI principles were translated into context-specific technical, clinical, governance, and organisational requirements. By documenting the process and publishing the complete requirements, the study provides a detailed case for critical scrutiny and potential uptake in other healthcare AI projects, though its transferability and scalability remain to be established in further studies.

117. Human-weighted ethical pluralism in AI decision support: introducing the weighted meta-decision engine (WMDE) through the Omelas dilemma

118. Measuring structural value alignment in sixteen models: LLMs have human-like moral spaces
Tang, Y. (2026). Measuring structural value alignment in sixteen models: LLMs have human-like moral spaces. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01368-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Evaluating whether large language models (LLMs) reason about morality in human-like ways requires more than measuring whether they produce the right outputs in isolated cases. Existing approaches – including scalar agreement, distributional analysis, rationale classification, and consistency testing – compare model and human responses one case at a time and cannot detect how a model structures its moral judgments. This paper applies and extends Peterson and Gärdenfors's (2024) geometric moral space framework to evaluate structural value alignment in LLMs, that is, whether the relational structure reconstructed from a model's responses matches the structure reconstructed from human respondents on the same task. Sixteen LLMs across three scale tiers (small, medium, and large) were evaluated on ten moral cases under both zero-shot and chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting. The results reveal phenomena invisible to case-by-case metrics. First, all five large models achieve full geometric alignment on these cases (moral space overlap, MSO = 1.00) in at least one prompting condition, and three do so in both; two medium models also reach MSO = 1.00. Second, structural alignment is bimodal in the sense that models either replicate the human moral space or fall substantially short, with no intermediate cases. Third, smaller models systematically lack a distinct fairness region, a failure of moral categorization rather than of labeling. Fourth, CoT prompting simultaneously improves case-by-case similarity correlations and worsens geometric alignment in smaller models, showing that the two can move in opposite directions. Because structural alignment is measured from model responses, it does not by itself establish that a model holds the values in question. It is, however, a more demanding test than case-by-case agreement, and a better motivated and more informative basis for value alignment measurement.

124. The delegation illusion: why deploying autonomous AI agents does not diminish principal responsibility
Kong, K.C.Y. (2026). The delegation illusion: why deploying autonomous AI agents does not diminish principal responsibility. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01383-x
Abstract (original): Abstract A growing literature on “agentic AI” — autonomous software agents that plan and execute multi-step actions on a principal’s behalf — has revived the thesis that such systems open a responsibility gap : because the deploying principal neither intends, foresees, nor controls the specific actions an autonomous agent selects, no human can be held fully responsible for resulting harms. This paper argues that the responsibility-gap thesis, as applied to principal-deployed AI agents, rests on a conflation called here the delegation illusion : the inference from a principal’s causal and epistemic remoteness from an outcome to a diminution of her answerability for it. Distinguishing attributability, answerability, and accountability, the paper argues that autonomy, opacity, and adaptivity bear on attributability while leaving intact an answerability grounded, through a standard tracing structure, in the deployer’s guidance control over the prior act of delegation. It defends a principle of responsibility conservation under delegation and operationalises that principle’s central criterion — foreseeability of harmful dispositions at the level of types — by specifying an epistemic base, a standard of diligence, and a constraint on type individuation that blocks vacuous redescription. Answerability is argued to be epistemically graded, but indexed to the deployer’s ex ante access to the dispositional profile of the deployment specification rather than to the particular act. The method is conceptual analysis and normative argumentation; the scope and limitations of the claims are stated explicitly.

126. Knowing whether to rely: continuous awareness as a moral right in human-AI interaction
Sawaf, K. (2026). Knowing whether to rely: continuous awareness as a moral right in human-AI interaction. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01387-7
Abstract (original): Abstract A person relying on a system has a right to know what it is; disclosure secures that right but only while the object of reliance remains materially stable. Under AI, the object does not stay still, and a model’s integrity can change without warning. A moment after disclosure is issued, the system described may have evolved, drifted, or been compromised while the user continues relying on it unaware of its current state. A person’s interest in what they rely on is continuous, but disclosure is episodic, and that epistemic mismatch is not closed by repeating the notice. This paper reconceives AI transparency around a single claim: A person relying on an AI system holds a moral right to continuous awareness of the system’s status, grounded in the same autonomy that informed consent protects. How continuous awareness is delivered is the corollary of that right. The status signal that delivers it must be continuous and vouched from outside the system, since a captured system cannot certify its own integrity. Shaping that signal is therefore an ethical act, not an engineering detail.

129. Restoring Trust in Health Care: Public Scholarship Based Certification of Key Opinion Leaders with Non-Profit Shared Academic-Patient Governance

https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10279
Abstract (original): Abstract Collaboration between physician Key Opinion Leaders (KOLs) and the health care industry accelerates the adoption of new therapies, but public awareness of industry payments and research linking such payments to prescribing behaviors has fueled a sharp decline in trust. 130. Cooptation justice: how litigation weakened the position of married-out women in China
Xiao, H. & Chan, P.C. (2026). Cooptation justice: how litigation weakened the position of married-out women in China. Law & Society Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10116
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines how litigation reshapes civil rights mobilization under authoritarianism through a study of married-out women’s struggles for land rights in rural China over more than three decades. Drawing on in-depth empirical observations, it develops the concept of cooptation justice to explain how litigation shifted from a pressure-building tactic within a broader repertoire of contention to a mechanism of institutional closure. In the earlier period, litigation operated alongside petitioning, protest, media exposure, and support from lawyers and civic actors to generate leverage over local authorities. In the later period, however, shrinking political opportunities and the promotion of formally accessible but politically bounded legal opportunities redirected grievances into courts. Under these conditions, judicial proceedings did not simply fail to remedy discrimination. They absorbed contentious claims into authoritative processes that validated exclusion, weakened alternative avenues of mobilization, and increased the costs of continued contention. The article argues that cooptation justice is a mode of authoritarian governance in which law functions not only as an instrument of legitimacy and dispute management but also as a mechanism for containing civil rights mobilization.

131. When is prosecution in the public interest?
Cornford, A. (2026). When is prosecution in the public interest?. Legal Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lst.2026.10137
Abstract (original): Abstract Prosecutors in England and Wales may start or continue a prosecution only if to do so is in the public interest. No general account has previously been given, however, of what the public interest consists in. This paper aims to provide such an account, drawing on a study of all policies and guidance published by the Crown Prosecution Service (CPS). Its principal conclusion is that, in most cases, CPS guidance imposes on prosecutors an obligation to seek an appropriate penalty for those against whom there is sufficient evidence. In more serious cases, this obligation effectively renders prosecution mandatory. In less serious cases, it may be outweighed by countervailing factors, and out-of-court disposals may constitute an appropriate penalty. However, only a limited range of factors are clearly recognised as counting against prosecution. CPS guidance also lacks clear principles regarding when prosecutors may exceptionally pursue non-punitive aims.

132. Necessity and exception: rethinking the Belgian state of siege in World War I
Dessantis, E.R. (2026). Necessity and exception: rethinking the Belgian state of siege in World War I. International Journal of Law in Context.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s1744552326100640
Abstract (original): Abstract This article takes a closer look at the creation of the Royal Decree of 11 October 1916, which regulated the state of war and the state of siege during World War I. It gave the government the power to restrict certain fundamental rights to such an extent that it effectively amounted to a suspension of those rights. It is notable that the Belgian Constitution prohibits this through Article 187 (formerly Article 130), which states that the Constitution can never, as a whole or partially, be suspended. 134. Deference done with certainty
Zhang, S. (2026). Deference done with certainty. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag058
Abstract (original): Abstract Many experts are modest—they are uncertain whether their opinions are deference-worthy. We can’t defer to such experts by adopting their probabilities as our own, on pain of inconsistency. So how should we defer to them? This paper proposes a new deference principle for modest experts, which I call Total Certainty. Roughly, it says: If you are certain that the expert prefers option X to option Y given your total evidence, then you should prefer X to Y. I give three different characterizations of this principle and compare it with other existing deference principles.

137. A Critical Assessment of the Two Previous Public Deliberation Workshops in South Korea
Lee, K. (2026). A Critical Assessment of the Two Previous Public Deliberation Workshops in South Korea. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70190
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Mini‐publics, which constitute a microcosmic public deliberation process, are increasingly being used in addressing broad policy decisions. Following the trend of applying mini‐publics in diverse policy decisions, the Korean National Health Insurance System experimentally held 16 public deliberation workshops since 2012. This study discusses the 5th and 10th workshops among the 16 conducted, using them as a case example to evaluate the utility and informativeness of mini‐publics in health policy making. After briefly explaining the method and results of the two workshops, this study reviews the main arguments that call for the use of public deliberation in societal decisions. 138. Protests as World‐Making Projects
Dror, L. & Hirji, S. (2026). Protests as World‐Making Projects. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70068
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT On one common way of distinguishing forms of protest, civil disobedience is a communicative act that aims to achieve its goals through moral persuasion, whereas direct action has as its goal to directly obstruct or deter a perceived injustice. In this paper, we'll argue that this distinction does not do justice to a wide range of protest tactics, including encampments, riots, and strikes, that combine a symbolic element with an element of material contestation. Instead, we'll argue that, for a range of protest tactics we call “world‐making projects,” the communicative or persuasive value of the action depends on the parts of the action often deemed violent or uncivil. In particular, we'll suggest that actions that employ material contestation have the ability to undermine the justification for certain ideological beliefs by changing, even just temporarily and in small ways, what the world is like. We cannot accurately describe the function of these protests, evaluate their effectiveness, or assess their justification without first recognizing how their communicative function is bound up with the ways they intervene into public spaces.

140. The Mistake Account of Manipulation
Noggle, R. (2026). The Mistake Account of Manipulation. Journal of Practical Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.3998/jpe.9951
Abstract (original): This paper discusses the four main elements of a theory of manipulation which I call the Mistake Account, and which I defend in my 2025 book on manipulation. This theory claims that manipulation is influence that operates by inducing a mistake into the target’s psychological states or processes. It is often effective due to imperfections in human reasoning, including the shortcuts we use to make good decisions rapidly most of the time. It is often bad because it harms or undermines the autonomy of the target, but it is always bad because it induces mistakes, and mistakes are bad.

141. How to Think about the Basic Moral Status of Manipulation
Cohen, S. (2026). How to Think about the Basic Moral Status of Manipulation. Journal of Practical Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.3998/jpe.9952
Abstract (original): Drawing on the broader theoretical framework that I developed in The Concept and Ethics of Manipulation, this paper examines the question of the basic moral status of manipulation, challenging the prevailing assumption that manipulation is inherently morally problematic. I first argue that we may conclude that manipulation is not pro tanto wrong by showing that no relevant wrong-making feature—such as deception, exploitation, or disrespect for autonomy—is necessarily present in all cases of manipulation. I then address a stronger claim: that manipulation may be inherently morally bad even if not strictly wrong. Against this view, I illustrate the central role of manipulation in the good human life, especially emphasizing its function as a “social lubricant” that enables cooperation, alleviates psychological barriers, and facilitates interpersonal understanding in contexts where rational persuasion is ineffective or counterproductive. I argue that once we appreciate how pervasively manipulation is integral to and indispensable for the human good, it becomes implausible to view it as pro tanto bad. I conclude with a note on “metaethical humanism”: the indispensability of philosophical anthropology for determining moral valence.

145. How to Consider Future People’s Preferences: Generations vs. Transgenerational Communities as Proxies for Preference Aggregation
Negenborn, C.v. (2026). How to Consider Future People’s Preferences: Generations vs. Transgenerational Communities as Proxies for Preference Aggregation. Ethical Theory Moral Pract.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10677-026-10582-8
Abstract (original): Abstract How should the preferences of future people be considered in today’s decision making? Two challenges arise. First, there is uncertainty regarding the content of future preferences: the epistemic challenge lies in eliciting these preferences correctly. Second, present people must be motivated to respect these preferences: the psychological challenge is to induce incorporation of future people into present-day decisions. I compare two methods of intergenerational procedural justice. The first takes a generation-first approach: it considers future individuals primarily as members of their respective generations and seeks to represent their interests through generational ombudspersons. The second focuses on the social group to which a future person belongs. This community-first approach makes room for an individual’s preferences by giving a voice to present members of her transgenerational community. Existing theories of intergenerational justice by and large implicitly follow the generation-first method. However, the community-first approach better addresses both the epistemic and the psychological challenge.

149. “Unprecedented Injustice”: network determinism in the Dutch digital welfare state
Huyskes, D. (2026). “Unprecedented Injustice”: network determinism in the Dutch digital welfare state. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01350-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Automated decision-making systems in the welfare state have produced discriminatory outcomes at scale, as critical scholarship has extensively documented. This article examines the mechanisms through which such outcomes are produced by tracing the cultural, institutional, and technical determinants embedded in their design and use. Drawing on qualitative research conducted in the Netherlands between 2023 and 2024, it combines semi-structured interviews, ethnographic observation, and documentary analysis across three cases: SyRI, the childcare benefits scandal (Toeslagenaffaire), and Rotterdam’s welfare fraud detection system. Rather than asking whether automated systems discriminate, the article asks how discriminatory social classifications become translated into algorithmic risk and acquire the authority of seemingly objective decisions. The analysis identifies four recurring mechanisms: manual pre-selection of groups for scrutiny, the use of records of past suspicion as training data, proxy substitution for sensitive attributes, and the cumulative, intersectional layering of risk indicators. To conceptualize these dynamics, the article develops the notion of network determinism: the practical closure produced when administrative institutions, databases, policy objectives, statistical categories, organizational routines, and computational systems repeatedly reinforce one another, turning contingent classifications into effectively deterministic outcomes for those subjected to them. The findings show that discrimination cannot be attributed to algorithms alone, but emerges from chains of human and institutional choices that distribute both decision-making and responsibility. The Dutch cases therefore demonstrate why technical or legal corrections are insufficient without addressing the socio-cultural assumptions that automated systems are built to operationalize.

151. Will AI save ethics—and make ethicists obsolete?
Tretter, M. (2026). Will AI save ethics—and make ethicists obsolete?. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01384-w
Abstract (original): Abstract In 1982, Stephen Toulmin famously argued that medicine “saved the life of ethics.” This article asks whether AI is now doing something similar—and finds that the answer splits. Working from a broadly Toulminian conception of ethics as a discipline that responds to societal demand for normative orientation by providing analyses and reasoned recommendations, the article develops its argument in two stages. The first is diagnostic. AI is undeniably revitalizing ethics: it generates unprecedented demand for normative orientation and brings ethical questions to the center of public and academic debate. Yet AI appears to be on a trajectory toward becoming increasingly capable of supplying the very outputs for which ethicists have traditionally been consulted. If it can provide these outputs faster, more cheaply, and, in some respects, better, demand for ethics and demand for ethicists may increasingly come apart: ethics as a discipline may flourish even as ethicists as a profession face the prospect of becoming obsolete. In light of this diagnosis, the second stage asks how ethicists might respond. Four strategies are reconstructed: (1) defense and counterattack, (2) adaptive specialization, (3) ethics as an activity, and (4) cooperative transformation. Each strategy is critically assessed, culminating in a reflective appraisal of their respective strengths and limitations. The article concludes by arguing that ethicists must fundamentally reconsider the nature of their expertise and the role they can still claim for themselves in an age increasingly shaped by AI.

153. Individualism Without Individuality: Friedrich Hayek and the Neoliberal Citizen
Tzur, T. (2026). Individualism Without Individuality: Friedrich Hayek and the Neoliberal Citizen. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70081
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Critics of neoliberalism increasingly examine its corrosive effects on democracy. Scholars often turn to Friedrich Hayek's political theory, arguing that it produces citizens who passively conform to spontaneous social orders. Yet insufficient attention has been paid to the normative conception of the human individual underlying his neoliberalism. This article demonstrates that Hayek's theory actively rejects individuality, understood as the cultivation and expression of one's distinct personal character. Individuality requires an experimental approach toward social action and critical reflection on whether social rules express one's character, yet such attitudes are systematically stifled in Hayek's account because they threaten the unequivocal obedience his spontaneous orders demand. 154. Was there ever an Animal Dasein? Heidegger's Concept of Life in the 1924 Aristotle Lecture
Firenze, A. (2026). Was there ever an Animal Dasein? Heidegger's Concept of Life in the 1924 Aristotle Lecture. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70120
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay examines Heidegger's interpretation of the Aristotelian concept of life in his 1924 lecture Basic Concepts of Aristotelian Philosophy . While scholars have emphasized the text's decisive role in the preparation of Being and Time , their readings have largely centered on the notions of praxis and human existence. Departing from this conventional focus, the article brings attention to a neglected aspect of the lecture: the question of animality and its deep ontological connection to human Dasein . I argue that, in contrast to his later works, especially the 1929–1930 lecture The Fundamental Concepts of Metaphysics. World, Finitude, Solitude , where an ontological abyss separates the world‐forming human from the poor‐in‐world animal, the Heidegger of 1924 maintains an unexpected ontological continuity between the two. The essay first shows how the generic category of zoe establishes both humans and animals as ‘being‐in‐the‐world’ ( In‐der‐Welt‐sein ) who share an originary mode of ‘being‐with‐one‐another’ ( Miteinandersein ). It then analyzes how this continuity is grounded in a shared affective ‘disposition’ ( Befindlichkeit ), through which the animal exercises a primordial form of ‘having‐oneself’ ( Sich‐selbst‐Habens ). By revisiting this formative moment in Heidegger's thought, the essay challenges the prevailing view of an absolute divide between animal life and human existence as the definitive paradigm of Heidegger's conception of animality.

155. Norm enforcement, learning and repair: three moral logics in organisational responses to unintended and unwanted healthcare outcomes
Hartman, L. et al. (2026). Norm enforcement, learning and repair: three moral logics in organisational responses to unintended and unwanted healthcare outcomes. J Med Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1136/jme-2026-111894
Abstract (original): Unintended and unwanted outcomes are an inherent aspect of healthcare practice, posing significant moral challenges for organisations on how to do justice to the situation and the people involved, aimed at strengthening safety and preventing future harm. How an organisation responds to these outcomes is crucial, as this affects the quality of care, public trust and can have a profound impact on both patients and healthcare professionals. This paper develops a conceptual framework delineating three distinct moral logics for responding to unintended and unwanted outcomes: norm enforcement (1), learning (2) and repair (3). Enforcement logic (1) emphasises safeguarding patients by dealing with unprofessional or unlawful actions; learning logic (2) seeks to prevent recurrence by improving systems and practices; and repair logic (3) focuses on addressing harm to patients and restoring trust. Our analysis reveals that, while analytically distinct and potentially complementary, these moral logics often overlap in practice and may generate tensions because they rely on different understandings of justice, accountability and appropriate organisational response. Our analysis underscores both the distinctions between these logics and a selected set of tensions that arise when they intersect, focusing on three central configurations of tension while recognising that other combinations are also possible. We identify three key causes of tension: first, norm enforcement (1) allows little room for doubt or reflection, which is essential for learning (2) and repair (3); second, current quality and regulatory systems pay relatively little attention to repair (3); and third, societal demands for publicity and transparency put pressure on learning (2) and repair processes (3). Our study’s key contribution is recognising and understanding such moral logics and tensions. This can guide the development of more coherent and effective responses that ultimately enhance the quality of care and sustain trust in healthcare systems.

160. Terminology matters in medical ethics: on the use of ‘diagnosis’ in philosophical analysis
Saio, T. (2026). Terminology matters in medical ethics: on the use of ‘diagnosis’ in philosophical analysis. J Med Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1136/jme-2026-112540
Abstract (original): Bair’s recent article ( J Med Ethics , 2026) offers a thoughtful Wittgensteinian analysis of ‘understanding’ in informed consent which proposes that comprehension should be evaluated through demonstrated capacity rather than passive reception. However, the author’s choice to frame this conceptual clarification as a ‘Wittgensteinian diagnosis’ introduces significant terminological confusion. In clinical medicine, a ‘diagnosis’ represents a rigorous, empirical and consequential judgement derived from systematic assessment and differential analysis, with consequences for subsequent management. Borrowing this clinical term as an unmarked metaphorical transfer of clinical terminology for philosophical conceptual analysis risks misleading clinician-readers into expecting a novel diagnostic instrument, psychological scale or psychiatric evaluation framework. In an interdisciplinary journal dedicated to medical ethics like the Journal of Medical Ethics , precision in terminology is essential. Humanistic inquiries intersecting with clinical medicine must respect the specific weight and established operational definitions of clinical vocabulary. This response argues that referring to a purely philosophical clarification as a ‘diagnosis’ undermines clear communication between philosophers and clinicians. Conceptual errors are best designated as conceptual errors—not medical conditions requiring ‘diagnosis.’

161. The spatial politics of International Climate Finance: A case of India
Banerjee, S. et al. (2026). The spatial politics of International Climate Finance: A case of India. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261484352
Abstract (original): Do political parties shape where International Climate Funds are allocated? We examine this question in the context of India, where International Climate Funds create opportunities for the central government to leverage externally funded resources to advance domestic political interests. Drawing on a novel geo-coded dataset of project locations from the Kyoto Protocol’s Clean Development Mechanism, we show that projects are more likely to be allocated to constituencies represented by co-partisans of the Prime Minister’s party, and that this effect is strongest in competitive constituencies within states governed by opposition parties, where the central government has the sharpest incentives to build new support. This is surprising because existing literature on the Clean Development Mechanism suggests that decisions on project allocations are depoliticized. By demonstrating otherwise, this article contributes to the literature on International Climate Funds by demonstrating the importance of domestic politics in shaping its distribution.

163. State Neutrality and Housing Tenure: What Can States Promote?
Wells, K. (2026). State Neutrality and Housing Tenure: What Can States Promote?. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70128
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Many existing states, including the US and the UK, promote a particular housing tenure, owner‐occupation. Yet liberalism is commonly associated with commitment to a principle of state neutrality which holds that states ought to be neutral regarding different ideas of the good life. This raises important questions, and ones that have received little philosophical attention. Does a commitment to liberal state neutrality rule out the promotion of owner‐occupation? More broadly, what follows from a commitment to the neutrality principle for state action around housing tenure? In this article, I argue that a weaker neutrality principle, neutrality of justification, allows states wide latitude to promote particular housing tenures, including owner‐occupation. However, on a stronger and, I argue, more tenable reading of the neutrality principle, state promotion of owner‐occupation is plausibly ruled out. What is not ruled out, however, is state promotion of a different tenure: renting. Before making these arguments, I defend the application of the neutrality principle to state action around housing tenure.

167. Mathematical Explanations and Axioms as Rules
Pimentel, E. & Poggiolesi, F. (2026). Mathematical Explanations and Axioms as Rules. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10406-5
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper bridges two distinct traditions – philosophy of mathematics and proof theory – to provide a formal framework for explanatory proofs in mathematics. We focus on explanatory proofs that uncover the grounds of mathematical theorems, and we formalize their structure using a new “axioms-as-rules” approach. Our method ensures the transformation of axioms into inference rules while preserving key logical properties such as soundness, completeness, and cut-admissibility. Two classical examples, the Quadrangle Theorem and Pythagoras’ Theorem, are revisited to show how explanatory steps can be systematically isolated and formalized. 168. The paradox of justice in divided settings
David, R. (2026). The paradox of justice in divided settings. Law & Society Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10115
Abstract (original): Abstract Increasing polarization across many contexts raises the question of whether justice measures exacerbate or mitigate social divisions. To develop hypotheses about how justice operates in divided contexts, this article draws on theoretical perspectives from the interdisciplinary field of transitional justice. It examines convergence and divergence in fairness evaluations using a fully randomized conjoint experiment. The experiment was embedded in nationally stratified online surveys in Japan ( n = 2,700) and South Korea ( n = 3,000). The bilateral setting provided a useful research site for a study of a divided context: contested colonial legacies and a variety of justice measures for addressing the past, which were suitable for experimental manipulation. The results show that most justice measures elicit opposing responses in the two countries while simultaneously reducing differences between them. This reveals a paradox of justice: even divisive justice measures can, under certain conditions, bring groups closer together. This framework enables to classify the effects of justice measures into convergent, divergent, and paradoxical configurations and to consider the theoretical and empirical implications for existing and future experimental research on social conflict and divided societies across disciplines.

170. The Responses to the New Classical Natural Law Theory of Law in Continental European Thomistic Legal Philosophy
Popović, P. (2026). The Responses to the New Classical Natural Law Theory of Law in Continental European Thomistic Legal Philosophy. The American Journal of Jurisprudence.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag017
Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper, I analyze the responses of three emblematic continental European legal philosophers associated with Thomistic legal philosophy—namely, Francesco Viola, Michel Villey, and Javier Hervada—to the main arguments regarding the nature of law advanced by the new classical natural law theory of law, more precisely, by the main proponent of this theory, John Finnis. While Viola’s arguments largely overlap with Finnis’s account of the nature of law, according to which all human law is necessarily posited, Villey and Hervada, in their direct or indirect dialogue with Finnis, ground their theories on the essence of law in phenomena or features such as the thingness of law and law’s radical dependence on the virtue of justice, which enable them to establish the existence of elements of real juridicity outside of the scope of humanly posited law. I argue that it is possible to unify these various Thomistic attempts to elucidate law’s nature within Aquinas’s broad idea of law, which necessarily includes (1) a reference to law’s thingness, (2) law’s conceptual dependence on the virtue of justice, (3) law’s essential reliance on the phenomena of legal norms (both natural and positive), and (4) the connection between the nature of law and human good.

171. The Sir Gerald Fitzmaurice Option: A Classical Natural Law Method for Interpreting the European Convention on Human Rights
Casey, C. (2026). The Sir Gerald Fitzmaurice Option: A Classical Natural Law Method for Interpreting the European Convention on Human Rights. The American Journal of Jurisprudence.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag018
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay makes the case for an alternative approach to the interpretation of the European Convention of Human Rights, one rooted in the classical natural law tradition. The foundation of the alternative approach is a reconstruction of the jurisprudence of one of the European Court of Human Rights’ most heterodox judges, Sir Gerald Fitzmaurice, who was the United Kingdom’s nominated judge between 1974 and 1980. In his dissents, concurrences, and extrajudicial writings, I argue, are the conceptual and normative seeds from which a more promising approach to interpreting the Convention can be grown. My alternative approach does not represent a repudiation of the moral purpose or point of the Convention but is in keeping with one of the principal moral impetuses behind its creation: the desire to protect the most basic and fundamental human rights that are owed as a matter of justice to all persons. I also suggest this alternative method would, if adopted, deflate a great deal of the legal and political critique directed toward the Court.

172. The Principle of Sufficient Reason in Ethics: Lessons from Wolff
Stratmann, J. (2026). The Principle of Sufficient Reason in Ethics: Lessons from Wolff. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag043
Abstract (original): Abstract The principle of sufficient reason (PSR) implies that everything has a sufficient reason. Yet its role in ethics has gone undertheorized. A stark historical exception is found in Christian Wolff, the most influential German philosopher between Leibniz and Kant. I argue that the PSR has indispensable non-normative and normative uses in ethics for Wolff—uses without which ethics would not be possible at all. In sketching these uses, I highlight how Wolff aspires to skirt two Humean challenges. The first concerns how the PSR, which concerns what is, can imply anything about what ought to be. The second concerns how the reasons established through the PSR can be morally motivating. Although hardly unproblematic or uncontroversial, Wolff’s discussion of the PSR in ethics offers both a welcome exception to centuries of undertheorizing and a potential challenge to opponents of the PSR.

173. The changing meaning of left-right self-placement in the United Kingdom
Tasker, J. (2026). The changing meaning of left-right self-placement in the United Kingdom. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261481637
Abstract (original): Understanding how voters use the left-right scale is essential, as it is not only related to vote choice but also underpins how political scientists study ideology. This article finds that the way voters use the left-right scale is variable. Using British Election Study Internet Panel data from 2014 to 2024, I find that while economic and cultural values are both related to left-right self-placement, this varies both between individuals and over time. Those on the left of the self-placement scale can best be differentiated from one another by the strength of their economic values. For those on the right, cultural values are more useful in separating between different self-placement levels. Over time, left-right self-placement has become increasingly related to cultural values alongside economic values, driven by movements by people on both sides of the left-right scale. These findings indicate that even within one country, the meanings of ‘left’ and ‘right’ vary.

https://doi.org/10.1177/00483931261489617
Abstract (original): This article examines the tension between sociology and history as a methodological problem of reconciling general concepts with historical specificity. 175. Justificatory Injustice: Epistemic Injustice in the Availability of Reasons
Mähringer, G. (2026). Justificatory Injustice: Epistemic Injustice in the Availability of Reasons. Moral Philosophy and Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1515/mopp-2025-0001
Abstract (original): Abstract Most people have a need to regard their significant life choices as justified. More than that, they want to be able to give acceptable justificatory reasons to the people in their social milieu. In this paper, I argue that structural disadvantage can lock people into a situation in which they struggle to appeal to their best reasons. I start out with a paradigmatic example inspired by Jennifer Morton’s work on disadvantaged students aiming at upwards mobility and identify a struggle that has more than one cause but amounts to a distinctive experience: that you have objectively good reasons for a life choice, which, however, are not available to you because of your social position. I analyse this ‘unavailability’ of a reason with the help of existing literature on epistemic injustice, in particular hermeneutical injustice, and coin the new term ‘justificatory injustice’. While it can be described as epistemic injustice broadly conceived, justificatory injustice goes beyond epistemic access in a narrow sense and is entangled with features distinctive of practical justification taking place in a concrete social milieu. Justificatory injustice, I argue, can only be remedied in a more integrated justificatory community, which requires equality of condition, also in economic terms.

176. Decoupling Generational Sovereignty: The Cross-Temporal Role of the Sovereign People
Campos, A.S. (2026). Decoupling Generational Sovereignty: The Cross-Temporal Role of the Sovereign People. Moral Philosophy and Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1515/mopp-2025-0072
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines the viability of framing intergenerational power asymmetries in terms of sovereignty, particularly in non-overlapping contexts. It is divided into four parts. First, it surveys the different dimensions of sovereignty, focusing in particular on absoluteness, self-justification, and perpetuity. Second, it applies these dimensions to the concept of generation within constitutional theory. From the perspective of McTaggart’s A-series of time, this analysis shows that intermediate generations do not satisfy the criteria for sovereignty, thereby raising questions about the asymmetric distribution of power in relation to the founding generation. Third, the article adopts McTaggart’s B-series of time and examines fully constituent generations (such as the generation responsible for establishing the first historical constitution), concluding that the concept of sovereignty remains inapplicable. Finally, it considers the conceptual and normative implications of this conclusion. It examines the consequences of simply replacing sovereignty with related concepts (such as independence) in discussions of generations, as well as the possibility of reconciling sovereignty with limits on the power exercised by each generation. Such a reconciliation may draw on arguments from intergenerational justice and political liberalism, but ultimately depends primarily on an argument from cross-temporal popular sovereignty.

177. IslamicLegalBench: Evaluating LLMs knowledge and reasoning of islamic law across 1,200 years of Islamic pluralist legal traditions
Elmahjub, E. et al. (2026). IslamicLegalBench: Evaluating LLMs knowledge and reasoning of islamic law across 1,200 years of Islamic pluralist legal traditions. Artif Intell Law.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10506-026-09535-4
Abstract (original): Abstract As millions of Muslims worldwide turn to LLMs like GPT, Claude, and DeepSeek for religious guidance, a critical question emerges: Can these AI systems reliably reason about Islamic law? This paper introduces , the first multi-school benchmark for evaluating LLM performance across a broad range of Islamic jurisprudence ( fiqh ). Drawing from 37 foundational legal texts spanning 1,200 years and seven schools of jurisprudence, we create 718 evaluation instances across 13 tasks, manually collected and organized by complexity, from basic recall to sophisticated reasoning including legal rationale identification ( ‘illah ), analogical application ( qiyās ), and cross-school synthesis. Our evaluation of nine state-of-the-art LLMs reveals significant limitations. Even the best model achieves only 67.65% correctness with 21.25% hallucination; several models achieved correctness below 35% and hallucination exceeding 55%. Furthermore, few-shot prompting yields no consistent benefit; per-model changes range from -3.64 to +4.16 points, with gains concentrated in already-weak models, consistent with insufficient Islamic legal knowledge in current training data that prompting, under closed-book conditions, could not compensate for . Our analysis reveals why: moderate-complexity tasks (from a human expert’s perspective) requiring exact, verbatim knowledge (e.g., enumerating contract conditions, synthesizing statutory articles) show consistently high error rates and hallucination rates up to 73%, whereas high-complexity tasks show better performance because models rely on semantic generalization and verbose reasoning, projecting competence while lacking precise textual understanding. False premise detection reveals risky sycophantic behavior: under few-shot prompting, 5 of 9 models accept misleading assumptions at rates exceeding 40% (worst: 86.27%). Critically, few-shot prompting worsens sycophancy by 3.49 percentage points. The strong and statistically significant negative correlation (Pearson’s r = –0.91, n = 9, p < 0.01) between the false premise acceptance rate and overall performance indicates that models with higher false Islamic query acceptance rates tend to exhibit lower overall reasoning accuracy. Our findings carry important implications: the path forward for Islamic NLP lies not in lightweight post-hoc techniques such as prompt or instruction tuning but in enriching knowledge. The Islamic NLP community must prioritize training models on comprehensive, large-scale Islamic legal corpora that span classical Hadith collections, jurisprudential works from the major Islamic law schools ( madhabs ), and codified legal compendia.  provides the first systematic evaluation framework for Islamic legal AI systems, revealing critical limitations in platforms that Muslims increasingly rely on for spiritual guidance.

Souverain, T. (2026). Watermarking large language models in Europe: interpreting the AI act in light of technology. Ethics Inf Technol.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10676-026-09918-w
Abstract (original): Abstract To foster trustworthy Artificial Intelligence (AI) within the European Union, the AI Act requires providers to mark and detect the outputs of their general-purpose models. The Article 50 and Recital 133 call for marking methods that are "sufficiently reliable, interoperable, effective and robust". Yet, the rapidly evolving and heterogeneous landscape of watermarks for Large Language Models (LLMs) makes it difficult to determine how these four standards can be translated into concrete and measurable evaluations. Our paper addresses this challenge, anchoring the normativity of European requirements in the multiplicity of watermarking techniques. (iii) Watermarking Comparison: We compare current watermarking methods for LLMs against the operationalised European criteria and show that no approach yet satisfies all four standards. Encouraged by emerging empirical tests, we recommend further research into watermarking directly embedded within the low-level architecture of LLMs.

181. Blueberry Workers, Legal Change, Resistance, and Accommodation in the Coastal North of Peru
Zúñiga, N.T. (2026). Blueberry Workers, Legal Change, Resistance, and Accommodation in the Coastal North of Peru. Law Crit.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10978-026-09439-x
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines the protests of blueberry workers in Peru in 2020 against an agrarian labour regime that legalizes precarious labour conditions, as well as the aftermath of that mobilization. It investigates whether the legal change demanded and achieved can be categorized as resistance, understood as oppositional acts seeking rupture with the system, or as its opposite, accommodation, meaning reformist acts of opposition, acceptance and co-option. The analysis of the workers' mobilization in Peru tells a straightforward story about the function of law in constituting the rules of agrarian capitalism by distributing resources, such as land, into the hands of a few and legitimating unequal bargaining power to the detriment of workers. The findings suggest that the workers' protest constituted an act of accommodation: while it aimed to improve working conditions, it did not seek to overcome the underlying features and dynamics of the agrarian economic model that led to the entrenchment of precarity, unfreedom, and low levels of unionisation. While the mobilization did achieve legal change, its aftermath indicates that workers' actions were accommodating because they reinforced dynamics of co-option that curtail their capacity to organize and engage with projects of rupture.

182. From Dollar Hegemony to Dollar Dominance
Tzouvala, N. & Whyte, J. (2026). From Dollar Hegemony to Dollar Dominance. Law Crit.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10978-026-09444-0
Abstract (original): Abstract This article describes and diagnoses a major shift in the modalities, uses and legal forms of the international role of the US dollar. Drawing from the work of Ranajit Guha, we argue that the balance between coercion and persuasion has shifted in comparison to the neoliberal era of dollar hegemony (early 1980s to early 2010s) and that this shift has reshaped the legal forms through which the currency power of the USA is articulated. Starting from the position that hegemony does not preclude the existence of coercion, we show that during the neoliberal era dollar hegemony enjoyed the consent of ruling classes around the world. Even when it was used to further geopolitical goals of the US, the US used international institutions, international law-making and co-operation amongst legal authorities in order to socialise its views and interests amongst political and legal elites. In contrast, the Trump administration exacerbated an already unfolding trend of openly coercive usages of currency power and the concomitant adoption of unilateral, highly visible executive or legislative action as the predominant legal forms through which the international role of the dollar is sustained and mobilised. We describe this shift as a transition from dollar hegemony to dollar dominance and argue that it has triggered less visible but equally consequential efforts by other states to hedge, rather than outright abandon, the dollar as the dominant currency of global capitalism.

183. Intention-like representations in language models?
Williams, I. (2026). Intention-like representations in language models?. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02605-y
Abstract (original): Abstract A growing chorus of AI researchers and philosophers posit internal representations in large language models (LLMs). But how do these representations relate to the kinds of mental states we routinely ascribe to our fellow humans? While some research has focused on belief- or knowledge-like states in LLMs, there has been comparatively little focus on the question of whether LLMs have intentions . I survey five properties that have been associated with intentions in the philosophical literature, and assess two candidate classes of LLM representations against this set of features. The result is mixed: current evidence suggests that LLMs have representations that are intention-like in many—perhaps surprising—respects, but they differ from human intentions in important ways. 184. Continuity, catastrophic risk, and severe uncertainty
Roussos, J. & Stefánsson, H.O. (2026). Continuity, catastrophic risk, and severe uncertainty. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02610-1
Abstract (original): Abstract The Continuity axiom implies that no catastrophe is so bad that one should not be willing to accept a gamble that might result in that catastrophe, as long as the gamble is sufficiently likely to instead result in some improvement on one’s status quo. The axiom is implied by most normative decision theories, including risk averse alternatives to expected utility theory. We argue that Continuity should be abandoned when evaluating policy options where (a) there is sufficient uncertainty about the correct probabilities, and (b) some options could be catastrophic. However, unlike Bartha and DesRoches (2021) and Steel and Bartha (2023), we argue that there are examples of policy evaluation where, although the outcome could be catastrophic, the relevant probabilities are sufficiently well-grounded such that Continuity should be satisfied.

185. Logic as Elaboration, not Pure Convention
Heyndels, S. (2026). Logic as Elaboration, not Pure Convention. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10477-4
Abstract (original): Abstract While logical expressivism and logical conventionalism share many commitments, the most prominent versions of these positions diverge in the kind of logical inferentialism they endorse. Jared Warren’s logical conventionalism adopts a form of unrestricted inferentialism, whereas logical expressivism naturally supports restricted inferentialism. This contrast is most evident in their respective treatments of the ‘tonk’ problem. This paper develops an expressivist defense of restricted inferentialism in light of Warren’s recent arguments for unrestricted inferentialism. I argue that the restricted inferentialist can accept Warren’s ‘Translation Mistake’ diagnosis of Unilateralist Tonklish, given that it interprets the addition of ‘tonk’ as yielding a semantically conservative extension of the original language. In the case of Bilateralist Tonklish, I argue that the restricted inferentialist can accept Warren’s verdict that it is a meaningless language because it is unfollowable, but that this unfollowability, in turn, is to be given a principled explanation in terms of constraints on inference rules. I contend that Warren’s discussion of ‘tonk’ reinforces the idea that logical connectives must play the expressive role that logical expressivists attribute to them, and that this role motivates restricted inferentialism.

186. Decentering Disruption: Toward a Framework for Intercultural Conceptual Disruption
Claassen, K. (2026). Decentering Disruption: Toward a Framework for Intercultural Conceptual Disruption. Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00624-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Conceptual disruption is typically theorised as a consequence of technological innovation: a new artefact or practice unsettles an entrenched category, and a conceptual gap, overlap, or misalignment results. This paper argues that disruption also occurs when conceptual schemes collide, that is, when concepts travel across geopolitical boundaries and moral frameworks. It introduces Intercultural Conceptual Disruption (ICD) to theorise this second source of disruption. By using the example of the concept of PRIVACY in the African relational ontology of Ubuntu, the paper illustrates that conceptual disruption is not uniform across contexts. What counts as severe in one conceptual scheme may be mild or unremarkable in another, depending on the flexibility of that scheme and its ontological commitments, and the movement of concepts is shaped by asymmetries of epistemic and political power rather than by conceptual need alone. ICD is articulated as three interlinked processes: (1) identifying the type, level, and severity of disruption within a given conceptual scheme; (2) decentering dominant frameworks by addressing the epistemic injustices that privilege some conceptual resources over others; and (3) decolonising concepts that carry undue colonial influence in postcolonial contexts.

188. The Social Dimension of the 1951 Refugee Convention: Balancing International Protection and Social Security Coordination in Central and Eastern Europe
Siwierska, A.M. (2026). The Social Dimension of the 1951 Refugee Convention: Balancing International Protection and Social Security Coordination in Central and Eastern Europe. Camb Yearb Eur Leg Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1017/cel.2026.10036
Abstract (original): Abstract This article advances a doctrinal reinterpretation of the social dimension of the 1951 Convention Relating to the Status of Refugees. It challenges the assumption that bilateral social security coordination mechanisms operate as neutral instruments of transnational administration, arguing that, in situations of forced migration, they may instead function as structural barriers to the effective enjoyment of refugee rights. It argues that Articles 24 and 25 of the Refugee Convention require host states to ensure effective access to social security through functional substitution. The article concludes that refugee protection requires protection-sensitive social security coordination consistent with international and European legal obligations.

191. Affective Knowing in Wang Yangming
Angle, S.C. & Chan, C. (2026). Affective Knowing in Wang Yangming. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70122
Abstract (original): Abstract Wang Yangming 王陽明 (1472–1529) has provoked controversy from his day to our own with his teachings about varieties of knowing ( zhi 知). A notable dimension of Wang's theoretical philosophy is his repeated insistence that the inherent reality of both the heartmind itself, and of knowing, is deeply affective in nature. While scholars have often noted Wang's emphasis on affect, we argue that affect is more fundamental to his view than previous interpretations have generally recognized. We develop an affect‐first interpretation of Wang's view of knowing in three steps. First, we show how joy, sorrow, and stillness jointly characterize the inherent reality of the heartmind. Second, we argue that the affective nature of our heartminds was important to Wang for two reasons, namely that it explains how the heartmind can be self‐sustaining and self‐correcting. Finally, we make explicit how Wang's innovative notion of good knowing ( liangzhi 良知) relates to the heartmind's affective inherent reality and fits together with other varieties of knowing. On our account, liangzhi is the heartmind's inherent reality as viewed through the lens of its responsiveness to the myriad things in the world. We argue that Wang's concepts of “extended knowing” or “genuine knowing”—which are the same thing—are nothing more than pure episodes of liangzhi. Taken as a whole, Wang's account of the heartmind's ways of knowing is consistent and distinctive, notable both for its influence within Neo‐Confucianism and its potential to catalyze new lines of thinking today.

193. When home isn’t home anymore: Towards a political theory of displacement without movement
Souter, J. (2026). When home isn’t home anymore: Towards a political theory of displacement without movement. Eur J Polit Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14748851261477196
Abstract (original): Normative political theorists typically view displacement as a phenomenon that entails forced movement. In this article, I challenge this orthodoxy by arguing that displacement is fundamentally a loss of one's place and/or home that may occur either with or without such movement. After developing a series of conditions to explain when place-based changes precipitate ‘displacement without movement’, I contend that there are at least two key theoretical, ethical and political advantages to introducing this notion into normative political theory. First, the notion allows us to identify a common condition that unites the experiences of those who lose their place and/or home through forced movement and those who lose it in their habitual locations. Second, recognising this common condition may serve as a pathway to much-needed forms of solidarity among not only these two groups, but also non-displaced populations. I then discuss how the idea of displacement without movement may dilute the normative status that is often ascribed to displacement, while arguing that this implication is not problematic for my account. I conclude that displacement without movement should be viewed as a pressing global harm which deserves far greater attention by normative political theorists.

195. Must Longtermists Wish Us All Dead? A Quadrilemma
Eyal, N. et al. (2026). Must Longtermists Wish Us All Dead? A Quadrilemma. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70129
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Longtermists assign great moral importance to making the long‐term future as good as possible, especially by ensuring the survival of humanity. We argue, to the contrary, that on assumptions that are plausible, conservative, or shared by leading longtermists, human extinction will likely augment total utility among human and non‐human animals on Earth. The reason for this surprising conclusion is that our extinction would increase the number of sentient non‐human animals, which in turn would dramatically increase total utility. Possible responses by longtermists leave them with a difficult quadrilemma. They must either (a) welcome human extinction; or (b) welcome the extinction of most non‐human animals; or (c) tolerate continued human existence and related animal suffering as mere means to ushering in high‐tech transformations of the animal kingdom, space colonization, or digital sentience – relatively esoteric, unpopular, and speculative parts of longtermism; or (d) reject key tenets of longtermism like anti‐speciesism or the equal evaluation of making beings happy and of making happy beings. Embracing any horn of this quadrilemma would cut deep into longtermists' current commitments and/or dash central longtermists' hopes of popular appeal.

199. The Juridification of Unliveable Social Housing: Ownership, Citizenship, and Responsibility
Woodhouse, R. (2026). The Juridification of Unliveable Social Housing: Ownership, Citizenship, and Responsibility. Law Crit.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10978-026-09441-3
Abstract (original): Abstract With the increase of social housing deaths and crisis of housing conditions in Britain, it is imperative to understand the role of law and state-led forms of privatisation in making this situation possible. This article will focus on the historical and political context of the recent death of Awaab Ishak, who died in his socially-rented home due to mould overexposure. I argue that the law was not lacking here but will show how the juridification of housing governance through ownership mechanisms has been key in protecting the legitimacy of the state and capital accumulation more broadly in a British society ridden with economic crisis and social immiseration. To do this, I will theorise the development of a juridical framework that maintains the link between housing, the state, and capital by interpolating ownership and citizenship, in turn making social housing tenants responsible for unliveable social assets through the relocation of risk into their everyday lives. Going beyond critiques of housing privatisation and individualisation within contemporary accumulation strategies that depart from spatial forms of social organisation, I will argue that the link between law and capital needs to be understood temporally to account for the ongoing structural presence of non-accountability in the sector, that will not be simply remedied with incoming legislation. As such, renewed entwinements of property and racialisation that break with modern norms of individualistic ownership practices produce the conditions for the Ishak family’s experience of the sector and, ultimately and maddeningly, Awaab’s death.

200. What is AI ethics, and what is it good for? Clarifying debates on AI ethics
Schomäcker, A. et al. (2026). What is AI ethics, and what is it good for? Clarifying debates on AI ethics. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01326-6
Abstract (original): Abstract The rapid integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into various aspects of society has raised pressing ethical concerns, from privacy and fairness to autonomy and safety. In response, the field of AI ethics has grown significantly, with a particular focus on developing guidelines to support the responsible development and use of AI. However, this trend has faced criticism, with concerns about “ethics washing” and skepticism regarding the overall value of AI ethics. In this paper, we argue that such criticisms often rely on an incomplete understanding of AI ethics. To address this, we propose an understanding of AI ethics that distinguishes between diverse instances of AI ethics (viz., research, education, practice, and governance instruments) and the purposes they serve (viz., knowledge, communication, and application). By disentangling these elements, we can illuminate different ways of doing AI ethics and provide a structured way to assess their value. Our arguments highlight that critiques of specific instances and purposes of AI ethics do not justify dismissing AI ethics altogether. We conclude by demonstrating how our proposed distinctions can reconcile existing defenses of AI ethics and guide future discourse, ensuring that the field remains both robust and relevant in addressing the ethical challenges posed by AI.

202. Evaluation methods and metrics towards trustworthy AI
Leersum, C.M.v. & Maathuis, C. (2026). Evaluation methods and metrics towards trustworthy AI. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01349-z
Abstract (original): Abstract The ongoing developments in Artificial Intelligence (AI) are introducing new technologies, initiatives, and applications. To assure that these developments do not translate into potential risks that are not captured, understood, measured, and managed, a trustworthiness approach is necessary. AI trustworthiness is treated as a multidimensional construct that spans transparency, safety, security, privacy, robustness, and fairness. We argue that these properties they should be translated into explicit, testable criteria. This article aims to synthesize evaluation approaches that make AI trustworthiness observable and measurable, enabling stakeholders to identify gaps, prioritize improvements, and align system behaviour with ethical, social, and legal values. A scoping review (resulting in 27 papers) is augmented with knowledge of 6 experts to map (i) the main trustworthy AI aspects, (ii) the evaluation methods used to assess these aspects, (iii) the metrics applied within them, and (iv) recommendations proposed to strengthen trustworthiness. The results suggest that current evaluation practices interpret and operationalize trustworthy AI in diverse ways. They often rely on established methods or benchmarks that may imply bias, limited comparability, or provide limited insight into the reasoning quality. We recommend to design evaluation frameworks that explicitly target trustworthiness aspects from a multidisciplinary angle and being instantiated to specific contexts or problems.

205. Why applied AI ethics in healthcare requires the integration of empirical inquiry and normative ethical analysis
Johansson, J.V. & Fahlquist, J.N. (2026). Why applied AI ethics in healthcare requires the integration of empirical inquiry and normative ethical analysis. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01370-2
Abstract (original): Abstract AI technologies in healthcare are increasingly subject to ethical scrutiny, often structured around high‑level ethical principles such as fairness, transparency, and accountability. While these principles are widely endorsed, their application frequently remains abstract, underspecified, and detached from the empirical contexts in which AI systems are developed and used. This paper argues that empirical ethics provides a necessary foundation for applied AI ethics in healthcare by enabling the ethically relevant contextualisation of normative principles through a reciprocal relationship between empirical and normative ethical analysis. In doing so, the paper addresses a persistent gap between ethical principles and empirical research in AI ethics by showing how these domains can be methodologically integrated rather than treated as separate or sequential. Drawing on empirical examples and our own experience from medical AI research and stakeholder engagement, we illustrate how empirical inquiry and normative ethical analysis, can work together to clarify the meaning of ethical values such as fairness, identify ethically salient risks and trade‑offs, and support ethically robust decision‑making across the AI lifecycle, including research, development, and procurement. We argue that without systematic and methodologically grounded empirical engagement, AI ethics risks becoming either toothless or merely procedural, or sliding into forms of ethical relativism in which empirical findings are treated as normatively sufficient. The article contributes to ongoing debates on how to operationalise AI ethics and highlights the role that empirical ethics can play in bridging ethical theory, law, and practice.

206. Taxonomies for connecting technical components of AI-based fraud detection systems and fairness approaches
Berendsen, S. & Beauxis-Aussalet, E. (2026). Taxonomies for connecting technical components of AI-based fraud detection systems and fairness approaches. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01375-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Our study investigates the inter-dependencies between the technical components of AI-based fraud detection systems and fairness approaches. Here, fairness approaches refer to any method or process to address, specify, measure or mitigate unfairness in AI systems. Despite extensive research on both AI-based fraud detection systems and AI fairness, these domains are often treated separately, leading to a fragmented understanding of both domains. This fragmentation leads to inconsistent terminologies and concepts, and fails to identify the inter-dependencies between technical components of fraud detection systems and fairness approaches. These are the research gaps we address in this study. Through a literature review of 78 papers, we synthesize existing concepts and terminologies into two structured taxonomies that allow to document these inter-dependencies. One taxonomy describes the technical components of AI fraud detection systems and another describes fairness approaches. A joint evaluation of the taxonomies reveals how technical components are impacting fairness, and how fairness considerations should govern the design and implementation of these technical components. Our findings show that four technical components (regarding class imbalance, overlapping class distributions, data quality, and adaptive learning) are intertwined with fairness approaches in fraud detection. The proposed taxonomies, and the inter-dependencies we identify, support AI practitioners and decision-makers in designing fair fraud detection systems. Additionally, the taxonomies provide structure to research investigating how technical components and technical decisions impact fairness and, vice versa, investigating how fairness approaches impact the technical design of fraud detection systems.

207. The pitfalls of AI detection in academic writing: bias, false positives, and the need for inclusive assessment
Angelier, V. (2026). The pitfalls of AI detection in academic writing: bias, false positives, and the need for inclusive assessment. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01376-w
Abstract (original): Abstract AI-text detectors are increasingly used to police authorship in student assessment and scholarly publishing. This paper argues that they are the wrong instrument for that task. Its design combines a critical synthesis of empirical, information-theoretic, and policy evidence with an original documented exploratory multi-tool audit. The review synthesises empirical evaluations, information-theoretic results, and institutional policy decisions to develop a conceptual account of what detectors can directly observe: statistical or learned properties of submitted text, not provenance itself. Predictability-related properties such as low perplexity, reduced variation in local predictability, and formulaic density provide one mechanism through which both additional-language writing and highly conventional academic prose may become vulnerable to machine-authorship classification. While a growing number of universities across several national higher-education systems have disabled or restricted AI detection in high-stakes assessment, and a New York court has annulled a misconduct finding that rested on a detector score, detector reliance persists in three under-examined arenas: individual grading practice at scale, scholarly journal screening, and institutions without formal policy revision. The paper adds a documented exploratory multi-tool audit in which a single human-authored manuscript, submitted to five commercial detectors on the same day, received materially different classifications spanning from “0% human” to “Human Generated”, demonstrating substantial cross-tool inconsistency and limited independent verifiability of the underlying classifications. A paired exploratory comparison further found that extensive machine rewriting by a commercial “humanizer” materially degraded the manuscript while producing only a small change in the reported detection score. The paper distinguishes genuine false positives from definitional boundary cases involving AI-assisted translation and polishing, engages the counter-argument that AI-polished prose may itself flatten linguistic diversity in academic English, and proposes a concrete process-oriented assessment model based on declared starting points, versioned drafting, process artefacts, and targeted oral verification. The model predates generative AI in its component practices but acquires renewed relevance because final-text classification cannot establish authorship, understanding, or misconduct on its own.

208. How ethics operationalization breaks down in practice: expert perspectives from healthcare AI
Minocher, R. et al. (2026). How ethics operationalization breaks down in practice: expert perspectives from healthcare AI. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01381-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Efforts to promote “Trustworthy AI” have focused on operationalizing high-level ethical principles through tools, standards, and governance mechanisms. Yet a persistent gap remains between ethical guidance and day-to-day development practice. We conducted semi-structured interviews with ten experts in AI ethics, healthcare AI, and socio-technical research to examine how ethical principles are operationalized in real-world AI development. Participants described several ways in which efforts to operationalize ethical principles break down in practice. While existing operationalization approaches were viewed as useful, participants discussed conceptual, epistemic, institutional, and socio-technical conditions that constrain their effectiveness. Our findings suggest that the operationalization gap cannot be understood independently of the broader contexts in which AI systems are designed, developed, and governed.
