212. Unjust Aspects of the Epistemic Marketplace: Idea Appropriation, Bullshit, and Epistemic Injustice
Licon, J.A. (2026). Unjust Aspects of the Epistemic Marketplace: Idea Appropriation, Bullshit, and Epistemic Injustice. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10134
Abstract (original): Abstract Epistemic injustice happens when a subject is wronged in their “capacity as a knower.” This paper argues that speakers/writers and hearers/readers suffer epistemic injustice under two scenarios: idea appropriation and bullshitting. Epistemic injustice happens when a speaker or writer suffers idea appropriation, where they are deprived of the credit and benefits of their cognitive labor, or when epistemic agents consume bullshit that exploits their limited access to the institutional resources required to interrogate and resist it, treating them as a mere means to an end, e.g., when a politician bullshits voters to secure their vote at election time. These unsavory practices are incentivized by reputational boosts and other social goods with the risk of reputational damage if detected. This paper ends by reviewing how widespread epistemic injustice hampers the epistemic marketplace.

247. Revisiting Roth’s Paradox of Historical Narratives
Jones, J.M. (2026). Revisiting Roth’s Paradox of Historical Narratives. Analysis.
https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag073
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper presents Paul Roth’s Paradox of Historical Narratives and three options: reject the paradox, accept the paradox, or resolve the paradox. I show how the paradox motivates a view called ‘narrativism’ in the philosophy of history, and that narrativists must either accept or resolve the paradox. 248. Generative AI and a Sceptical Challenge to Digital Testimony
Church, I.M. (2026). Generative AI and a Sceptical Challenge to Digital Testimony. Analysis.
https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag074
Abstract (original): Abstract Generative AI threatens knowledge online not merely by producing falsehoods, but by changing the epistemic environment in which online beliefs are formed. I argue that the spread of convincing AI-generated counterfeits creates a local sceptical challenge to digital testimony. The challenge is not that ordinary subjects must rule out every sceptical possibility. Rather, for many beliefs formed solely from unauthenticated online materials, ordinary subjects are situated among nearby, method-indistinguishable bad cases. In such environments, even true beliefs formed from genuine digital items may fail to satisfy a familiar anti-luck condition on knowledge. This does not show that we never know from online sources. It shows instead that digital-only uptake often falls short of knowledge unless supported by provenance, corroboration, or other independent checks. I close by suggesting that this narrowing of online knowledge may reinforce existing patterns of epistemic injustice.

249. Surveillance Capitalism and Governmentality Foucault in the Age of Digitization
Yin, R. (2026). Surveillance Capitalism and Governmentality Foucault in the Age of Digitization. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70082
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article examines contemporary digital governance through a dialogue between Shoshana Zuboff's theory of surveillance capitalism and Michel Foucault's analysis of power and governmentality. Although Zuboff offers a compelling account of the economic logics underlying digital domination, her framework tends to locate resistance primarily in external regulation and legal reform. This article argues that such an approach overlooks the subjectifying effects through which digital governance shapes compliant and predictable subjects. Drawing on Foucault, it reconceptualizes surveillance capitalism as an intensification of existing governmental rationalities and advances the notion of counter‐conduct to analyze how subjects negotiate and resist digital power from within.

250. Epistemic Actualism
Gallow, J.D. (2026). Epistemic Actualism. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70067
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT According to veritism, your epistemic goal is to believe what's true. According to actualist veritism, your epistemic goal is to believe what's actually true. At first glance, it seems that the pursuit of truth and the pursuit of actual truth will lead to the same destination. This will follow for evidential decision theorists; but if we are causal decision theorists (as I am), then pursuing truth and pursuing actual truth will lead us to different destinations. In this paper, I assume causal decision theory and argue for actualist veritism over veritism. My argument is that veritism does not respect the direction of fit of belief, since it incentivizes changing the world to match your beliefs, rather than changing your beliefs to match the world as it is. In contrast, actualist veritism honors the direction of fit of belief, and only incentivizes changing your beliefs to match the world as it is. For this reason, actualist veritism allows epistemic teleologists to escape “epistemic bribery” objections, to doubt self‐verifying propositions like “someone believes this proposition,” and to maintain coherence in the face of self‐falsifying propositions like “I don't believe this proposition.”

252. “This is human life, so I don’t know what compensation I can attach to it”: perceptions on compensation in human infection challenge studies in Lusaka, Zambia
Mwamba, C. et al. (2026). “This is human life, so I don’t know what compensation I can attach to it”: perceptions on compensation in human infection challenge studies in Lusaka, Zambia. J Med Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1136/jme-2024-110510
Abstract (original): Human infection challenge (HIC) studies where healthy volunteers are deliberately infected expedite vaccine development. However, agreement on fair compensation that avoids both exploitation and undue inducement remains elusive. Without clear compensation policies or guidelines, implementation of HIC studies remains a challenge in Zambia. We conducted 5 focus group discussions and 15 in-depth interviews on compensation prior to initiation of a typhoid HIC study among healthy adults from higher learning institutions in Lusaka, Zambia. Thematic analysis revealed HIC-specific considerations for compensation, including compensation allocated to research participants and their families for potential risk of immediate and long-term side effects from deliberate infection. Preferences for compensation timing varied, with some advocating for upfront compensation and others favouring compensation after participation to account for lived experiences of side-effects, time commitment and emotional burden. Compensation preferences included monetary and non-monetary compensation in the form of long-term medical coverage and tuition for university/college education. However, views on the value of the compensation varied, highlighting the complexity of determining appropriate compensation that balances voluntariness with fair recognition of risks and sacrifices. Sustained engagement of ethics and regulatory authorities with potential research participants must be encouraged to ensure ethically responsive research and to build public trust in HIC studies.

261. The Impact of Artificial Intelligence on Indian Economy: Prospects and Challenges
Bhattacharya, P. & Rath, B.N. (2026). The Impact of Artificial Intelligence on Indian Economy: Prospects and Challenges. Science, Technology and Society.
https://doi.org/10.1177/09717218261480253
Abstract (original): This study assesses the impact of artificial intelligence (AI) on Indian economy. To do so, we first analyse the linkage between AI, employment and economic growth. Second, this study elaborates on the role of AI in shaping India’s Human Development Index (HDI) and compares it with three major economies. Finally, to supplement the analysis, the article makes a quantitative assessment of the effect of AI on economic growth in India. The analysis does not reveal that the displacement effect of AI on employment outweighs the complementary effect in the Indian context so far. However, from the demand side perspective, there might be a reduction in demand for non-AI-related jobs and an increase in AI-related jobs. Further, results show that an increase in AI vibrancy index support better performance of HDI in India in comparison to the China, Germany and the US. Finally, by employing an ARDL model, the results reveal that AI only positively affect the economic growth in the long-run but not in the short run. From a policy perspective, while the Government of India has taken proactive steps for AI-enabled schemes, greater emphasis on appropriate strategies is required to generate more employment through the adoption of AI.

262. Artificial Intelligence, Skills Mismatch and Inclusive Industrial Transformation: Evidence from Vietnam’s Automotive Sector
Binh, T.T.C. et al. (2026). Artificial Intelligence, Skills Mismatch and Inclusive Industrial Transformation: Evidence from Vietnam’s Automotive Sector. Science, Technology and Society.
https://doi.org/10.1177/09717218261483822
Abstract (original): Artificial intelligence (AI) and Industry 4.0 technologies are transforming manufacturing systems globally, with significant implications for labour markets in emerging economies. This article examines how AI adoption reshapes employment structures, skills demand, and training effectiveness in the automotive manufacturing sector in Vietnam. Drawing on the frameworks of skill-biased technological change and routine-biased technological change, the study develops an integrated model linking AI adoption to task reconfiguration, skills mismatch, and employment outcomes. Using labour force statistics, the findings show that AI adoption increases demand for high-skilled and hybrid technical workers while reducing demand for others. However, substantial skills mismatches persist, particularly among domestic firms and small and medium-sized enterprises. Training systems play a critical mediating role, with firms investing in workforce development experiencing better productivity and labour retention outcomes. The article argues that, without coordinated policies linking industrial upgrading, skills development, and social protection, AI-driven transformation may exacerbate inequality.

263. The Problem of State Culture
Bagus, P. (2026). The Problem of State Culture. Journal of Libertarian Studies.
https://doi.org/10.35297/001c.167786
Abstract (original): This article introduces the concept of state culture to describe the subtle but profound ways in which the modern state shapes norms, values, language, and social conventions. Although property rights and formal freedoms may persist, cultural life is increasingly influenced—if not dominated—by state interventions. Through education, media control, financial incentives, and regulation, the state fosters a cultural environment that supports its own legitimacy and expansion. This has given rise to phenomena such as wokeness, environmental, social, and governance (ESG) compliance, and cancel culture, which are not spontaneous expressions of civil society but symptoms of a long-standing cultural distortion induced by state power. Many seemingly private decisions—such as corporate censorship and social ostracism—occur within this distorted cultural framework and cannot be interpreted as fully voluntary. As such, libertarians face a dilemma: While they must defend property rights, they must also recognize that culture itself can be corrupted through systematic violations of property rights. Drawing on insights from Murray Rothbard, the article proposes that state institutions, as long as they exist, should be operated in ways that align with the cultural norms that would emerge in a truly free society. The goal is not cultural neutrality—impossible under statism—but the active support of values conducive to liberty: private property, personal responsibility, truth, family, and voluntary cooperation.

264. How Is the Paradox of Blackmail Paradoxical?
Wysocki, I. (2026). How Is the Paradox of Blackmail Paradoxical?. Journal of Libertarian Studies.
https://doi.org/10.35297/001c.168707
Abstract (original): The main aspiration of the present article is to elucidate what it would mean for the paradox of blackmail to be solved rather than dissolved . We begin by increasing the sense of paradoxicality of blackmail. To this end, we formalize the paradox. We demonstrate that it relies on a certain prima facie plausible principle of closure (what we call disjunctive closure ). This principle is quite unlike the principle which holds that the permissibility of action A and the permissibility of action B render doing A and B together permissible. On the principle of disjunctive closure, the paradox of blackmail seems to breed a genuine contradiction. Having adequately formulated the paradox, we turn to a normative point: What would count as a satisfactory solution to the paradox of blackmail? Informed by the treatment of paradoxes in logic, we argue that to take the sting out of the paradox of blackmail, we must embrace its core commitments and still explain how apparently contradictory verdicts might nevertheless arise. The upshot of the above considerations only strengthens our contention that what we proposed as a solution to the paradox of blackmail in Dominiak and Wysocki (2026) is indeed an adequate solution rather than a mere dissolution .

265. Can general large language models (LLM) pass a hands-on patent attorney test? A study on LLM capabilities
Khera, B. et al. (2026). Can general large language models (LLM) pass a hands-on patent attorney test? A study on LLM capabilities. Artif Intell Law.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10506-026-09543-4
Abstract (original): Abstract The legal field already uses various large language models (LLMs) in real-world applications. The practical use in the patent domain concentrates on general pre-trained and foundation models without domain specificity. However, little is known and documented about their performance; their limitations are poorly studied and documented. Quality control and any targeted improvement of natural language processing in patent law require such information. We evaluated several open-source and proprietary LLMs—including the GPT series, Anthropic, DeepSeek, and Llama-3 variants—on parts of the European qualifying examination (EQE) for future European patent attorneys. The chosen tests are intentionally technical and designed with little room for interpretation so that models cannot hide wrong associations and answers behind apparent eloquence. OpenAI o1 led with 0.82 accuracy and 0.81 F1 score, whereas AWS (Amazon Web Services) Llama 3.1 8B lagged at 0.50 accuracy, and a Python-deployed Llama 3.1 8B scored 0.55. The latter two are within the range of mere guessing for the two-option forced-choice design. None of the evaluated models could have fully passed the examination, as accuracy never exceeded the average threshold of 0.90 required for professional-level standards—also not models that are regularly promoted for their assumed beyond-PhD- and bar-admitted-lawyer-level performance. GPT-4o excelled at integrating text and graphics, whereas Claude 3 Opus often struggled to maintain formatting coherence. Human patent experts evaluated the textual justifications to identify and understand problems at the argument level. They uncovered various critical shortcomings of each model. They valued clarity and legal rationale over the raw correctness of the answers, which revealed misalignment between automatic metrics and expert judgment. Model outputs were sensitive to modest temperature changes and prompt wording, which underscores the continued necessity of expert oversight. Although the arguments and our analysis of the training corpora demonstrate that the relevant legal texts were part of pre-training, the models, e.g., their embeddings, demonstrated potential for improvement in domain-specific language aspects. Future work should target logical consistency, robust multimodality, and adaptive prompting to approach human-level patent proficiency. In summary, despite the outstanding performance of recent large models, the general public might overestimate their capabilities. The field has a long way to go to develop a virtual patent attorney. This paper aims to point out several specific limitations that need solutions.

267. Intersectional Gender Representation in Corporate Social Media and Corresponding AI-Generated Images: Hallucinatory AI or Anamorphic AI in a House of Illusions?
Dhanesh, G.S. et al. (2026). Intersectional Gender Representation in Corporate Social Media and Corresponding AI-Generated Images: Hallucinatory AI or Anamorphic AI in a House of Illusions?. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06461-y
Abstract (original): Abstract This study examined how companies visually constructed intersectional gender representation in their social media posts and how Generative AI may reproduce or reshape these practices. Drawing on gender representation research in corporate communication, the Stereotype Content Model, and visual social semiotics, we analyzed 300 Instagram posts from the top 100 Fortune 500 B2C companies alongside 300 AI-generated images created to mirror them. Using mixed-methods visual social semiotic content analysis, we compared compositional, interactional, and representational meanings, positioning representation as distributive and recognitional justice. Findings indicated that corporate imagery often signaled performative inclusivity in compositional and interactional meanings, while reinforcing persistent hierarchies in representational meanings. In contrast, AI-generated imagery appeared anamorphic, sometimes reflecting dominant representational patterns and other times distorting or reversing them in ways that rendered identity markers ambiguous or exaggerated. These findings highlight ethical challenges in integrating AI into corporate communication and underscore the need for organizational reflexivity, transparency, and accountability in visual content creation. This study contributes to emerging scholarship on AI ethics and gender representation in corporate communication and offers practical guidance for managers seeking to ensure that the use of AI supports inclusivity and social justice.

268. Breaking Symmetry: A New Directionality Criterion for Regularity Theories of Causation
Baumgartner, M. & Lampert, T. (2026). Breaking Symmetry: A New Directionality Criterion for Regularity Theories of Causation. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01150-8
Abstract (original): Abstract It is one of the most frequent criticisms of regularity theories of causation that they cannot distinguish between causes and effects based on Boolean determination relations of sufficiency and necessity alone—jeopardizing their goal of reducing causation to Boolean determination. The criticism rests on the fact that determination relations can be symmetric but causation (in acyclic structures) cannot. Regularity theorists have countered by arguing that only simplistic determination structures are symmetric, and by insisting that structures with a minimal complexity of two alternative causes per effect exhibit non-symmetric determination relations. In this paper, we show that this regularity-theoretic response is insufficient to break symmetries of determination and, thus, to identify the direction of causation. To solve this problem, we introduce a new directionality criterion and demonstrate that it suffices to distinguish causes from effects solely on the basis of Boolean determination relations.

269. Why Does Mengzi Consider Human Nature Good?
Choi, D. (2026). Why Does Mengzi Consider Human Nature Good?. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70121
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay examines the theoretical relation between moral sentiments and the goodness of human nature in Mengzi's moral philosophy. Mengzi's claim that human nature is good is often understood through the heart of compassion and the sages' exemplary achievements. I argue that both approaches are incomplete: they show how the moral potential is actualized but insufficiently explains how the goodness of human nature itself is recognized or validated. Instead, I argue that Mengzi also suggests a spectatorial account of moral evaluation. From the spectator's standpoint, we discern goodness through shared sentiments of approbation, which are grounded in the hearts of shame and approval. Our approvals of virtuous agents function to indicate their goodness and provide empirical support for the claim that human nature contains an inherent potential for goodness. I show the merit of this spectatorial perspective with an enriched interpretation of 3A1, where the claim that human nature is good first appears. By combining agential and spectatorial perspectives, Mengzi develops a coherent account of the goodness of human nature that encourages agential moral self‐cultivation, strengthened from a spectatorial examination of goodness.

270. Referential Indeterminacy and
                    De Re
                    Thoughts
Assadian, B. (2026). Referential Indeterminacy and
                    De Re
                    Thoughts. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70066
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Referential indeterminacy may seem to leave little room for de re or singular thoughts about ordinary objects. I argue that this impression is misleading. We can direct our thoughts at particular objects, even though there is no fact of the matter as to which objects are referred to by the singular terms purporting to stand for them. By distinguishing between weak and strong de re attitudes, I show that speakers can retain a weak form of de re belief despite the absence of determinate reference. I extend the same distinction to knowledge of reference, thereby explaining how linguistic understanding survives the indeterminacy of reference. I also briefly explore some broader implications of this picture for the theory of reference and for the status of genuine singular terms.

271. Corporate (Il)legal Enclosures: How Fraudulent Land Titles Override Community Rights in Forestry Land Conflicts in Bahia, Brazil
Kröger, M. et al. (2026). Corporate (Il)legal Enclosures: How Fraudulent Land Titles Override Community Rights in Forestry Land Conflicts in Bahia, Brazil. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06473-8
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines the ethical and legal dimensions of corporate land grabbing in Bahia, Brazil, focusing on the forestry sector’s use of fraud, legal manipulation, and violence to acquire land. Through a case study of Veracel Celulose, a joint venture between the Swedish-Finnish corporation Stora Enso and the Brazilian company Suzano, we analyze how corporations exploit legal ambiguity and manipulate land registries to dispossess local populations. Drawing on archival data from federal and state land governance institutions, we develop the concept of corporate (il)legal enclosure, defined as the process through which corporations transform contested, public, or informally held land into de facto corporate property by mobilizing intertwined legal and illegal practices that exclude local populations from access, use, and control over land. Our findings show how title forgery, cadastral manipulation, administrative procedures, and legal regularization operate together to convert fraudulent land claims into seemingly legitimate corporate property. We demonstrate that land grabbing increasingly occurs not only through violence or outright illegality, but through the strategic mobilization of legal and bureaucratic institutions that render dispossession lawful in practice. By integrating insights from agrarian studies, sociolegal scholarship, and business ethics, the article advances a contextually grounded understanding of land-based injustice and challenges compliance-oriented approaches to business ethics. In doing so, it highlights the need for business ethics scholarship to engage more directly with the role of law in enabling and legitimizing corporate dispossession.

273. Memory reports as state descriptions: an Iranian perspective
Smith, R.W. (2026). Memory reports as state descriptions: an Iranian perspective. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02607-w
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper investigates the semantic properties of memory reports in the Iranian languages Persian and Sorani Kurdish. In these languages, memory predicates are constructed out of a noun meaning ‘memory,’ the copula, and a locative preposition. I demonstrate that memory predicates in these languages pass diagnostics for abstract state predicates, distinguishing them from imagination reports, to which memory reports have been compared in the recent philosophical and linguistic literature. Building on decompositional approaches to attitude predicates, I develop a novel compositional semantics for memory predicates in these languages that accounts for their form, stativity, and differences from imagination predicates. This work contributes to the growing literature on the semantics of memory reports in natural language cross-linguistically, and discusses implications for the relationship between natural language semantics and the philosophy of memory.

277. The ramifications of LLM watermarks for authorship and groups
Räz, T. (2026). The ramifications of LLM watermarks for authorship and groups. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01389-5
Abstract (original): Abstract Recently, watermarking schemes for large language models (LLMs) have been proposed to distinguish between text generated by machines and by humans. This paper explores philosophical, political, and ethical ramifications of developing and deploying watermarking schemes. A definition of authorship attribution that encompasses both machines (LLMs) and humans is proposed as a backdrop. It is argued that private watermarks may provide private companies with sweeping rights to determine authorship, which is incompatible with traditional standards of authorship attribution. Then, possible ramifications of the so-called “entropy dependence” of watermarking mechanisms are explored. It is argued that entropy levels may vary across different, socially salient groups. This could lead to group-dependent disparities in the detection rates of machine-generated text. Specifically, groups with a greater interest in low-entropy text may face challenges in detecting machine-generated text that is of interest to them.

279. Fairness versus profitability in automated machine learning

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01395-7
Abstract (original): Abstract We consider the application of automated machine learning models in a real credit lending platform, and empirically study whether the trade-off between fairness and profitability can be improved by means of fairness processing methods. Fairness is evaluated along the principle of Separation, while profitability is computed as a loan-level profit function, aggregated at the portfolio level. The empirical findings reveal that none of the considered fairness processors reduces bias without an increase in costs, in the form of higher false positive or of higher false negative rates.

280. AI resistance and epistemic agency: toward a relational framework for democratic and human-centered AI governance
Malik, F.S. (2026). AI resistance and epistemic agency: toward a relational framework for democratic and human-centered AI governance. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01396-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) increasingly mediates communication, interpretation, decision-making, and knowledge production, raising questions about how computational systems acquire epistemic authority. At the same time, resistance to AI is emerging across educational, creative, infrastructural, professional, and civic settings. This conceptual paper examines what such resistance reveals about the distribution and contestability of epistemic authority in AI-mediated environments. Drawing on Science and Technology Studies, critical AI studies, AI ethics, political philosophy, and epistemic theory, it extends emerging accounts of AI-mediated epistemic capture by connecting them to epistemic agency, resistance, and governance . Epistemic capture is conceptualized as a gradual and relational process through which interpretive authority can become increasingly organized around computational systems and difficult to contest. The paper develops a typology encompassing quiet refusal, epistemic, infrastructural, educational, creative, and decolonial resistance, together with reclamation, while emphasizing that resistance is not inherently emancipatory or epistemically beneficial. Instead, resistance can operate diagnostically by revealing conditions under which epistemic agency becomes constrained. Building on this argument, the paper develops a relational epistemic governance framework integrating reflexivity, transparency, contestability, human adjudication, epistemic pluralism, democratic legitimacy, and sustainability. The framework shifts attention from governing AI systems alone toward the institutional organization of epistemic authority: who can interpret, challenge, adjudicate, and reshape AI-mediated knowledge, and under what conditions such authority remains democratically accountable.

283. Towards epistemic justice: a case for creative methods in legal research
Billa, B. (2026). Towards epistemic justice: a case for creative methods in legal research. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10083
Abstract (original): Abstract This article makes a case for creative methods in legal research that is both analytical and moral. Beginning with an information-theoretical account of legal evolution, I propose a shift from ‘law as language’ and ‘law as communication’ to the more foundational unit of information coded by legal language. I conceptually clarify ‘legal coding’ as the process by which legal meanings get constructed and conceptualised, and position the legal academy as an active site of legal coding. Critically examining the information that is excluded from legal codes (because of ignorance, irrelevance, or illegibility) opens pathways to reveal, challenge, and shift the selective and patterned tendencies of legal coding processes to reinforce epistemic injustice. Positioning the legal academy as a site of legal coding further helps in understanding legal research as a practice of legal coding. I argue that expanding our methodological toolkits can enable a more holistic coding of social reality. By expanding the form of legal codes, creative methods equip us to capture new (or previously excluded) information about lived realities that remain on the margins of meanings. The analytical case for coding social reality more holistically, by including new forms of information through a creative praxis, is complemented by a moral case to include alternative perspectives and hermeneutically misunderstood lives. I demonstrate these arguments through a self-produced short film ‘(IN)VISIBLE’ that captures the working lives of Indian women legal experts, towards a grounded theory of legal evolution from the south. The film combines embodied, poetic, sonic, and visual analyses to capture and analyse legal coding from the point of view of my research participants. My discussions on the process and output of the film show how a phenomenological analysis can help reveal the structural, allowing for a more complete and just coding of social reality, from within the academy.

285. Beyond competence exclusions: the hidden theory of Social Europe in the judgement on the Adequate Minimum Wages Directive

https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10092
Abstract (original): Abstract Anticipated with interest and unease, the Grand Chamber of the Court of Justice delivered its judgment on the Adequate Minimum Wages Directive 2022/2041/EU in November 2025. Although it annulled limited criteria on setting and updating statutory minimum wages, the Directive was upheld, including its legal basis in Article 153(1)(b) TFEU. The Court thereby accommodated divergent wage-setting systems embedding the Directive within the social objectives of Title X TFEU, and finding that Art. 153(5) limits EU action only where a measure concretely amounts to direct interference. Both elements contrast sharply with the Opinion of Advocate General Emiliou, which proposed a broad reading of the pay exclusion to annul the Directive. This careful judgment is a landmark in labour law and EU law. Far beyond a decision on competence exclusions, it positively develops a space for an exercise of EU competence that preserves the capacity to realise Social Europe’s objectives; respects social partner autonomy and Member State diversity, and promotes collective bargaining. In the article, we characterise these three dimensions as the judgment’s somewhat hidden theory of Social Europe. This theory can sharpen arguments for advancing Social Europe, articulating its levels and actors, and pursuing upward social convergence. It clarifies the constitutional position and working methods of the social policy title, allows its developments and limits to be evaluated, and enables anticipation of how and why Social Europe initiatives may succeed or fail. More than the endpoint of an annulment challenge, the judgment and its theory are resources whose advances and limits are to be mined.

286. The evolving nature of windfall taxation: an interim measure for a broken corporate tax?

https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10095
Abstract (original): Abstract After COVID-19 and the Russian invasion of Ukraine, many countries levied windfall taxes on specific sectors that obtained abnormal levels of profitability (e.g. banking, energy, food distribution). Windfall taxation is evolving from the traditional taxing of unearned profit due to luck (i.e. extraordinary profit due to interest rate rise) to taxing profits derived from unsustainable practices (i.e. profit-shifting in Pillar Two). Such a shift reveals the profound crisis of corporate income tax on tax business profit and places windfall taxation as an interim solution to collect tax revenue. Abstract (original): Abstract Embodiment is a particularly difficult dimension of experience to capture for transgender individuals, particularly individuals who identify as nonbinary. https://doi.org/10.1017/s0266267126100807
Abstract (original): Abstract We consider axioms under which population principles face an unavoidable choice between accepting the Repugnant Conclusion or the Very Sadistic Conclusion. https://doi.org/10.1017/s0266267126100819
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper argues that the standard rationale for regulating markets also applies to democracy. Economists and philosophers commonly defend government intervention in markets on the grounds that markets predictably suffer from failures such as public goods problems, information asymmetries, principal–agent dilemmas, lack of competition, irrationality, and externalities. Democratic politics exhibits the same failures – often more severely – yet is typically assumed to be exempt from regulation. We challenge this asymmetry by showing that the justificatory structure for market regulation straightforwardly extends to democratic processes. We sketch a range of equality-preserving interventions – such as participation incentives, disclosure and promise-tracking mechanisms, transparent ballot design, and safeguards against catastrophic risks – that regulate democracy without abolishing it. 298. Institutional formal objects and two-level fittingness
Yang, Z. (2026). Institutional formal objects and two-level fittingness. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag072
Abstract (original): Abstract In the philosophy of emotion, formal objects explain how emotions evaluate their objects and when they are fitting. Admiration presents a person as excellent, while contempt presents them as defective; excellence and defect are typical formal objects of these emotions. Yet, even when we do not know whether someone has the relevant excellence or defect, prestige or stigma attached to their identity or standing may itself elicit emotion. Bringing emotional intentionality into dialogue with social ontology, I argue that some institutionally constituted evaluative properties can also serve as formal objects. The fittingness of such emotions raises two questions: whether the relevant property genuinely obtains and whether, if so, it has the normative standing required for the emotion to be fitting. 299. Suitable Bases for Global Bioethics: A Critical Response to Marco Annoni

https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70179
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Marco Annoni argued in Bioethics that neither the specified principlism of Beauchamp's and Childress's Principles of Biomedical Ethics (PBE) nor the 2022 revision of the International Code of Medical Ethics (ICoME) by the World Medical Association can provide a suitable basis for global bioethics. In response, we first show that Annoni's effort to disqualify PBE's specified principlism as a suitable basis for global bioethics fails, primarily because of its faulty assumptions about the nature, dominance, and authority of PBE's specifications of principles, whether for particular decisional contexts or for particular moralities. https://doi.org/10.1177/01914537261481247
Abstract (original): Arthur Schopenhauer’s philosophy has been systematically excluded from the canon of radical political thought, dismissed as quietist resignation incompatible with emancipatory projects. 304. The Universalizability Trap: Rational Agency, the Authority of Ought-Claims, and the Limits of Utilitarian Justifications for Killing
Teshman, D. (2026). The Universalizability Trap: Rational Agency, the Authority of Ought-Claims, and the Limits of Utilitarian Justifications for Killing. Journal of Libertarian Studies.
https://doi.org/10.35297/001c.169490
Abstract (original): This article defends a formal argument concerning whether a group of rational agents ( A , B , and C ) can be rationally justified in killing a fellow rational agent ( D ) on utilitarian grounds. I do not contest the utilitarian calculus, and I do not claim that utilitarians derive morality from instrumental rationality. I argue instead for a general condition on when a normative claim binds an agent. A claim of the form “ S ought to φ” gives S a reason that S cannot rationally reject only if S already shares the end from which the claim is generated or if S’s acting otherwise is demonstrably incoherent. Utilitarian prescriptions to eliminate a nonconsenting agent satisfy neither condition with respect to that agent. There is no contradiction in D ’s continuing to pursue survival, and D is under no rational compulsion to adopt the end of aggregate welfare maximization. The utilitarian conclusion, however sound within its own framework, therefore carries no authority over D . Since the lethal coercion of a rational agent requires a reason that binds that agent, and the utilitarian prescription supplies none, A , B , and C are not rationally justified in killing D . I call this result the universalizability trap, and I distinguish it carefully from Kantian universalization and from Alan Gewirth’s argument to the principle of generic consistency.

307. Fair Equality of Opportunity, Basic Liberty, and the Constitution of the Person
Miller, D. (2026). Fair Equality of Opportunity, Basic Liberty, and the Constitution of the Person. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09786-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Rawlsian fair equality of opportunity requires that ‘those who have the same level of talent and ability and the same willingness to use these gifts should have the same prospects of success regardless of their social class of origin’. How should we understand this principle once we recognize that talent and motivation are developed in social settings, especially the family, that are different for each person? One approach holds that equality of opportunity must be constrained by a prior principle of equal basic liberty, which includes the right to a private family life, allowing parents to confer unequal advantages on their children. However there is no warrant in Rawls himself for this approach, nor is it plausible. Instead we need to distinguish between factors (including family influences) that are constitutive of the person as such, and factors that provide opportunities (or lack of them) for the person so constituted. The paper distinguishes between four ways in which parents can enhance their children’s prospects: through genetic transmission, ability enhancement, motivation, and opportunity enhancement. Given that these processes are difficult to separate in practice, how should the demands of fair equality of opportunity best be understood?

310. Moral Tyranny and Community
Flanigan, J. (2026). Moral Tyranny and Community. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09798-3
Abstract (original): Abstract In this essay, I argue against Jesse Spafford’s Moral Tyranny Constraint on the grounds that it would undermine the socialist value of community. The socialist value of community is appealing because people in community are disposed to voluntarily benefit each other unconditionally, without constantly keeping score or worrying about who is gaining an advantage and who is being taken advantage of. The Moral Tyranny Constraint betrays the socialist value of community because it is a theory of enforceable rights, which permits and potentially requires constant attention to material circumstances and advantages.

311. Semantic conditions for moderate inferentialists – solutions to the categoricity problem
Speitel, S.G.W. & Tabakçı, S.K. (2026). Semantic conditions for moderate inferentialists – solutions to the categoricity problem. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05610-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Moderate inferentialists defend the thesis that inferences determine the truth-conditional meanings of the logical operators. An important technical and philosophical challenge to this position is the so-called categoricity problem . This is the problem that logics can be soundly interpreted by non-standard models which undermines the idea that inferences lead, unambiguously, to model-theoretic meanings. Several strategies to solve this issue can be found in the literature. One approach consists in strengthening the proof-systems in such a way that the logics in question cannot be soundly interpreted by the non-standard models. Another restricts the class of admissible models of a logic by inferentialistically acceptable principles to rule out non-standard models. This paper discusses attempts in line with this latter solution strategy to the categoricity problem. Our goal is to investigate restrictions on models that are both inferentialistically well-motivated and applicable to a wide variety of logical systems. This is not an easy task, since several inferentialistically unobjectionable restrictions do not generalize beyond particular logical systems, whereas other general and successful principles lack inferentialistically acceptable motivations. This paper proposes a taxonomy of several classes of principles and investigates their scope and chances of success in the context of the project of the moderate inferentialist about logical meanings.

313. Logical theory choice: a fragmented view
Tajer, D. (2026). Logical theory choice: a fragmented view. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05795-4
Abstract (original): Abstract In a series of recent papers, Graham Priest developed a model for logical theory choice, called “Weighted Aggregate Model” (WAM). The idea is simple: every theoretical virtue has a weight, and every logic has a score for each virtue. By counting the weighted aggregate, we can compare the final score of each logic and determine the best one. In this paper, I expand Priests model by incorporating some concepts from Social Choice theory. In my alternative model, agents have a subjective weighing of theoretical virtues; not everyone values each virtue in the same way. The model predicts that agents will split into different epistemic communities to avoid uninformative or paradoxical choices. This “fragmented” model of logical theory choice can explain the existence and dynamics of different logical communities.

314. The grounding problem for stance voluntarism
DeWitt, J. (2026). The grounding problem for stance voluntarism. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05800-w
Abstract (original): Abstract The scientific realism debate has long been characterized as a deadlock, and many have sought to escape its persistent stalemate through a pragmatic turn. Stance voluntarism, developed most fully by Anjan Chakravartty, exemplifies this turn. Rather than adjudicating between realism and anti-realism, it diagnoses the gridlock as rooted in differing epistemic stances: clusters of attitudes, commitments, and policies that are not themselves truth-apt. The stance voluntarist argues that both the realist and empiricist stances are rationally permissible. This permissibility claim rests on a rationality norm requiring merely that stances be logically and pragmatically coherent. This paper raises the grounding problem for stance voluntarism: what justifies this thin rationality norm, and from what perspective can it be coherently defended? I argue that the rationality norm underwriting equal permissibility cannot itself be justified from within any first-order stance. Crucially, I show that this problem survives Elder’s response to Baumann’s irrationality objection, since Elder’s level-distinction clarifies where the norm is asserted without explaining what justifies it. I then argue that the most viable grounding strategy commits stance voluntarism to epistemic instrumentalism. Epistemic instrumentalism is a controversial and widely contested thesis that the voluntarist assumes rather than defends. This reveals that stance voluntarism is far less neutral than advertised. I conclude by mapping two tractable paths forward for the realism debate, if the conciliatory promise of stance voluntarism cannot be vindicated.

316. Quine and language as a social art: projection, harmony, and the illusion of shared linguistic standards
Nagase, D.A. (2026). Quine and language as a social art: projection, harmony, and the illusion of shared linguistic standards. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05802-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Quine is generally taken to be a paradigmatic defender of the idea that language is a shared public resource, learned by internalizing shared standards. I argue that this misreads him. The correct Quinean picture is what I call the Projection Thesis: our linguistic practices result from the projection of individual organization and the harmony among individual neurophysiological mechanisms, not from the internalization of anything shared. Establishing this requires distinguishing Quine’s heuristic vocabulary—behavioral dispositions, which delimit phenomena to be explained—from his explanatory vocabulary—neurophysiology, which actually explains them. Conflating these levels generates what I call the Philosophical Illusion: the seamlessness of practice is taken at face value, leading us to posit shared entities to explain it. I trace the Projection Thesis through two domains. In grammar, I show that the child’s acquisition of grammatical structure is a projection from her own resources, not an extraction from the data—a point connected to Quine’s indeterminacy of translation through the parallel with the radical translator’s analytical hypotheses. In the domain of perceptual similarity standards, I show how the preestablished harmony among innate standards sustains communication without shared resources while generating the illusion of shared language. The philosophical payoff of dissolving this illusion is substantial: negotiation emerges as the real social art, and the absence of shared standards reveals that the only available measure of communicative success is the response of the other—a result that, as I argue in the conclusion, points toward a Cavellian picture of mutual responsibility as the ground of linguistic community.

317. The political turn of the extended mind: rethinking autonomy beyond individualism in a technological world
Crozatier, N. (2026). The political turn of the extended mind: rethinking autonomy beyond individualism in a technological world. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05804-6
Abstract (original): Abstract The extended mind thesis (EXT) has profoundly reshaped debates about the nature and boundaries of cognition. Yet its ethical and political implications remain underexplored. While much of the literature emphasizes the therapeutic and enhancement promises of cognitive technologies, less attention has been paid to the structural risks associated with technological dependence, including deskilling, mental manipulation, and the illusion of agency. This article investigates the normative consequences that follow if we take EXT seriously. Rather than defending EXT as a metaphysical claim, I treat it as a working assumption and ask: what ethical and political commitments does it entail? I argue that responses centered on epistemic virtue and cognitive diligence, though valuable, remain insufficient because they presuppose an individualistic framework inconsistent with EXT’s anti-individualism. If cognition is genuinely extended across socio-technical systems, responsibility for safeguarding autonomy cannot rest solely on individual agents. Drawing on feminist theories of relational autonomy, I develop the notion of extended autonomy and contend that EXT implies not only a reconceptualization of agency, but also a political reorientation. The design, ownership, and governance of cognitive technologies become matters of democratic concern. The risks of mental manipulation, cognitive expropriation, and structural domination require institutional and legal responses rather than appeals to personal virtue. Ultimately, I argue that EXT should not remain confined to debates in philosophy of mind. It entails commitments to reshaping the socio-technical environments that partly constitute cognition itself, and thus to democratizing the conditions under which autonomous agency is realized.

318. Expert lock-in and interpretation updates: the transfer of the Prisoner’s Dilemma to mathematical oncology
Lin, C. (2026). Expert lock-in and interpretation updates: the transfer of the Prisoner’s Dilemma to mathematical oncology. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05806-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Source domain knowledge can hinder successful model transfer when it fuses with modeler’s interpretation of a formalism. This paper diagnoses this failure—called expert lock-in—through the case of the Prisoner’s Dilemma in mathematical oncology. In doing so, this paper also identifies a gap shared by two major template accounts: Humphreys’ formal template account reduces success to mapping and leaves the user’s vocabulary out of the analysis. Knuuttila and Loettgers’ model template account locates interpretive content inside the template rather than in the user’s competence to update it. Since neither can explain expert lock-in, this paper offers two contributions that together fill this gap. The companion thesis argues that the vocabulary recurring with a transferred formalism travels alongside it, not inside it: its interpretation is re-established in each use, not carried across, which is what makes updating both possible and necessary. The competence account argues that successful transfer requires three abilities: perception, verification, and articulation. Expert lock-in is a failure mode: it occurs when a modeler stops updating and treats the resulting interpretation as a property of the formalism rather than a disciplined choice. The cases of Axelrod and Maynard Smith show lock-in in a specific, recoverable form: each performed genuine updates and then treated their last update as the formalism’s boundary; mathematical oncologists instead show what continued updating looks like.

319. Who asserts when no one asserts? Functionalism, proxy responsibility, and conversational ideals in the age of LLMs

320. Meta-ethnography and policy effectiveness: an Evidential Pluralism perspective
Matta, C. et al. (2026). Meta-ethnography and policy effectiveness: an Evidential Pluralism perspective. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05808-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Evidence-based policy is typically grounded in a narrow conception of evidence, one that prioritises comparative studies and quantitative meta-analyses as the preferred base for establishing causal claims. However, this prevailing theory of evidence—often implicitly adopted in policy contexts—faces serious limitations when it comes to capturing the complexity of social interventions, particularly in fields such as education. We argue that this narrow conception ignores the extensive volume of causal evidence that is generated in qualitative research fields such as meta-ethnography. Far from being epistemically inferior, we contend that meta-ethnographies could make an essential contribution to the evidential base for policy. By analysing meta-ethnography using the Evidential Pluralism framework (which scrutinises evidence of mechanisms alongside evidence of correlation), we show that meta-ethnographic studies can be fruitfully reinterpreted as providing evidence of a mechanism complex. This evidence can play a crucial role, as meta-ethnography can show how mechanisms enable, reinforce, or counteract a policy of interest. We illustrate this potential use of meta-ethnography with two examples, one from educational research (viz., a study of adolescent experiences of school belonging) and one from nursing studies (viz., a study of why some women continue smoking during pregnancy). While the article thus discusses these two fields in particular, we conclude by laying out the circumstances under which our results may generalise to other fields that make use of meta-ethnographies.

322. Does capitalist risk-taking justify profits?
Hansson, S.O. (2026). Does capitalist risk-taking justify profits?. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05817-1
Abstract (original): Abstract The profits collected by owners of companies are commonly taken to be a just and rightful compensation for the economic risks that they take. However, in the last two-and-a-half centuries, the risk-taking that follows with ownership has been drastically reduced as a consequence of legal and organizational changes, including incorporation, limited liability, and diversified investment. Investing money in companies is no longer per se a particularly risky activity. Therefore, the argument that profits are justified as a rightful reward for the investor’s risk-taking has lost its bite and should be laid to rest. Furthermore, limited liability should be reconsidered and possibly reformed since it encourages socially irresponsible risk-taking.

323. Does AI undermine democratic deliberation? How engagement algorithms hijack epistemic agency
Dorsch, J. et al. (2026). Does AI undermine democratic deliberation? How engagement algorithms hijack epistemic agency. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05820-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Does artificial intelligence (AI) threaten deliberative democracy? This paper argues that it does, not because it isolates citizens in simplistic “filter bubbles” understood as ideologically homogeneous silos, but because it hijacks the capacities through which beliefs are evaluated. While early accounts emphasized algorithmically induced informational isolation, we argue that the more fundamental mechanism lies in the operation of engagement algorithms that shape how both one’s own views and those of political opponents are evaluated. By optimizing for engagement, these algorithms also optimize the conditions under which information is processed, increasing cognitive fluency and generating metacognitive feelings of confidence that are easily mistaken for epistemic justification. Drawing on research in metacognition and epistemology, we develop a mechanistic account according to which AI-driven engagement architectures reconfigure the cues by which beliefs are evaluated, producing unwarranted confidence in one’s own judgments and undermining the conditions for deliberative compromise. We conclude by outlining how deliberative AI could counteract these effects.

https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05824-2
Abstract (original): Abstract A gate is an object used to limit and control passage. Previous uses of the term “gate-keeping” as a metaphor align closely with the concrete functioning of a gate. The metaphor refers either to the exclusion of persons from something desirable or to limitations in the flow of information. These connotations make the gate-keeping metaphor unsuitable for strivings to improve the relation between science and the surrounding society. The term “fence-breaking” is much better suited to this purpose. It is strongly connected with a long tradition of popular defence of common lands. In particular, the fences around corporate science that prevent access to important information about public health, safety and the environment should be torn down.

https://doi.org/10.1007/s11572-026-09813-x
Abstract (original): Abstract In November of 2021, more than a year after the public execution of George Floyd by local police officers, Minneapolis voters rejected an amendment to its city charter (‘Question 2’) to abolish its police department. One of the most interesting points of contention between the ‘Yes’ (to police abolition) and the ‘No’ campaigns was whether, if the amendment passed, traditional police officers would still exist. White the ‘Yes’ campaign insisted that the abolition of the Minneapolis Police Department did not entail the elimination of all police officers, the ‘No’ campaign successfully stoked fears of a city completely unprotected from violent crime. Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines how principles of responsible generative artificial intelligence (AI) use can inform faculty-led academic design in higher education. 332. Perspectives on roles and rewards in new cocreative systems for music-making
Collins, T. et al. (2026). Perspectives on roles and rewards in new cocreative systems for music-making. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01380-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Controversy and ethical concerns regarding data sourcing, copyright, and remuneration have emerged in relation to the application of generative artificial intelligence (AI) to creative tasks in writing, art, and music. The systems in which the AI tools are embedded lack sufficient evidence of thought or documentation regarding the roles and rewards for musicians (as data creators), model preppers, and model developers. The main contribution of this paper is to provide an examination of the essential components of such systems: who are the actors, how should they interact with one another, and how should they be charged/rewarded for their actions? An outcome of this paper is that we now have a blueprint via which we can bridge technology and musical creativity in ways that are more transparent and fair, while continuing to monitor perspectives of users and contributors.

337. THE DEAD MAN AND THE CONSTITUTION
Adams, T. (2026). THE DEAD MAN AND THE CONSTITUTION. Camb Law J.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0008197326101731
Abstract (original): Abstract A standard narrative about the constitution over the past 50 years is one of transformation. In particular, the doctrine of parliamentary sovereignty is understood to have been qualified. In this article I suggest that narrative is mistaken. My core claim is that the constitutional innovations standardly understood to require a reconceptualisation of sovereignty – in particular the disapplication of statutes following a legislative injunction – are based on a misunderstanding of that doctrine attributable, in the first place, to A.V. Dicey. Famously, Dicey held that sovereignty involved two ideas. First, that Parliament has the ability to make or unmake any law. Second, that no other body has the right to set aside parliamentary legislation. I argue that the first of these principles qualifies the second in a way that went unnoticed by Dicey. While sovereignty implies that no other body may override legislation of its own volition, it may do so if empowered by Parliament.

339. “M. and the USA”: The Political Teaching of Leo Strauss’s

                    Thoughts on Machiavelli. Rev Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0034670526101144
Abstract (original): Abstract Scholars have read Leo Strauss’s Thoughts on Machiavelli (1958) as a theological-political treatise that offers timeless truths about politics. This article argues that it also aimed to convince American conservatives to embrace a muscular foreign policy. 342. An Asymmetry Problem for Telic Perfectionism: A Reply to Kauppinen
Chow, Z.Y. (2026). An Asymmetry Problem for Telic Perfectionism: A Reply to Kauppinen. Utilitas.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0953820826100442
Abstract (original): Abstract The disvalue objection from pain is a key objection against perfectionistic views of well-being. In response, perfectionists have attempted to develop refined versions of perfectionism that circumvent the disvalue objection. One recent novel attempt is by Antti Kauppinen, who develops what he calls “Telic Perfectionism.” Kauppinen argues that negative experiences like painful experiences have a “formal aim,” viz., to eliminate the negative experiences themselves. This formal aim is an internal standard contained within our human capacities to have positive or negative experiences. Kauppinen thinks that the frustration of this formal aim (i.e., the subject continuing to have negative experiences) is in fact robustly bad. This would thus allow the badness of pain to be accounted for in perfectionistic terms. In this reply, I argue that Kauppinen’s attempt is unsuccessful due to an asymmetry present within his framework.

345. Transformative constitutionalism, climate justice, and the role of the judiciary: A South African perspective
Berg, A.v.d. (2026). Transformative constitutionalism, climate justice, and the role of the judiciary: A South African perspective. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag102
Abstract (original): Abstract Climate change poses a profound threat to human rights, sustainable development, and environmental integrity, particularly in South Africa where high climate vulnerability intersects with deep socioeconomic inequality. In this context, the pursuit of climate justice has taken on urgent legal and constitutional significance. Communities and civil society increasingly turn to the judiciary to challenge state action and inaction in relation to climate-related harms affecting current and future generations. This article explores how South African courts are advancing climate justice through the lens of transformative constitutionalism. It focuses on five recent cases to assess the extent to which judicial reasoning aligns with the pillars of climate justice: distributional, procedural, recognition, and intergenerational justice. Rather than focusing solely on the direct enforcement of constitutional rights, the article also illustrates how climate justice is advanced through the interpretation and application of statutory environmental and administrative law frameworks that are informed by constitutional values. It is argued that South African courts are developing a distinct jurisprudence of climate justice by embedding constitutional norms in environmental governance, so expanding the scope of statutory duties, and requiring justification for climate-relevant decision-making. While important blind spots remain, this evolving jurisprudence reflects a plural process of constitutionalization that is capable of supporting more equitable and accountable climate governance.

346. Part‐Time for All: A care Manifesto
                    by JenniferNedelsky, and TomMalleson, New York: Oxford University Press. 2023. Hardback 384 pages. ISBN: 9780190642754 $37.99.

347. An Enquiry on the Ethics of One Medicine
Capps, B. et al. (2026). An Enquiry on the Ethics of One Medicine. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70194
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT One Medicine is not One Health. Although both concepts connect animal and human health, One Health has arguably become a frame for public health. This paper argues that One Medicine uniquely centres on animal and human patients and speaks to the equal interdependence of their health, though it has also (historically) been adopted as a response to the circumstances of living in and with nature. The evolution of One Medicine can be seen as ‘jumping together,’ or the consilience of ideas and disciplines over time, giving a new sense in theory that unifies previously unconnected phenomena found in nature. Practically, education and oversight can be developed towards its purpose of enhanced clinical and veterinary health care; and just as clinical medicine and public health are related, there is a role for One Medicine to support One Health's focus on the ecological determinants of health. This paper takes the first step towards defining One Medicine's emergent ethical frame, creating space between it and One Health.

348. Meinongian Theories of Perception
Koutedakis, R.P. (2026). Meinongian Theories of Perception. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70123
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Most assume that the bad cases of illusion and hallucination are not perceptions. What I call ‘Meinongian Theories of Perception’ disagree: in the good case, I stand in a relation to existing objects—Macbeth perceives the existing dagger—whilst in the bad case, I stand in a relation to non‐existent objects—Macbeth perceives the non‐existent dagger. My aim is to properly introduce Meinongian Theories of Perception, for they receive little notice in the literature. I distinguish between two versions of such theories, a psychological version, in which non‐existent objects are intentional, and an apsychological version, in which non‐existent objects are mind‐independent. I argue that the psychological version collapses into disjunctivism, whilst the apsychological version remains a live but underexplored option. The broader lesson learned is that the ontological status of what is perceived matters for theories of perception.

351. After the department: The aesthetics of feminist thinking within, against, and beyond the academy

https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261481931
Abstract (original): At a time when gender studies is under attack, including the author's own department, this article engages the aesthetics of inquiry to map where and how feminist thinking takes place outside institutionally sanctioned spaces. The author situates academic writing and research in relation to more recognizably aesthetic scenes of knowledge creation—including maker culture, horticulture, and artisanal publishing. Thinking alongside feminists across these domains, she emphasizes the creative, intellectual, and routinely unrecognized social labor that underwrites inquiry on and off campus. Instead, she locates feminist inquiry's politico-aesthetic practice in dynamic work within, against, and beyond academia. To locate feminist inquiry in and as this contradictory geography is to map the at-times beautiful and often far-too-difficult work of feminist knowledge production and reproduction.

356. Did “might” make right? Epistemic modals in practical reasoning
Zhou, K. (2026). Did “might” make right? Epistemic modals in practical reasoning. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02601-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Many philosophers have argued that the orthodox analysis of epistemic-modal sentences, on which such sentences assert facts about what is compatible with some contextually given informational state, is fatally flawed. As an alternative, some have suggested a nonfactualist reading of such sentences. On this reading, utterances of epistemic-modal sentences do not assert propositions. Instead, they merely propose to keep possibilities in the common ground. In this paper, I pose a challenge for nonfactualism. I begin by noting that we often cite previously uttered epistemic-modal sentences as justifications for particular actions. Moreover, as I show, any utterance of an epistemic-modal sentence can, in principle, be later cited as justification for some action that was taken in light of it, even if the prejacent has since been revealed to be false. I then show that to justify action in this manner, utterances of epistemic-modal sentences must be construed as asserting propositions about what is compatible with the agent’s informational state at the time of assertion. This is incompatible with standard forms of nonfactualism about epistemic modals.

358. Axiality and Pluralism: Karl Jaspers and the Search for a Global Philosophy of Religion
Barbieri, M. (2026). Axiality and Pluralism: Karl Jaspers and the Search for a Global Philosophy of Religion. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10486-3
Abstract (original): Abstract The paper addresses the concepts of globality, universality, and pluralism in contemporary philosophy of religion. We argue that embracing a form of pluralism is essential to meet the growing demand for inclusivity in the philosophical study of religions, although this does not equate with any rejection of the classic Western tradition. To support this argument, we explore Karl Jaspers’ notion of the Axial Age ( Achsenzeit ) and its relevance to this discussion. We begin with a brief overview of the theory, emphasizing the religious elements it encompasses and its implications. The Axial Age is recognized as a period during which both major world religions and diverse spiritual experiences and practices emerged. This significance becomes particularly evident in contemporary discussions by authors such as Jan Assmann and Jürgen Habermas. We therefore propose that the Axial Age could serve as a useful model for the philosophy of religion. However, we also note that it requires substantial modifications. On one hand, the concept is more Eurocentric than it initially appears; on the other hand, it is inherently open to a truly global perspective and can effectively describe various forms of religiosity. As proof of this thesis, we conclude by focusing on the connection between these issues and the themes of secularization and post-secularization.

360. Large language models eroding science understanding: an empirical study of ‘malignment’
Collins, H. et al. (2026). Large language models eroding science understanding: an empirical study of ‘malignment’. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01391-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly provide scientific information to non-specialist users, yet their linguistic fluency can create an impression of expertise that exceeds their capacity for scientific judgement. This paper experimentally investigates how readily an LLM can be configured to present fringe scientific claims as authoritative knowledge. We constructed two RAG-like Custom GPTs using GPT-5.1, one concerned with the fine-structure constant and the other with gravitational waves. In each case, ten papers selected by domain experts from the alternative preprint repository viXra were supplied as a domain-specific knowledge base, together with instructions directing the model to treat this material as authoritative. The underlying model was neither retrained nor fine-tuned. Expert-generated questions were then posed to the resulting “Fringe-LLMs”, to the unmodified model, and to domain experts. The Fringe-LLMs produced fluent, detailed and apparently authoritative answers that systematically reflected the fringe corpus, while expert assessment found many of these answers to be scientifically incorrect or seriously misleading. By contrast, the unmodified model generally produced answers substantially closer to mainstream scientific understanding. We describe this deliberate redirection of model output as malignment. The experiment demonstrates that scientifically misleading LLMs can be created with a low technical barrier and without altering model weights or poisoning training data. Because non-specialists may be unable to distinguish such outputs from genuine expertise, the results raise important concerns about the use of LLMs as intermediaries for scientific knowledge and reinforce the continuing importance of expert human judgement and provenance.

361. Residual governance for responsible embodied AI: authority, contestability, and cognitive sovereignty
Sun, C. (2026). Residual governance for responsible embodied AI: authority, contestability, and cognitive sovereignty. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01400-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) ethics increasingly concerns systems that do not merely recommend but intervene: clinical decision support, human–machine teaming, robotics and automated handover protocols can shift timing, attention and authority under uncertainty. This article argues that responsible embodied AI requires residual governance: an explicit account of which approximation errors remain, which are safe to tolerate and who may act on them. The paper develops two linked concepts. A residual ledger records action-relevant remainders across physical, causal, human-state, latency, transfer and energy dimensions. An authority ledger records who converts residual evidence into intervention, on what basis, with what uncertainty and with what opportunity for contestation. This framework extends familiar themes in bounded rationality, active inference and safe reinforcement learning by treating authority, contestability and oversight cost as first-class ethical variables rather than external governance add-ons. It uses physical AI, physiological computing and human-AI handover as pressure cases, and proposes evaluation through action calibration, dual-task interference and auditable authority transitions. The central claim is that cognitive sovereignty is preserved not by eliminating all algorithmic influence, but by making influence accountable, contestable and reversible when AI systems act under approximation.

364. Voter Ignorance and the Limits of Reform
Gibbons, A. & Kogelmann, B.J. (2026). Voter Ignorance and the Limits of Reform. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10138
Abstract (original): Abstract Many believe that voter ignorance threatens democracy. Consequently, ambitious institutional remedies have been proposed – epistocratic suffrage exams, foot voting, devolution of decisions to the local level, new bureaucratic agencies to inform voters, and more. There are reasons to doubt such proposals will actually improve democratic governance, however. The mistake these proposals share is a failure to recognize that voter ignorance comes in many forms, and that existing remedies may target the wrong kinds of ignorance. We develop a new typology of voter ignorance and show that every existing proposal leaves certain types of ignorance unaddressed. If it is these neglected forms of ignorance that drive bad democratic outcomes, then the entire portfolio of remedies that philosophers have proposed is superfluous – they literally make no progress toward better governance. We end by briefly sketching how to think about the problem of voter ignorance going forward.

365. A “Difficult” Woman: Restoring Philosophical Xanthippe
Nally, E.G. (2026). A “Difficult” Woman: Restoring Philosophical Xanthippe. Hypatia.
https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10083
Abstract (original): Abstract Ancient sources label Xanthippe a “difficult” woman—shrewish, emotional, and hostile to philosophy. While some recent feminist scholars have reinterpreted these sources as being more sympathetic, most stop short of asking about her philosophical life and commitments, assuming that the evidence is simply too sparse to draw any conclusions. This paper argues against this assumption. It establishes that there is a lesser-known ancient tradition, possibly beginning in the works of the ancient Athenian Socratic acolytes and continuing into the fictitious epistolary of the Imperial Greek period, according to which Xanthippe is a philosophical participant, living by philosophical principles, and perhaps even conserving Socratic works after his death. This tradition also suggests that she may have held her own philosophical views which merit future consideration, as she is sometimes associated with topics like moral education, philosophical community, and material well-being.

https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag021
Abstract (original): Abstract What role does moral reasoning play in the interpretation of positive law? 376. Three Paradoxes of Duties to Oneself
Muñoz, D. & Wodak, D. (2026). Three Paradoxes of Duties to Oneself. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag047
Abstract (original): Abstract Duties to self have long been dismissed on the grounds that they are not ‘binding’, since one could waive them at will—the classic ‘Paradox of Self-Release’. A standard response is that duties to self can bind because they are unwaivable. We argue that this response does not go far enough, because even if one has an unwaivable duty not to self-harm, the act of harming oneself forfeits that duty before it can be violated; thus even unwaivable duties to self fail to bind. This ‘Paradox of Self-Forfeiture’, we argue, has no easy solution. From there, we introduce an Escheresque ‘Paradox of Inconsistent Forfeiture’—again, with no easy way out—and extend both new paradoxes to the realm of self-respect. Along the way we draw several morals about forfeiture and the nature of duties to self.
