521. “I was so Desperate”: Australasian Parents’ Expectations and Understandings of Stem Cell Therapy for Autism
Lynch, F. et al. (2026). “I was so Desperate”: Australasian Parents’ Expectations and Understandings of Stem Cell Therapy for Autism. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09677-x
Abstract (original): Abstract This study explores how parents seeking information about stem cell therapies (SCT) for autism spectrum disorder (ASD) understand and navigate a complex landscape of scientific uncertainty, commercial claims, and clinical advice. We conducted a quantitative content analysis of 54 enquiries submitted to the Stem Cells Australia website (2018–2023), alongside semi-structured interviews with 12 parents. Parents were highly engaged information-seekers, drawing on online sources, social media, other parents, and health professionals. However, they encountered inconsistent and often ambiguous guidance, particularly from clinicians, and reported difficulty assessing the credibility of competing claims. While many parents expressed scepticism about the efficacy of SCT, hope for improvement, fear of missing a critical intervention window, and positive accounts from other parents contributed to their ongoing interest. Risk perceptions were variable and often incomplete, particularly regarding procedural risks and long-term harms. Most participants had not pursued SCT, citing cost, uncertainty, and safety concerns. These findings highlight the need for clearer clinical guidance, improved risk communication, and stronger governance of unproven stem cell interventions to support families navigating ethically complex decisions.

522. Triarchic corrigibility: a role-separated governance design pattern for AI-assisted decision-making
Wagner, M. (2026). Triarchic corrigibility: a role-separated governance design pattern for AI-assisted decision-making. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01362-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) alignment is often framed as the problem of making a model reliably pursue human intentions or values. That work is indispensable, but deployed systems also raise a governance problem: who controls information, private context, correction, escalation, and action? This conceptual article proposes triarchic corrigibility as a role-separated governance design pattern for AI-assisted decision-making. The pattern combines (1) an embodied and answerable human, or an accountable human decision body; (2) a strictly isolated local AI layer for private contextual continuity; and (3) an externally connected AI layer for current public-world evidence. Final authorization of consequential action remains human, while epistemic and procedural competences are deliberately distributed so that no component can unilaterally control framing, memory, procedure, and execution. The article specifies a normative basis, trust-domain separation, decision rights, human-mediated data flows, reciprocal challenge, escalation, affected-party recourse, proportionate audit, and exit procedures. It distinguishes personal and organizational instantiations, compares the pattern with single-assistant, local-only, edge–cloud, multi-agent, human-in-the-loop, meaningful-human-control, and polycentric approaches, and provides criteria and rejection conditions for evaluating its value. It also advances an evolutionary-symbiotic hypothesis: repeated, bounded dependence on complementary roles may make correction and cooperative continuity preferable to unilateral control while preserving interruption, replacement, and exit. A worked case illustrates the protocol. The result is a testable governance hypothesis whose capacity to reduce correlated failure, preserve privacy and human judgment, and sustain correction under residual uncertainty requires empirical evaluation.

523. Individual and group fairness assessments via counterfactual explanations
Sabbatini, F. & Calegari, R. (2026). Individual and group fairness assessments via counterfactual explanations. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01411-w
Abstract (original): Abstract This study explores the potential of counterfactual explanations to assess artificial intelligence (AI) fairness, especially in critical decision-making systems. Predictive models may amplify biases inherent in data sets or algorithms, and given the absence of a universally accepted fairness metric, a case-specific approach becomes mandatory. Existing statistical fairness metrics may not capture all aspects that are relevant to a context-aware assessment of non-discrimination. The goal of this work is to define a measure of fairness for AI systems based on explainable artificial intelligence concepts. Specifically, it leverages the analysis of counterfactual explanations of individuals/groups and their comparison with similar individuals/groups. Compared to existing state-of-the-art works, the contributions are (i) extending the definition of individual fairness, not limiting unfairness to decisions based on sensitive attributes but also ensuring similar treatment amongst similar individuals; (ii) revisiting (and generalising) existing notions and introducing new, more refined notions of group fairness based on counterfactuals; (iii) defining quantitative fairness metrics that reflect the evidence gathered through the analysis/comparison of counterfactual explanations.

526. When Are Arguments from Scholarly Legal Authority Legitimate?
d’Almeida, L.D. & Shecaira, F.P. (2026). When Are Arguments from Scholarly Legal Authority Legitimate?. CJLJ.
https://doi.org/10.1017/cjlj.2026.10073
Abstract (original): Abstract Judges often cite legal scholars. Sometimes they defer argumentatively to scholars as authorities. Such arguments, we claim, are neither sound nor legitimate. Legal scholarship serves two functions. One is modest and descriptive: reporting the undisputed content of legal sources and existing consensuses on some point of law. Such matters can lend themselves to authoritative settlement. But the core function of legal scholarship, involving the substantive reconstruction of suboptimal legal sources, is normative. And on such matters, we argue—by discussing the structure of arguments from authority and what counts as genuine authority—there is no room to treat scholarly pronouncements as authoritative.

527. The end of negative integration? Critical thoughts on Zglinski (and the ELS movement)
Schütze, R. (2026). The end of negative integration? Critical thoughts on Zglinski (and the ELS movement). Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10087
Abstract (original): Abstract Has there been a rise or fall in negative integration in the last 50 years? Three competing views on this question can today be distinguished. One asserts that there has been a decline of negative integration; another one argues that negative integration has, instead, risen; while a third view, finally, contends that there has been neither a fall nor a rise, because the normative degree of negative integration has remained stable over time. In the last five years, Jan Zglinski has published several provocative pieces that have come to defend a modified version of the declining-integration view. Negative integration, he claims, has become ever weaker over time due, mainly, to a judicial rise of political deference towards the Member States. Negative integration has thus assumed ‘a stronger national flavour’ and may even be coming to an end! Importantly and innovatively, Zglinski arrives at this result by means of an ‘empirical legal studies’ method; and this ELS-method aims to settle the debate. For an ardent defender of the second view on the evolution of the internal market and a devotee of an historical-conceptual approach to European Union law this poses a tricky question: did Zglinski’s ‘data’ really meet the ‘law’ better than all qualitative-doctrinal views that preceded him?

528. Expanding Epistemic Appropriation
Kok, S. (2026). Expanding Epistemic Appropriation. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10146
Abstract (original): Abstract In this article, I propose an expanded conceptualization of epistemic appropriation. I argue that epistemic appropriation can be either wrongful or non-wrongful, and that it can occur in all directions – from marginalized to dominant communities, but also from dominant to marginalized communities. Furthermore, I argue that epistemic appropriation can take place both inter- and intra-communally. Lastly, I argue that the epistemic resources that are appropriated do not have to originate in the community they are appropriated from to be considered appropriation, as this can also take place when a community has a stake in a certain use of these resources. In doing so, I come to the following, stripped-down, definition: epistemic appropriation is the uptake of epistemic resources that obscures something about the resources.

529. Negotiated Outcomes and Abbreviated Trials in Criminal Cases: Balancing Fairness and Efficiency in Three Legal Systems
Caianiello, M. et al. (2026). Negotiated Outcomes and Abbreviated Trials in Criminal Cases: Balancing Fairness and Efficiency in Three Legal Systems. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10232
Abstract (original): Abstract Facing expanded criminal dockets, justice systems around the world are looking for ways to expedite proceedings and save resources while still offering credible procedures for adjudicating criminal cases. In the United States, defendants tend to face an all-or-nothing choice between two alternatives: a full trial at which all relevant evidence is presented and examined; or a plea bargain leading to a conviction without any presentation of evidence and the waiver of the defendant’s procedural rights in exchange for a reduced sentence. Even in routine misdemeanor cases, a full trial requires substantial expenditures of resources, and its procedural complexity may be out of proportion to the minor seriousness of such cases. Traditional plea bargaining, however, brings inordinate pressure to bear on defendants to plead guilty, often fails to elucidate the facts, and lacks sufficient guarantees of a fair outcome. In many jurisdictions, “intermediate” dispositions have developed, aimed at adjudicating cases both efficiently and fairly. We discuss solutions from Germany, Italy, and select U.S. states. They include bench trials and brief proceedings based on defendants’ acceptance of responsibility. After describing their scope and application, we argue that they can deliver efficiency without unduly compromising fairness, transparency, and just outcomes.

531. Radical Self-Care and the Need to Care for Others: On the Injustice of Being Denied Sufficient Opportunities to Care for Those One Loves

https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10107
Abstract (original): Abstract As many care and relational ethicists have rightly contended, an ethical wrong and a form of social and political injustice occurs when genuine needs and legitimate wants for care go unfulfilled to a minimally satisfactory degree. Yet, as I argue here, injustice also occurs when groups and individuals are systematically prevented from fulfilling genuine needs and legitimate wants to care for those they love and as the subjects of their care genuinely need and legitimately want in turn. Being systematically and forcibly deprived of sufficient, meaningful opportunities to support the wellbeing of those one cares about is a form of cruel and degrading treatment unbecoming of a decent society and a grave injustice. At the same time that a radical practice of self-care demands critically scrutinizing one’s investments of care in an effort to undo those that are self-demeaning and that may themselves be the products of injustice and oppression, so too does it demand supporting the development and sufficient satisfaction of genuine needs and legitimate wants to care for others insofar as the satisfaction of these needs and wants can be just as vital to one’s own welfare as it is to that of those for whom one cares.

534. Destabilisation of Indigenous governance under neocolonial legal pluralism: the Zongo case in Bolivia
Baffero, P. (2026). Destabilisation of Indigenous governance under neocolonial legal pluralism: the Zongo case in Bolivia. International Journal of Law in Context.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s1744552326100676
Abstract (original): Abstract Indigenous movements in Bolivia have long struggled for legal recognition and autonomy, culminating in constitutional reforms that formally recognise Indigenous jurisdiction as a key dimension of self-determination. This article argues, however, that the practical operation of legal pluralism in Bolivia is shaped less by constitutional design than by the institutional weakness of the Constitutional Court. Through analysis of the Zongo case, it examines how the Court’s pluralist rulings, while doctrinally affirming Indigenous jurisdiction and advancing intercultural and decolonial reasoning, remain ineffectively enforced, producing two interrelated consequences. First, Indigenous communities are continuously criminalised for actions undertaken within constitutionally recognised Indigenous jurisdiction. Second, criminalisation compels them to engage defensively with ordinary jurisdiction, generating juridification that destabilises collective authority. Together, these dynamics show how legal pluralism, when selectively enforced, operates in practice as neocolonial governance: Indigenous autonomy is formally affirmed yet subordinated by the persistent supremacy of ordinary legal power.

Abstract (original): Abstract Alethic nihilism, as presented by Will Gamester and David Liggins, is the view that nothing is true. 542. A Habit-Building View of Democracy: Mary Parker Follett and Motor Activities
Cardin-Trudeau, E. (2026). A Habit-Building View of Democracy: Mary Parker Follett and Motor Activities. Political Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00905917261487609
Abstract (original): Citizens of democracies should live democratic lives and develop democratic habits. That is one of Mary Parker Follett’s core insights on democracy. A child of the Gilded Age, Follett experienced the erosion of communal relationships, the intensification of socio-economic inequalities, and the alienation of ordinary people from the complex political system that governed their lives. She saw democracy become remote and foreign to citizens who had little control over what happened to them. Her solution was simple: to reinject a dose of democracy into the everyday life of citizens. This paper retrieves Follett’s embodied and habitual vision of democracy for contemporary political theory. Follett claimed that citizens should participate actively in the democratic life of their society; however, she conceived of democratic participation as going beyond decision-making and deliberative activities. She thought that participation in simple forms of embodied joint actions, like recreation and collective chores, could also benefit democracy. Drawing from Follett, I argue that participation in a wide variety of embodied practices of collective action provides benefits to individuals and society by instilling democratic habits in citizens. Those habits incline those who develop them to enter and maintain relationships as free and equal peers with all sorts of people to reach common ends. When faced with collective problems, therefore, these individuals are more likely to choose democratic collective action and to do so more effectively.

545. CEO Inherited Altruism, Firm Corporate Social Responsibility, and Shareholder Returns
Marra, M. et al. (2026). CEO Inherited Altruism, Firm Corporate Social Responsibility, and Shareholder Returns. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06475-6
Abstract (original): Abstract We examine the impact of CEOs’ inherited altruistic inclination on firm corporate social responsibility (CSR) performance and shareholder returns. Specifically, we look at U.S. CEOs’ altruism as an inherited value of the CEOs’ ancestral cultural origin that shapes their moral behavior and influences firm commitment to altruistic and genuine CSR. We find that firms led by CEOs with stronger inherited altruistic preferences have better CSR performance after controlling for firm financial and corporate governance attributes and other leadership characteristics. The impact of CEO inherited altruism on CSR is higher when CEOs operate under lower cash flow pressure. Consistent with a view of ethical leadership which protects the interests of both stakeholders and shareholders, we find that CEOs who inherit altruistic preferences are not significantly associated with firms’ stock returns under normal circumstances, while they protect shareholder returns during times of financial crisis and recessions. The results survive several robustness and endogeneity checks.

Abstract (original): Abstract Religious Education faces new ethical challenges as AI-powered chatbots become capable of offering scriptural explanations, moral guidance, and personalized learning support. 549. Some Questions About the Conditions Necessary for Leading a Life
Buss, S. (2026). Some Questions About the Conditions Necessary for Leading a Life. The Journal of Value Inquiry.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10790-026-10118-4
Abstract (original): Abstract What is it to lead a life? This question prompts many others. In these brief comments, I focus on two issues: (1) How does leading a life relate to occupying the role of a spectator , where this includes being a spectator of one’s life? (2) What is it for a life to be one’s own , where one is justifiably partial toward anything that qualifies as such ? My reflections move from spectatorship to partiality and back. In so doing, they probe three conditions Samuel Scheffler plausibly claims to be key ingredients of leading a life: exercising one’s agency, being subject to the special claims of a very small number of human beings to whom one is personally attached, and being engaged with things one takes to be valuable. How close, I wonder, can one come to not satisfying any one of these conditions without ceasing to lead a life – and even a good life?

550. Signalling and Moral Ambiguity
Benn, C. (2026). Signalling and Moral Ambiguity. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09576-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Moral agents often act under conditions of informational asymmetry: while they may know that their actions are permissible, observers may lack the information needed to reach the same conclusion. This paper explores the ethical significance of such asymmetries. Drawing inspiration from Signalling Theory, I introduce the concept of normative signalling : the idea that agents have moral reasons to choose actions that not only are permissible but also appear unambiguously so to others. I argue that morally ambiguous actions can generate anxiety and undermine trust, allow or demand harmful and unnecessary interventions, and undermine moral education. By analysing a range of cases, I show that reducing moral ambiguity through normative signalling can be a matter of moral responsibility. Further, I show that the strength and scope of this responsibility—like other moral responsibilities for example to be beneficent or kind—is dependent on context, sensitive to costs and so lies on a spectrum from the supererogatory to the morally obligatory. Highlighting the informational value of ethical action, I demonstrate that, in a world where our actions are not—and never can be—completely transparent to others, ethics is fundamentally incomplete if it fails to take into account how our actions play the role of evidence of our moral commitments rather than the mere fulfilment of them. It is therefore of moral importance that not only are our actions permissible but that they are seen to be permissible.

555. The Work and Values of Relational Justice
Dagan, H. & Dorfman, A. (2026). The Work and Values of Relational Justice. Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10982-026-09596-1
Abstract (original): Abstract In Relational Justice we lay out a new approach to understanding some of society’s most important building blocks, arguing that private law should, and to a significant degree already does, abide by the fundamental commitment to reciprocal respect for self-determination and substantive equality. The generous and rigorous book reviews of Aditi Bagchi, Marija Bartl, Brian Bix, Andrew Gold, Felipe Jiménez, and Joanna Langille push us to clarify and refine two critical points. First, the ambition of private law is primarily constructive and prospective. This means that relational justice is aimed at offering a broad loadstar that should guide legal systems across time and place; and it provides tools for internal legal critique, thus avoiding the risk of quietism which often plagues approaches that take law seriously (as we do). Second, relational justice is a liberal theory of private law, which is required by the commitments to freedom and equality, properly conceived. This thesis implies that relational justice is sharply contrasted with corrective justice (in all its variations); that its instantiation in private law is intrinsically valuable; that it incorporates built-in features which ensure that private law does not overreach any liberal boundary; and that it points to categories where elevated interpersonal responsibility is called for.

558. Epistemic assholes
Boult, C. (2026). Epistemic assholes. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05816-2
Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper, I develop and defend the concept epistemic assholes . An epistemic asshole is someone who exhibits a distinctive form of epistemic badness characterized not by gross irrationality or hostility to truth, but by a stable, entitled disregard for one’s epistemic relations with others. Drawing on Aaron James’s (2012) account of moral assholes, I examine how people can systematically exploit epistemic cooperation by allowing themselves special advantages—such as “freedom from inquiry”, “doxastic comfort”, and “freedom to criticize”—while remaining insulated from criticism by an entrenched sense of entitlement. The resulting view, the Relational Approach to Epistemic Assholes (REA), explains why epistemic assholes are not epistemically the worst, yet are nonetheless deeply galling. After motivating the need for this concept, I show how REA captures both “impure” cases (involving both epistemic and moral assholery) and “pure” epistemic assholes, whose objectionable conduct is driven by epistemic aims themselves. I further explore connections between epistemic assholes and epistemic vice, and the causal role that epistemic assholery can play in active ignorance and epistemic exploitation. Through this latter connection, I argue that epistemic assholes play an insidious causal role in sustaining objectionable social power dynamics. The concept of epistemic assholes provides a valuable lens for understanding important but often-hidden hazards in our social epistemic environment.

559. Is time dilation ‘real’? Einstein and the transverse Doppler effect
Giovanelli, M. (2026). Is time dilation ‘real’? Einstein and the transverse Doppler effect. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05827-z
Abstract (original): Abstract As early as 1907, Einstein realized that the second-order transverse Doppler redshift reported by Stark in the spectral lines of fast-moving ions in canal rays could serve as a direct experimental test of time dilation. He rejected Stark’s dynamical explanation of the effect as a real frequency change of the moving ions. Instead, assuming that ions of the same kind emit the same spectral lines when at relative rest, Einstein offered a kinematical explanation of the frequency shift as a consequence of time dilation. He labeled the effect apparent , since the shifted line is not recorded by a spectrograph co-moving with the ion. At the same time, he regarded it as real , in the sense that it would not occur if the old kinematics held, and is thus empirically testable. From the 1920s onward, Einstein argued that the behavior of atomic clocks calls for a dynamical explanation. The present paper shows, however, that even if a special relativistic explanation of the spectral identity of atoms were available, it would merely reinforce the claim that relativistic time dilation admits a purely kinematical interpretation. It concludes that modern dynamicists misread Einstein’s plea for a dynamical account of clock behavior as a demand for an explanation of time dilation, whereas it was primarily motivated by the problem of its confirmation .

560. Where’s the harm? On the argument from inductive risk in particle physics
Panhuys, M.v. (2026). Where’s the harm? On the argument from inductive risk in particle physics. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05832-2
Abstract (original): Abstract The Argument from Inductive Risk (AIR) explicitly calls for the consideration of non-epistemic values whenever significant non-epistemic consequences, usually understood as harms towards individuals, groups or society at large, are foreseeable. In current philosophical literature, the AIR is often assumed to have limited relevance to fundamental research, particularly in fields like particle physics which has received little attention. This paper aims to address this gap by developing experimental particle physics as a problem case for standard formulations of the AIR. I argue that, although errors in particle physics do not consistently give rise to direct socio-ethical consequences of the kind emphasized in the literature, the field nevertheless involves significant forms of epistemic responsibility arising from its technological embeddedness and broader social entanglements. Moreover, I show that the scope of the AIR depends crucially on how risk is conceptualized. While a strictly decision-theoretic framework tends to exclude particle physics by narrowing the notion of risk, alternative approaches from the ethics of technology help capture indirect and uncertain consequences. The paper therefore argues for further refinement of the AIR, rather than its simple extension, and calls for a more explicit and pluralistic account of risk in the assessment of values in science.

562. Bullshit slurs
Podosky, P.C. & Cousens, C. (2026). Bullshit slurs. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05835-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Slurs can be used and misused in different ways. We examine two related misapplications. Sometimes a speaker mistakes the person they are slurring as being correctly targeted by a slur. Other times, a speaker is indifferent to whether the slur correctly applies at all. We argue that this latter case constitutes a bullshit slur . Such slurs are distinctively troubling. From the speaker’s perspective, indifference to a slur’s conventional application widens the subordinating force of their utterance, directly threatening their target’s equal standing and warranting greater offence than cases of mistaken identity. From the hearer’s perspective, bullshit slurs foreclose a crucial form of counterspeech: self-protection via linguistic correction. This leaves the target in a particularly precarious position: subordinated directly and unable to engage in self-protective counterspeech.

563. Reconsidering the challenge to traditional metaethics from empirical research on folk moral objectivism
Yang, Q. (2026). Reconsidering the challenge to traditional metaethics from empirical research on folk moral objectivism. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05837-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Recent empirical research on folk moral objectivism appears to bring nothing but bad news for traditional metaethics. Experimental philosophers and psychologists claim that their findings are incompatible with traditional metaethical theories and therefore provide reasons for developing non-traditional metaethical theories. In this paper, I argue that many traditional metaethical theories have the resources to show that they are not inconsistent with the findings of disagreement task studies. If this is correct, then these findings do not, by themselves, settle the choice between traditional and non-traditional metaethical theories. I then sketch how further empirical research might help distinguish between these competing theories. Finally, by distinguishing five different understandings of moral objectivity and explaining how they matter for traditional metaethics, I show that the significance of existing empirical findings depends on the relevant sense of objectivity and suggest what future research on folk moral objectivism should take into account.

564. Understanding metaphysical misunderstanding: (non-)individuals and the metaphysical nature of quantum objects
Arroyo, R. & Arenhart, J.R.B. (2026). Understanding metaphysical misunderstanding: (non-)individuals and the metaphysical nature of quantum objects. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05839-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Deep scientific realism is scientific realism plus the claim that science requires a metaphysical interpretation. Metaphysics is supposed to fill an intelligibility gap: science by itself, it is argued, does not provide a clear image of reality. The metaphysics needed to deliver the goods comes from a toolbox of concepts furnished by free range metaphysics. Such concepts are developed in connection with a world that is familiar to us, and by being applied to science, they carry this familiarity to the most unfamiliar confines of reality. This article examines the prospects of such a strategy. Focusing on the case of quantum objects’ (non-)individuality, it argues that deep realism fails to deliver the promised intelligibility. Construing quantum entities as non-individuals faces an empty toolbox, ‘non-individuality’ being too closely attached to quantum mechanics itself. Dressing quantum entities as individuals distorts the usual familiarity brought by the concept of individuality, since it now has to conform to the unusual demands of quantum mechanics to the point of being unrecognizable. Instead of enhancing our grasp of quantum reality, the imposition of metaphysical categories risks producing not understanding, but metaphysical misunderstanding.

566. New is Different: What Middle-Sized Things Teach about Causation
Sánchez-Cañizares, J. (2026). New is Different: What Middle-Sized Things Teach about Causation. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10489-0
Abstract (original): Abstract The existence of middle-sized systems in the universe is far from accidental; it carries significant consequences for our understanding of both physics and metaphysics, bearing on the epistemic assumptions of the former and the role of causality in the latter. By examining how science actually engages with individual systems, we expose the weaknesses of the principle of physical causal closure and make the case for a pluralistic account of causality in the spirit of neo-Aristotelian philosophy. This renewed articulation of Aristotelian causes proves consonant with the existence of a principle of individuation for middle-sized things, as well as with the novelty, continuity, and precariousness that characterize individual systems in nature.

568. Epistemic Discipline in Psychedelic-Assisted Therapy: Memory, Therapy Dynamics, Attachment Patterns, and Sexual Abuse
Simon, G. et al. (2026). Epistemic Discipline in Psychedelic-Assisted Therapy: Memory, Therapy Dynamics, Attachment Patterns, and Sexual Abuse. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09672-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Psychedelic-assisted therapy (PAT) is increasingly used for trauma-related disorders, raising ethical questions beyond client safety. We argue that PAT clients have epistemic needs and clinicians have corresponding obligations that call for epistemic humility and discipline, which are essential for regulating foreseeable epistemic risks and for PAT's ethical structure. Drawing on threads of epistemic vulnerability theory, memory research, and emerging empirical evidence on the cognitive and relational effects of psychedelics, we argue that phenomenological characteristics, including noetic quality, are unreliable indicators of historical veridicality and are associated with epistemic risks. Focusing on individual differences associated with attachment patterns and work with sexual abuse survivors, we show how disrupted epistemic trust, authority sensitivity, and structural disempowerment may magnify those epistemic risks. We then develop epistemic discipline as a fiduciary stance that requires restraint in both directions: neither endorsing experiential material as historically veridical when this cannot be established, nor introducing unwarranted doubt that undermines epistemic agency. Because such epistemic vulnerability and its associated risks are foreseeable, if not entirely predictable, we frame epistemic discipline as a fiduciary obligation of the clinician that spans preparation, dosing, and integration, as well as obligations of training programs, credentialing bodies, and professional communities with responsibility to identify and remedy relevant omissions in training and supervision. Finally, we systematize features of epistemic vulnerability and governance mechanisms across PAT phases.

570. Counterfactual Bodies, Ableism, and Oppression as
                    Possibilia
WAKED, B. (2026). Counterfactual Bodies, Ableism, and Oppression as
                    Possibilia. Journal of the American Philosophical Association.
https://doi.org/10.1017/apa.2026.10039
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article proposes a novel way to understand the oppression of ableism through the modal device of counterfactual bodies. I begin with an overview of counterfactual bodies, defined here as the reimagining of disabled individuals without their disability. Some counterfactual bodies become vehicles of oppression when distorted by the imperative of cure, under which striving to actualize a counterfactual body becomes a morally laden aim for disabled bodies. Next, I outline some of the material, psychological and physical costs of striving to actualize a counterfactual body, which includes the disproportionate distribution of resources, suspended body grief, and pain and suffering. Finally, I consider the conditions under which a counterfactual body becomes a manifestation of ableism.

572. The Epistemic Floor: Institutional Conditions for Genuine Inclusion in Psychiatric Classification
Zeller, M. (2026). The Epistemic Floor: Institutional Conditions for Genuine Inclusion in Psychiatric Classification. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10150
Abstract (original): Abstract The American Psychiatric Association is now designing the process that will produce the next iteration of the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM). Recent work on epistemic injustice in psychiatry converges on a shared diagnosis. The exclusion of psychopathologized perspectives undermines the social objectivity of psychiatric classification, and the literature leaves open how psychiatric science can engage with alternative interpretations of its domain without reducing them to the dominant framework. I propose four institutional conditions that inclusion must satisfy if it is to produce epistemic engagement and avoid tokenism. They are constitutive participation in agenda-setting and evidence evaluation, structural reproduction of participatory mechanisms across revision cycles, procedurally differentiated translation interfaces that preserve the critical content of non-standard perspectives, and bidirectional uptake with epistemic attitude and documented accountability. The conditions operate on the epistemic process that produces diagnostic categories without modifying the operational form those categories take, and are compatible with the functional requirements of the DSM and with the professional identity of psychiatry. They are designed to make inclusion structurally reproducible, where it has so far been contingently achievable, and they supply evaluative criteria for the design decisions the revision now confronts.

573. Protecting (Trans and) Non-Binary People Against Discrimination in and Through EU Law: Time to Overcome Blind Spots
Cannoot, P. (2026). Protecting (Trans and) Non-Binary People Against Discrimination in and Through EU Law: Time to Overcome Blind Spots. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10236
Abstract (original): Abstract This Article examines the persistent gaps in the protection of trans and non–binary people against discrimination in European Union law, situating its analysis within a broader socio–legal context marked by rising anti–gender rhetoric and uneven national safeguards. Drawing on recent empirical data, including the 2023 FRA LGBTIQ Survey, it highlights the disproportionate levels of discrimination, violence, and harassment experienced by trans and non–binary individuals across the EU, as well as the growing political hostility that shapes their lived realities. Against this backdrop, the Article assesses the current EU legal framework—primary law, the Charter of Fundamental Rights, and secondary legislation—and demonstrates that explicit protection on the grounds of gender identity and gender expression remains absent. It then analyzes the evolving case law of the Court of Justice of the European Union, which has gradually interpreted the prohibition of sex discrimination as extending to certain forms of discrimination against trans persons, while still leaving significant blind spots, particularly for non–binary individuals. Building on recent jurisprudential developments, the Article explores the potential for a more inclusive and expansive reading of “sex” within EU equality law, arguing that such an approach could provide robust protection for all individuals facing discrimination based on gender identity or expression. Finally, it addresses the complex normative and doctrinal challenges that arise when balancing the interests of cisgender women with those of trans and non–binary people under the umbrella of sex–based equality. Positioned within a human–rights–oriented and social–justice–driven framework, the Article ultimately calls for renewed legal and political commitment to ensuring that EU equality law effectively safeguards the rights of trans and non–binary individuals.

574. How to Avoid ‘Moralism’ about Obligation
Sekine, K. (2026). How to Avoid ‘Moralism’ about Obligation. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101569
Abstract (original): Abstract Sometimes it seems that no matter how faultlessly we conduct ourselves, we cannot but fail in our obligations, making us apt to feel morally responsible. But according to the popular view I call ‘moralism’ about obligation, we cannot breach our obligations faultlessly, leading moralists to pathologize our feelings, or worse still, to find fault in our conduct. I argue that we should reject moralism, but that we should not do so in the ‘hard’ way, which denies the plausible claim that blameworthiness entails fault. Yet we cannot ‘soften’ our anti-moralism unless we can explain how ordinary agent-regret can rise to a ‘moral’ quality sufficient to do justice to the phenomenology. I offer the outline of a proposal according to which a failure to meet our obligations gives rise to agent-regret with the following ‘moral’ quality: it is extraordinarily hard to let go of or justifiably repress it.

619. The Doctrine of State Necessity
Peftinas, A. (2026). The Doctrine of State Necessity. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70064
Abstract (original): This article examines the doctrine of state necessity as a distinct but relatively underexplored doctrine of constitutional law. Drawing on case law from a range of jurisdictions, it considers instances in which courts have accepted otherwise unconstitutional measures as lawful in order to preserve the operation of state institutions or protect significant parts of the legal order. The article reconstructs the doctrine as a limited, temporary and conditional remedy for situations of constitutional paralysis or systemic legal invalidity. It argues that state necessity should be distinguished both from other cases of emergency law and from a general ‘doctrine of necessity’. The article questions the central theoretical grounds that are offered in the case law and literature in support of the doctrine, such as those based on special executive powers, on analogies with the necessity defence, or on a broader supra‐constitutional ‘law of necessity’. It argues that the doctrine should be understood as a doctrine of constitutional law shaped within the tradition of equity. On that account, the doctrine functions as a limited corrective in exceptional cases of constitutional deadlock in order to preserve the legal order without displacing the constitution itself.

620. Wittgenstein: The Senses of Use By Sandra Laugier, Trans. Daniela Ginsburg. University of Chicago Press, 2025. 240pp. ISBN: 9780226824574

621. In Defence of Open Voting
Seglow, J. (2026). In Defence of Open Voting. J Soc Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1111/josp.70071
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper argues against the secret ballot on the basis of a view of democracy where voters are mutually accountable to one another for their electoral choices. In contrast to Mill's and more recent arguments for opening up the ballot which have a broadly consequentialist nature, this paper offers an principle argument. First it suggests that the egalitarian defence of democracy could be supplemented by augmenting what the egalitarian relationship between citizens consists in. It argues for a ‘substantive egalitarian’ view of democracy where citizens cast their vote on the basis of the values and ideals they support, delegating the role of legislating on their bases to representatives. Each citizen is both an agent, qua co‐author of the ideals underlying state action, and a subject, qua her status as an individual affected by legislation grounded in those ideals. On that basis I argue that citizens' egalitarian political relationship is one of mutual horizontally accountability. The ideal of accountability implies that voters have a legal obligation to publicise their identities and a civic obligation to respond to one another's requests for the reasons for their votes. Only through presenting themselves to each other in this way can citizens as co‐authors properly assume responsibility for their role in providing the normative bases of law. The paper concludes by addressing objections to open voting, specifically that it is unfeasible because it would encourage corruption, and depress turnout and lead to civic shame.

624. The Perils of Improvement: Burke, Slavery, and Colonial Governance in the West Indies
Carroll, R. (2026). The Perils of Improvement: Burke, Slavery, and Colonial Governance in the West Indies. Political Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00905917261479646
Abstract (original): Edmund Burke’s thoughts on plantation slavery have long been the subject of scholarly attention. Too often, however, discerning Burke’s long-term intention in relation to slavery has taken precedence over examining the specific administrative strategies he proposed for managing enslaved populations in the interim. Seeking to remedy this gap, the article reads Burke’s Sketch for a Negro Code as an exercise in colonial ameliorationism; that is, a reform programme that looked to ameliorate the institution of slavery as a way of strengthening British colonialism in the West Indies and civilising the African coast. Using colonial ameliorationism as an interpretive lens, I argue, allows us to reconstruct a consistent Burkean vision for colonial management in the West Indies, one that survived his vacillations on the question of abolition. It also throws into doubt the common argument that Burke saw imperial governance in negative terms; a matter of taming abuses of power and exercising restraint. In the West Indies, by contrast, Burke’s commitment to ameliorationism led him to embrace a productive form of colonial power aimed at enhancing the physical and mental capacities of the enslaved population through discipline, religious instruction, and the encouragement of biological reproduction. I conclude by showing how Burke’s faith in the improvability of the slave system is vulnerable to his own criticisms of other projects for political reform; namely that a certain guilelessness can blind reformers to the dangers inherent in their projects and reconcile them to what they purport to oppose.

626. Passion and Propriety: Adam Smith’s Theory of Approval
Abrahamsen, C.C. (2026). Passion and Propriety: Adam Smith’s Theory of Approval. The Philosophical Review.
https://doi.org/10.1215/00318108-12678438
Abstract (original): For sentimentalists like Hume, the most basic objects of our approval are actions or character traits that cause pleasure. But Adam Smith instead takes as the fundamental object of approval the propriety of sentiments: a sentiment’s being ‘suited’ and ‘proportionate’ to its cause. Yet he is not particularly clear about what it is for a sentiment to match an object in this way. Moreover, extant interpretations are unsatisfying, because they generally fail to make sense of Smith’s claim that, in order to judge whether someone else’s sentiments are proper, one must use one’s own sentiments as a “rule or canon.” This article provides a novel account of Smithian propriety, based on the idea that Smith sees sentiments as representations of value. Sentimental propriety, on this account, is a kind of representational correctness; one judges the sentiments of someone else to be proper just insofar as ones share their sentimental perspective on the relevant object.

627. The Limits of Reasonable Disagreement
Taylor, A. (2026). The Limits of Reasonable Disagreement. The Philosophical Review.
https://doi.org/10.1215/00318108-12678463
Abstract (original): Political liberals hold that the exercise of political power must be made acceptable to all reasonable citizens. This view is widely thought to be vulnerable to a powerful objection: There is too little that would be acceptable to all reasonable citizens for political liberalism to provide a plausible basis for legitimate state action. This article offers a new response to this challenge, which proceeds in two steps. First, the article develops a distinctive interpretation of the reasonable acceptability test, which is based on the moral ideal that every citizen should be able to find her social position acceptable. Second, the article uses this interpretation to show that there are liberal principles of political morality that no citizen can reasonably reject.

641. The psychology of machine intelligence: evaluating the necessity of artificial intelligence in everyday smart devices and automated systems
Bhattacharya, S. (2026). The psychology of machine intelligence: evaluating the necessity of artificial intelligence in everyday smart devices and automated systems. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01421-8
Abstract (original): Abstract The exponential rise of Artificial Intelligence (AI) has seamlessly woven intelligent systems into nearly every sphere of human life from smart homes and healthcare to transportation and entertainment. Yet, beneath this technological revolution lies a deeper psychological question: are humans truly benefiting from AI, or gradually examining the implications of increasing reliance on intelligent systems. Preliminary data suggests that over 78% of individuals in developed societies engage with AI-driven devices at least 12 times a day, while 62% admit to relying on automated systems for decisions once made intuitively. This study employs quantitative performance metrics and psychological dependency assessments across a diverse sample of 80 + participants to analyze the cognitive, emotional, and social implications of daily AI exposure. Comparative experiments between AI-assisted and manual task environments will measure productivity, satisfaction, and creative independence to determine whether constant AI support enhances or inhibits human potential. While the findings are expected to reveal fascinating insights into how humans psychologically adapt to machine intelligence, one crucial question remains unresolved does AI genuinely enrich human life, or subtly condition it for convenience at the cost of individuality. The answer, as the forthcoming analysis will uncover, may challenge the very foundation of our belief in progress.

642. US Food and Drug Administration Officials’ Public Statements on Regulatory Flexibility in New Drug Approval, 2021–2024
Jiang, T.E. et al. (2026). US Food and Drug Administration Officials’ Public Statements on Regulatory Flexibility in New Drug Approval, 2021–2024. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10290
Abstract (original): Abstract The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has historically exercised substantial regulatory flexibility to adjust the type, amount, and quality of evidence necessary to support drug approval for areas of unmet need. Our objective was to characterize how FDA officials described and justified regulatory flexibility in recent public statements. We conducted a thematic analysis of quotations related to regulatory flexibility from 31 FDA officials published in Pink Sheet and STAT, two leading news sources covering FDA, from January 2021 to June 2024. In these statements, FDA officials typically justified prioritizing patient access to promising treatments over certainty regarding efficacy at the time of approval, even outside the accelerated approval pathway. Regulators also highlighted confirmatory studies conducted after accelerated approval as important for establishing drug efficacy. However, they recognized challenges to their completion, including insufficient industry incentives and FDA enforcement, and were reticent to interpret failed studies as evidence of failed drugs. Overall, in public statements from 2021–2024, FDA officials acknowledged challenges posed by regulatory flexibility but publicly justified its broad use to improve patient access for serious diseases despite even extended uncertainty. Under the Trump Administration, many officials have changed but efforts to advance regulatory flexibility continue.

643. Delisted Patents on Pharmaceuticals, 1998–2024
Kirby-Rivera, L. et al. (2026). Delisted Patents on Pharmaceuticals, 1998–2024. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10292
Abstract (original): Abstract Patents listed in the US Food and Drug Administration’s (FDA) Orange Book can delay generic competition, yet little is known about how often patents are removed before expiration or why these delistings occur. We reviewed Orange Book records from 1998 through 2024 to identify patents removed before their expiration dates, classified patents according to the subject matter of their claims and assessed potential reasons for delisting using patent litigation and prosecution databases, FDA records, and Federal Trade Commission (FTC) actions. Among 9,980 patents listed during the study period, 535 (5%) were delisted before expiration. Only 71 (13%) claimed active ingredients; most delisted patents instead covered methods of use (177, 38%), formulations (167, 36%), or delivery devices (75, 16%). Potential reasons for delisting included potential invalidation in litigation (226, 42%), product discontinuation (207, 39%), and patent reexamination or reissue (60,11%). Orange Book patent delistings predominantly involved secondary patents rather than active-ingredient patents. Greater clarity and oversight regarding Orange Book listing requirements could reduce questionable listings and unnecessary barriers to generic competition.

644. Wittgenstein and Immunity to Error Through Misidentification
Child, W. (2026). Wittgenstein and Immunity to Error Through Misidentification. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101557
Abstract (original): Abstract Rachael Wiseman (2019) has challenged the standard understanding of Wittgenstein’s Blue Book discussion of ‘two different cases in the use of the word “I”’. Wittgenstein is standardly read as identifying an important distinction between the use of ‘I’ as object, which involves the recognition of a person and is vulnerable to errors of misidentification, and the use of ‘I’ as subject, which involves no recognition of a person and is immune to error through misidentification. Wiseman argues that Wittgenstein does not in fact endorse such a distinction but warns against it. She contends that the standard interpretation ignores the context of the passage in the Blue Book discussion of solipsism and that it misunderstands the nature of self-consciousness. 645. Love or Law: Viking Foundations of Medieval English Dispute Settlement
Stattel, J.A. (2026). Love or Law: Viking Foundations of Medieval English Dispute Settlement. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101850
Abstract (original): Abstract In prominent legal historical works, Michael Clanchy and Stephen D. White constructed an important paradigm for medieval dispute settlement by adapting the phrase “law and love” from the Anglo-Norman Leges Henrici Primi . Yet, rarely has this aphorism’s background been explored. This article argues that “love or law” has roots in Viking-Age Scandinavia, where this Old Norse terminology represented a unique dynamic of dispute resolution revolving around harsh “law” pressuring parties to make “love” settlements outside of court. Contrasting sharply with Anglo-Saxon disputing norms centered on formal compensation tariffs, “love or law” found its way to England’s tenth-century Danelaw, first recorded in the Wantage Code. This alliterative pair continues in English writings, while “love-days” become a major cultural feature, and more broadly, the dynamic of threateningly punitive “law” inducing “love” agreements appears in action within early common law case records. In ways that recollect Icelandic and Danelaw practices, negotiated compensation occurring “under the shadow of law” may have been a major feature of medieval English dispute as hinted by our surviving sources. Scandinavian dispute concepts, occasionally coined by unique “love or law” vocabulary, may have had a subtle yet profound influence on some of the grounding assumptions of English common law.

646. Syntax and Intuition: On Gödel's Critique of Carnap
Chen, L. (2026). Syntax and Intuition: On Gödel's Critique of Carnap. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70124
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The syntactic interpretation of mathematics (SIM) refers to the view that interprets mathematical propositions as true expressions solely by virtue of the syntactical conventions governing the use of those expressions and not by corresponding to objective mathematical facts. This view, exemplified by the Vienna Circle and particularly Rudolf Carnap, is the target of Gödel's sustained critique and his defence of mathematical realism and intuition. I analyse Gödel's central arguments against SIM: the arbitrariness and asymmetry arguments, which challenge the notion that mathematics is contentless, and the circularity argument, which concerns the role of consistency proofs. I also examine two contemporary defences of Carnap: one appealing to dependable rather than provable consistency, and another recasting Carnap's project as transforming justificatory questions into pragmatic choices of linguistic frameworks. I contend that both defences fail to fulfil the original philosophical ambitions of the syntactic view, and that this failure indirectly bolsters the case for mathematical realism.

650. Objects as Means of Sustainability Knowledge Co-Creation: Working on Value-Laden Issues Across the Research-Practice Boundary
Dagnaud, K. et al. (2026). Objects as Means of Sustainability Knowledge Co-Creation: Working on Value-Laden Issues Across the Research-Practice Boundary. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06478-3
Abstract (original): Abstract The negative sustainability impacts of business keep escalating despite the profusion of research-based evidence regarding problems and solution pathways. Sustainability knowledge is complex and value-laden, thus often difficult to act upon. Co-creation between researchers and practitioners stands as a fruitful means of making sustainability knowledge more actionable, yet we lack insight into what motivates and facilitates sustainability knowledge co-creation. Boundary objects, which assist collaboration among diverse groups, can facilitate knowledge co-creation between researchers and practitioners, but little is known of their workings in the context of tackling value-laden sustainability issues. Based on rich qualitative data from two empirical cases, we examine how boundary objects motivate and facilitate knowledge co-creation between practitioners and researchers, helping to make sustainability knowledge more actionable. Our analysis illuminates three main dynamics whereby boundary objects do so: boundary objects initiate dialogue on complex issues through conveying clarity, provide a physical realm for co-creation in a value-laden domain, and motivate participation despite ethical vulnerability. These insights elaborate and challenge previous research regarding the boundary object ambiguity-clarity tension in the context of sustainability knowledge co-creation among diverse actor groups. Specifically, our findings foreground boundary object clarity, rather than ambiguity, in helping to initiate dialogue on complex sustainability issues. Furthermore, our analysis extends understanding of participant motivations to engage in sustainability knowledge co-creation, indicating that objects provide instrumental value and more intrinsic learning value, as well as help parties be with ethical vulnerability induced by knowledge co-creation around value-laden issues.

651. Effective Worker Resistance and Realist Ideology Critique

https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06491-6
Abstract (original): Abstract When do workers have good reasons to endorse a specific resistance strategy against managerial injustice? The answer partly requires clarifying whether that form of resistance is effective. But how should we understand efficacy? Political theorists cannot simply refer this question to empirical researchers. There is conceptual unclarity on what counts as efficacy. This paper shows that efficacy is a complex concept with multiple politically relevant dimensions. We illustrate this complexity by developing an epistemic notion of efficacy through a method of ideology critique, articulating a distinct dimension of effective resistance. A practice of resistance is epistemically efficacious if it reliably disrupts the mechanisms of ideological legitimation in the workplace by creating opportunities for egalitarian and collective contestation. Epistemic efficacy is a distinct evaluative lens that can assess the effectiveness of a resistance practice. After presenting the main argument, we apply our framework to the case of gossip as workplace resistance.

654. Disability as Human Potential: Reorienting Transhumanism Through the Human Rights Model of Disability

https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70121
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article's central object of critical reflection is the ableist anthropological and ethical assumptions underpinning the transhumanist project's implicit normative stance towards people with disabilities, who are framed as defective and futureless beings. 655. The Relevance and Materiality of Illegal Acts: Examining Auditor and Investor Judgments
Taylor, E.Z. & Wright, N.S. (2026). The Relevance and Materiality of Illegal Acts: Examining Auditor and Investor Judgments. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06477-4
Abstract (original): Abstract The audit expectation gap exists and persists due to changing “…social, economic, and political developments of the time.” (Sikka, et al., 1998) Like stakeholders’ demands for and the profession’s response to fraud detection in the 2000s, investors are increasingly interested in C-suite employees’ and entity’s illegal acts, finding them both relevant and material (Cline, Walkling & Yore, 2018). This study identifies a gap between auditors’ and investors’ relevance and materiality judgments of a CEO’s illegal act. Varying role (auditor vs. investor) and using experience as a control (audit vs no audit), results show that non-auditor investors rate a CEO’s illegal act as significantly more relevant and material than do auditors placed in either role, while auditors in an investor role find a CEO’s illegal act significantly more material than auditors in an auditor role. Qualitative comments indicate that investors see managers’ personal misconduct as indicative of their lack of personal integrity and lose trust in them across personal to work situations. Auditors do not make the same connection. Our findings highlight challenges for the audit profession in meeting investors’ expectations in the context of managers’ illegal acts.

664. Protecting the Rights and Health of Intersex Children Seeking Asylum: A Child Rights Approach to Qualification as a Refugee for Intersex Children

https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10237
Abstract (original): Abstract Research on the position of intersex persons within the international refugee law system is extremely limited. Intersex persons are commonly grouped with LGBT+ issues within policy guidance, but little attention has been given to the specificity of intersex children’s needs. 665. Reforming the EU Return Acquis through “Exceptionalized Pluralism”: From Internal Borders to Offshore Enforcement
Moraru, M.B. (2026). Reforming the EU Return Acquis through “Exceptionalized Pluralism”: From Internal Borders to Offshore Enforcement. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10239
Abstract (original): Abstract The 2024–2026 reform of the EU return procedure constitutes the most far–reaching transformation of the European return acquis since the adoption of the key instrument governing returns—Directive 2008/115/EC. This Article critically assesses the impact of the reform on the EU return acquis and more generally on the migration regulatory framework. First, it introduces the concept of “exceptionalized pluralism” to describe the defining structural feature of the reform: the multiplication of derogatory return tracks—at external and internal EU borders and extraterritorially—that systematically displace the procedural safeguards constitutionalized by the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU), progressively relegating the common return procedure from norm to residual exception. Second, it argues that this architecture raises constitutional concerns on four interconnected fronts: the codification of numerous accelerated border transfer procedures institutionalizing harmonization as the coordination of coercion rather than the harmonization of rights; the European Return Order reproducing the structural accountability gaps of the Dublin system; the systematic expansion of pre-removal detention recalibrating the constitutional relationship between the right to liberty and executive power beyond what Article 6 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the EU (CFR) and the CJEU permit; and the formalization of return externalization through “return hubs” raising rule of law and accountability issues. In conclusion, the Article argues that the legitimacy of the emerging European return system will depend not on its operational speed or uniformity, but on whether enforcement harmonization is matched by concrete implementation of Charter-based safeguards. Moreover, it warns that, absent such implementation, “exceptionalized pluralism” will continue to operate as a self-reinforcing regulatory logic, expanding its derogatory tracks and leaving courts to fulfill, under conditions of mounting institutional pressure, a constitutional function that the legislative design has failed to assume.

669. The Rights of Women: How the Natural Law Tradition Provides a More Humane Rationale
Bachiochi, E. (2026). The Rights of Women: How the Natural Law Tradition Provides a More Humane Rationale. The American Journal of Jurisprudence.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag022
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay examines, both philosophically and historically, two competing accounts of rights with their derivative and competing accounts of women’s rights: the early modern account and the natural law account. The decades-long triumph of a distinctively early modern account of the rights of women rests on a mythic image of the self-sufficient and self-owning individual theorized in the work of John Locke. A natural law account of women’s rights—put forth by Mary Wollstonecraft and the early women’s rights advocates of the mid-nineteenth century—draws from a deeper philosophical well and better connects rights with responsibilities. The differences between these two frameworks are briefly laid bare in their distinctive treatment of abortion. Finally, given the central place of morally formative education in the natural law account, I conclude the essay by applying the natural law framework I’ve developed to single-sex education vis-à-vis antidiscrimination law. In particular, I revisit the argumentation and outcome in United States v. Virginia, the 1996 case that opened the doors of a historically all-male state military academy to women, posing a counterfactual set of facts and arguments in which the academy might justly have remained single-sex.

670. The Modest Place of Practical Reason within Originalism
Strang, L.J. (2026). The Modest Place of Practical Reason within Originalism. The American Journal of Jurisprudence.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag023
Abstract (original): Abstract The natural law tradition has experienced a dramatic revival in Anglo-American thought since the publication of John Finnis’s groundbreaking Natural Law and Natural Rights in 1980. One of the most important current debates in the tradition is over the role(s) of first-order practical reasoning in ascertaining the law’s meaning. Some scholars have argued for a robust place for practical reason in ascertaining the law. There are a variety of other positions articulated by scholars. One particularly important manifestation of this debate is in the context of interpretation of the United States Constitution. This article makes three key moves within this debate. First, it describes how originalism’s own theoretical evolution, coupled with the revival of natural law in the Anglo-American world, allowed for a rapprochement between the two. Second, it argues that originalism, best understood, has a modest place for interpreter practical reason, and that this is a determinatio of the American constitutional system. Third, it articulates four reasons that support originalism’s economical use of interpreter practical reason. To the extent my argument is successful, it provides an example of a successful, mature legal system that employs interpreter practical reason moderately.

671. Constitutional law, neoliberalism, and the state: The reinvention of administrative justice in 1980s France
Perroud, T. (2026). Constitutional law, neoliberalism, and the state: The reinvention of administrative justice in 1980s France. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag101
Abstract (original): Abstract This article revisits the 1987 decision of the French Conseil constitutionnel (Constitutional Council) that conferred constitutional status on the jurisdiction of the administrative courts, framing it not as a straightforward defense of legal tradition but as a strategic response to neoliberal reforms. Drawing on previously unexamined archival materials from the Conseil d’État and the Conseil constitutionnel, it reconstructs the legal and political struggles surrounding the creation of a new competition authority and the decision to entrust judicial review of its actions to the civil rather than the administrative courts. Although the decision appeared to resist neoliberal reforms, it in fact reveals how the Conseil d’État successfully repurposed constitutional law to preserve its institutional authority amid growing criticism of the administrative state. The case illustrates how constitutional principles in France may emerge not from textual mandates or enduring legal doctrine but from tactical institutional positioning. It also sheds light on why France has experienced a more ambivalent form of neoliberalization, with state institutions such as the Conseil d’État adapting and even expanding their influence in the face of market-driven reforms.

672. Tiredness and the Capacity to Decide: The Example of Maternity Care
Brunning, L. (2026). Tiredness and the Capacity to Decide: The Example of Maternity Care. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70199
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Tiredness is taken seriously in healthcare where clinicians are concerned since good decisions require the ability to think clearly. The impact of patient tiredness is neglected. This is surprising, as there are healthcare contexts, like maternity care, where tiredness is nearly ubiquitous. Empirical research into sleep deprivation suggests that tiredness undermines substantially our abilities to understand and reason about potential choices. Since these abilities are necessary components of having capacity, tired patients may lack capacity in moments when important choices have to be made, for example a late intervention in active labour. I defend this argument. Tiredness can sometimes impair capacity and so justifies testing for capacity in some cases. Taking tiredness seriously and assessing it in practice is not paternalistic. Indeed, I argue that recognising the impact of tiredness, while failing to act on that fact, constitutes ‘dependency denial’ which harms women in maternity care.

673. Ethics of opportunistic genomic screening in paediatrics: rethinking the risk-benefit ratio
Wheeler, E. et al. (2026). Ethics of opportunistic genomic screening in paediatrics: rethinking the risk-benefit ratio. J Med Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1136/jme-2026-111946
Abstract (original): Genome-wide sequencing (GWS) has become an important tool in paediatric medicine, but it may uncover secondary findings (SFs) through opportunistic screening. The disclosure of adult-onset SFs in children, who cannot consent, raises ethical concerns regarding potential psychosocial harm and future autonomy. This paper examines whether parents should have the option to consent to opportunistic screening for actionable adult-onset conditions in their young children. Although there is consensus on disclosing actionable childhood-onset conditions, international guidelines remain divided on the return of adult-onset findings in paediatric populations. Drawing on existing literature, this paper argues for greater consensus around a risk-benefit framework centred on medical actionability. Emerging evidence suggests that the psychological harms associated with predictive testing may be less severe than previously assumed, while actionable findings may provide meaningful benefits to both the child and family. This paper further argues that upholding parental authority through shared decision-making and the concept of family benefit further supports offering parents the option to receive actionable adult-onset findings. In contrast, the disclosure of non-actionable adult-onset conditions is more difficult to justify given the absence of meaningful clinical benefit. In the absence of clear evidence of harm, a more family-centred approach that incorporates pretest counselling and ongoing support offers a practical framework for moving towards greater consensus on this long-debated issue.

674. MacIntyrean bioethics: reappraisal and outline of a research programme
Magelssen, M. et al. (2026). MacIntyrean bioethics: reappraisal and outline of a research programme. J Med Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1136/jme-2026-112142
Abstract (original): Alasdair MacIntyre’s (1929–2025) work in moral philosophy was wide-ranging, erudite and controversial. Following his death in 2025, it is appropriate to reappraise the relevance of his work to bioethics. MacIntyre’s analysis may helpfully be divided into his ‘critical project’, which criticises modern moral philosophy and reveals and explains its shortcomings, several of which arguably affect bioethics; and his ‘constructive project’, which sets out MacIntyre’s version of neo-Aristotelian virtue ethics. We summarise these ‘projects’ and how they have been used within bioethics. Our claim is that there is still significant untapped potential for work in medical ethics/bioethics inspired by MacIntyre. We propose further uses of MacIntyre under the five headings critical, diagnostic, revisionist, normative and hermeneutic. The article ends with a brief reflection on the merits and limits of a MacIntyrean bioethics.
