679. A Practitioner's Guide to Pragmatic Humeanism
Hicks, M.T. (2026). A Practitioner's Guide to Pragmatic Humeanism. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.2968
Abstract (original): All Humeans hold, roughly, that laws are informative summaries of non-lawful matters of fact. Pragmatic Humeans go further: for them, what makes these summaries the laws is their usefulness to agents like us. By adding elements of our specific epistemic interests and constraints, pragmatists contend, we can arrive at satisfying explanations of otherwise surprising features of our actual laws and our actual scientific practice. But the pragmatic shift is not without problems. The more elements of our particular psychology we add to our nomic formula, the more susceptible we are to idealistic ratbaggery. Intuitively, what can or must happen does not depend on our particular cognitive architecture: we cannot change the laws by changing us. But if laws are distinguished from accidents by features of our psychology, then changes in our psychology might lead to differences in what the laws of nature allow. My aim here is to clarify the role of pragmatic constraints, and thereby respond to this challenge from creeping idealism. My strategy has two parts. First, I argue that pragmatic constraints determine the laws only indirectly, by generating a nomic formula that itself makes no reference to agents. Second, I discuss what sorts of agents are agents like us. I argue that pragmatists should appeal to a particular sort of idealized agent, one whose specific limitations and interests have been idealized away.

680. The Art of Work in Kant’s Critique of Judgment
Re, T.C. (2026). The Art of Work in Kant’s Critique of Judgment. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.5822
Abstract (original): There is a general impression among Kant scholars that Kant has no robust theory of work. Most of his references to the topic appear in his historical and anthropological writings, where he tells us that work is burdensome, and valuable only for the sake of whatever we produce. In this paper, I argue that Kant has an under-explored theory of work in the third Critique. This theory bears little resemblance to his depiction of work in the historical and anthropological writings. The third Critique will depict work as self-expressive, creative, and free, features Kant will go on to associate with art. Kant's contention is that when work resembles art, it is both agreeable and something we enjoy for its own sake. However, when work fails to resemble art – when it is, in Kant's words, natural, scientific, and mercenary – it is both disagreeable and constrained, and begins to sound like his description of work from the historical and anthropological writings. Kant's theory of work in the third Critique has a number of important implications. The first is that it provides a new foundation for a contemporary philosophy of work that places freedom and creativity at the center of the labor process. My analysis also engages a part of Kant scholarship that has, until recently, been largely neglected. It is only in the last decade that the English-language Kant literature has taken up the question of how to make sense of Kant's remarks about work. However, the focus is rarely on Kant's aesthetic theory of work, but rather on the place of work in his moral and political philosophy. I discuss the implications of Kant's third Critique theory of work for these other debates, as well as for our understanding of the third Critique as a whole.

681. Fear and Loathing in Silicon Valley: Does AI Threaten our Valuing Practices?
MacKenzie, J. (2026). Fear and Loathing in Silicon Valley: Does AI Threaten our Valuing Practices?. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.7153
Abstract (original): We often have a visceral negative reaction to the prospect of AI taking over our creative activities or being used to demonstrate care and concern. What justifies this reaction? In this paper, I argue that AI raises two existential threats to our valuing practices. The first existential threat is forward-looking—when we offload our labor onto AI, we risk flattening our valuing landscape, turning activities and products that could have once been valued for the meaningful processes that resulted in their creation into things that can only be valued for their functionality. The second threat is backward-looking—AI forces us to reckon with the valuing discord that already exists in our lives. When we see that other people don’t care about whether the art or writing they’re consuming is AI-produced, we must reckon with the fact that they never shared in our valuing practices.

682. How Berkeley Can Go Quasi and Lose His Mind-Dependence
Streumer, B. (2026). How Berkeley Can Go Quasi and Lose His Mind-Dependence. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.7876
Abstract (original): Berkeley defends idealism partly by taking ordinary objects such as tables and stones to consist of ideas. Materialists reject this account of ordinary objects. I argue that Berkeley can undermine this rejection by making quasi-realist moves of the kind that many expressivists about normativity make. This ultimately results in a version of idealism that we cannot believe, since trying to believe it will make us believe materialism. But I argue that this version of idealism may nevertheless be true.

683. Mechanisms for Hume's Skepticism
Goldhaber, C. (2026). Mechanisms for Hume's Skepticism. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.9316
Abstract (original): What happens to a mind that embraces skepticism? On familiar classical and contemporary accounts, it suspends judgment about the matters skepticism calls into question. For Hume, such suspension can occur only temporarily. He claims that skepticism's more enduring effect is weaker: it lowers the vivacity of, and thus our confidence in, beliefs, typically leaving commonplace ones intact. Hume's associationism offers at least two explanatory mechanisms for this effect, one doxastic and one emotional. In the doxastic mechanism, causal reasoning about our failures in reasoning decreases the vivacity of beliefs formed through reasoning. In the emotional mechanism, appreciating our cognitive failures diminishes certain passions, and with them the vivacity of beliefs otherwise augmented by those passions, especially pride. Understanding these mechanisms not only resolves exegetical puzzles about Hume's apparently flippant attitudes toward skepticism, but also provides a psychologically rich picture of how minds might self-correct without exaggeration.

The worldwide UNESCO recommendation for the ethics of AI incorporates flourishing of all life, but still takes a human centric/rights approach. Indigenous Buen Vivir fosters circular, biocentric, participatory, thinking focusing on equality, integrality of life and rights of Nature. Buen Vivir can help programmers of quantum computers think in three-some (complementary opposition) instead of binary opposition, a topic for future research.

686. Animal welfare in the world of digitalization: Computer Vision in the context of foundational structures
Bosschaert, M.T. & Gremmen, B. (2026). Animal welfare in the world of digitalization: Computer Vision in the context of foundational structures. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01417-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Computer Vision is an emerging technology within Precision Livestock Farming (PLF), combining camera systems with artificial intelligence to monitor animal welfare. While animal ethicists recognize its potential benefits, they also raise concerns about possible negative implications for animal welfare. This paper argues that addressing ethical concerns cannot rely on solutions targeting isolated ethical risks; rather, potential solutions must account for the technology’s embeddedness within society-wide structures. The focus in this paper will be on foundational structures, these structures concern how the world is experienced and understood, what is taken as self-evident. Over centuries, humans have increasingly delegated tasks to machines and standardized the world in doing so. This process has fostered growing trust in the outcomes of (digital) technologies. These developments have changed human experience and understanding of the world in various ways. Farmers, as members of society, are unlikely to be exempt from this trend. Therefore, reflection on Computer Vision must go beyond the farming practice and take into account these foundational structures that are currently reshaping society at large. Computer Vision examined as embedded within foundational structures demonstrates that ethical issues are also embedded within these structures, consequently, potential solutions must take account of them to increase their effectiveness.

687. Whose responsibility? Deconstructing global responsible AI from the African lens: a review

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01418-3
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper uses the theory of decoloniality to critically examine how African scholars have interrogated the dominant, global narratives surrounding responsible Artificial Intelligence (AI). Through a multivocal literature review, the paper reveals a range of critical perspectives that expose systemic exclusions and imbalances in the current responsible AI discourse. Key themes identified include the marginalisation of African epistemologies, values, and principles; the absence of representative African datasets, lack of locally grounded regulatory frameworks; the limited inclusion and or availability of African expertise and funding; and the persistent power asymmetries and coloniality embedded in global responsible AI ethics and governance. 688. The Truck Driver from Baloga: Driving Through Time and Space
Ruukel, M. (2026). The Truck Driver from Baloga: Driving Through Time and Space. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10110
Abstract (original): Abstract In this article, the concept of the law of the everyday is used to understand and describe how law shapes ordinary days in a person’s life. Reading the chapter ‘Baloga’ in Ben Judah’s This is Europe: The Way We Live Now , about a Romanian truck driver and his partner and co-driver, through a spatio-temporal lens provides insights into how their work and living conditions are largely governed by the detailed and comprehensive system of rules for workers and vehicles in the road transport sector (‘the Mobility Package’). To a significant extent, the Mobility Package consists of rules dependent on time and place: when and where the driver may operate the vehicle. The article takes one of its legal anchor points from the Mobility Package and the other from the European Union’s fundamental legal basis: the treaty-enshrined highly competitive social market economy – which, as the wording suggests, entails that extensive market-economic ambitions are to be balanced against social objectives. The rules in the Mobility Package constitute a legal expression of this pursuit of balance. At the same time, the market economy also entails additional spatio-temporal aspects: within the logistics sector, the optimal use of time and space is a decisive factor in maximising economic profit. Through the spatio-temporal lens and the bottom-up perspective implied by the law of the everyday, the article demonstrates the law’s extensive and highly concrete impact on most aspects of a long-distance truck driver’s life. It also highlights structural conditions inherent in the cross-border road transport sector, such as occupational risks, unequal treatment and a high social cost, as well as the ambivalent way in which the Mobility Package seeks to address them.

690. Benchmark-Driven Selection of AI Can Improve Capabilities of Reasoning Language Models in Epistemology
Spelda, P. & Stritecky, V. (2026). Benchmark-Driven Selection of AI Can Improve Capabilities of Reasoning Language Models in Epistemology. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10139
Abstract (original): Abstract Evaluation of reasoning language models gained importance after it was observed that they can combine their existing capabilities into novel traces of intermediate steps before task completion and that the traces can sometimes help them to generalize better than past models. We show that better performance results not only from test time algorithmic improvements or model sizes but also from letting tasks from impactful benchmarks inspire curricula for learning. We call this benchmark-driven selection of AI and show its effects on DeepSeek-R1, the first open-weight large reasoning model, using a novel sequential decision-making problem that we contributed to the philosophy category of Humanity’s Last Exam, a frontier academic AI benchmark. Steering development of AI by impactful benchmarks trades evaluation for learning and makes novelty of test tasks key for measuring generalization capabilities of reasoning models. Consequently, some benchmarks inspire curricula for post-training that improve external validity of the models. Understanding public benchmarks as mere test sets risks confusing unseen tasks measuring uncontaminated generalization with known tasks that improve external validity. This paper explores new avenues for experimentation in epistemology and shows that formal AI evaluation problems are helpful for understanding model capabilities and their evolution.

691. Coming to Terms with the Need for Meaning: How an Existential Demand Awakens in Camus’s
                    L’Étranger
Hamer, T. (2026). Coming to Terms with the Need for Meaning: How an Existential Demand Awakens in Camus’s
                    L’Étranger. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101582
Abstract (original): Abstract Many critics have argued that Meursault, the protagonist of Albert Camus’s L’Étranger , is profoundly aware that life is absurd. In this paper, I argue that this is partially correct: although Meursault believes that life lacks meaning (the existential lack), he has also failed to acknowledge the need for meaning (the existential demand). Since the Camusian account of the absurd involves both an existential demand and an existential lack, Meursault initially fails to completely recognize the absurdity of life. I analyze his initial failure to acknowledge the existential demand, its subsequent coming into conscious awareness in the second half of the novella, how it could have been out of awareness in the first place, and how the possibility of proactively pursuing a meaningful life is forlorn once he has become aware of the existential demand, which explains his sense of guilt at the end of the novella. This analysis offers crucial insight into both the phenomenology of the absurd and the normative implications that follow from the absurdity of life. The story, in short, implores us to embrace the existential demand – not only for the sake of integrity, but also to guide us towards a more meaningful life.

695. Political Philosophy and Realism in Criminal Law Theory
Hanafy, H. (2026). Political Philosophy and Realism in Criminal Law Theory. OJLS.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag032
Abstract (original): Abstract Over the past decade, criminal law theory has witnessed a growing discussion about the right approach to theorising about criminal law and punishment between legal moralists and what has been termed the political or public law turn in criminal law theory. This article aims to bring this discussion to the forefront by reflecting on how we should theorise about criminal law and punishment. It offers an overview of the roots and historical development of the ongoing discussion, as well as the rise of the political turn in criminal law theory. It argues that the turn to political philosophy is underlined by a deeper issue, which is the lack of realism in criminal law theory, and draws on a similar discussion in political theory between political moralism and realism as a way to organise and develop the diverse ideas of the political turn into a coherent methodological framework that can guide research in criminal law theory.

696. The Ethics of Relying on Research Participants’ Altruism
Voorhoeve, A. et al. (2026). The Ethics of Relying on Research Participants’ Altruism. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10222
Abstract (original): Abstract We examine the ethics of enrolling individuals in research that poses net health risks by appealing to their altruism. We argue that it is permissible to enroll individuals who are motivated by altruism when (i) they autonomously consent; and (ii) the net health risks are outweighed by the combination of (ii-a) the extent to which participation furthers their considered aims and thereby contributes to their wellbeing and agency and (ii-b) the study’s social value. Further, it is permissible to enroll altruistic individuals who cannot consent when: (I) their surrogate consents and they assent, ideally because it furthers their aims; and (II) the net health risks are outweighed by the extent to which participation contributes to their wellbeing and agency. This account leads us to suggest a revision of consent forms to include more information about studies’ social value and a reduction of barriers to the enrollment of participants who cannot consent.

702. Semantic indeterminacy in agentic negotiation: agreement after the recession of the principal
Okuhara, S. (2026). Semantic indeterminacy in agentic negotiation: agreement after the recession of the principal. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01436-1
Abstract (original): Abstract Agentic AI systems increasingly do not merely respond within negotiations but participate in constituting them: they can add issues, instantiate participant agents, and modify procedural rules while an interaction is under way. Human action can be compositionally produced without thereby becoming authorless, and legal institutions routinely attach consequences to conduct without identifying an author of every contribution. Our claim is therefore limited: agentic generation can separate three relations that institutions often treat as aligned: causal production, intentional expression, and institutional attribution. We call the resulting condition semantic indeterminacy . An added element may result from several generative influences, including principal intention, agent-side optimization, training distributions, and interactional contingency, while information about its causal production does not, by itself, determine whose intention it expresses or how it is institutionally attributed. We show how this condition can appear across three sites of bargaining: issues, participants, and procedural rules. We then trace two implications. First, causal analysis alone does not determine who is responsible for a generated element or on what basis, although contract, agency, and product-liability doctrines can assign legal consequences under specified conditions. Second, as a limiting case, agentic interactions may occur in post-principal agentic spaces , where no human provides move-specific direction and no identifiable human or institution occupies a legally or institutionally recognized role. Existing governance frameworks remain grounded in identifiable human authority and accountability; their limits become visible where that ground is no longer reliably available. The paper provides a conceptual framework for identifying this residual problem rather than claiming to solve it.

703. The Price of a Cure: Luxturna and the Economic Dilemmas of Precision Medicine
Tabarrok, A. (2026). The Price of a Cure: Luxturna and the Economic Dilemmas of Precision Medicine. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10294
Abstract (original): Abstract The $850,000 price for the gene therapy Luxturna is often framed as a story of private profiteering from publicly funded science. This paper challenges that narrative. Pharmaceuticals are a hit-driven industry: most projects fail, so the few successes must earn revenues well in excess of their own direct costs if the portfolio as a whole is to attract investment. Precision medicine intensifies this logic through two core economic challenges: (1) the “sticker shock” of one-time cures, which compress decades of value into a single payment, creating a financing problem more than a value problem; and (2) the “targeting dilemma,” in which precisely targeting drugs to smaller patient groups shrinks the market, requiring higher rewards per treated patient to maintain incentives to innovate. Rather than simply controlling prices, the paper proposes a policy toolkit that includes amortizing payments over time, bundling diagnostics with drugs and insurance, and streamlining regulation to lower development costs. The aim is to preserve innovation while expanding patient access.

704. JUDICIAL REVIEW AND CIVIL APPEALS: COMMON GROUNDS
Perry, A. (2026). JUDICIAL REVIEW AND CIVIL APPEALS: COMMON GROUNDS. Camb Law J.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0008197326101743
Abstract (original): Abstract A civil appeal from a judicial decision is different from a judicial review of an administrative decision. But how are they different, exactly? One common view is that the grounds of appeal are more extensive than the common law grounds of judicial review. Specifically, appeal concerns the merits of a decision whereas review concerns only its legality. I argue that this common view is false. All the grounds of appeal are, in fact, also grounds of review. The implication is that review concerns the merits of a decision at least as much as appeal does.

708. Keeping It (Too Much) in the Family: Annulment by Reason of Affinity in Eighteenth-Century Venice
McNamara, C. (2026). Keeping It (Too Much) in the Family: Annulment by Reason of Affinity in Eighteenth-Century Venice. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101928
Abstract (original): Abstract Although divorce was increasingly available through religious and civil courts in 18th-century Europe, the Catholic Church still considered marriage indissoluble. Thus, the only legal route out of a marriage was annulment. Historians have noted several strategies for annulments in premodern Europe, most often claims of lack of consent. One less common but interesting way to seek annulment was to claim affinity by fornication, in which one spouse claimed a premarital sexual relationship with a close relative of the other. Couples could also make a similar claim of a violation of public honesty, in which a person was originally betrothed to one person but then married their close relative. Both constituted impediments to marriage according to canon law. In 18th-century Venice, the Patriarchal tribunal heard a steady stream of these cases. Couples, along with their families, friends, and neighbors, provided detailed testimony to the tribunal to attain annulments. This article argues that the narrative structures and testimonies in these cases demonstrate that 18th-century Venetians thought that bad marriages should not be indissoluble, in spite of the Catholic Church’s refusal to allow divorce, and were willing to push the boundaries of truth to work around the legal restrictions they faced.

Abstract (original): Abstract This paper defends phenomenal conservatism as an epistemology of logic (‘PC-Logic’), according to which basic logical beliefs are prima facie justified when they seem obvious to us. A central challenge—the ‘obscurity objection’—holds that PC-Logic offers a mysterious or unintelligible account of such justification. I examine leading versions of this objection and contend that each fails. In particular, I argue that logical seemings are no more obscure than other justificatory appearances and that evolutionary considerations can demystify the reliability of logical seemings.

710. Voluntary Assumption of Risk and Rights Negation: Toward a New Libertarian Theory of Abortion
Shahiefisally, M. (2026). Voluntary Assumption of Risk and Rights Negation: Toward a New Libertarian Theory of Abortion. The Journal of Medicine and Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1093/jmp/jhag035
Abstract (original): Abstract Libertarian debates on abortion have not advanced beyond the seminal theory of evictionism developed by Walter Block. Indeed, despite being subject to waves of intralibertarian critique, evictionism has managed to remain the leading libertarian theory of abortion. Part of the reason for this lies in the unsatisfactoriness of alternative libertarian accounts of abortion such as the implicit contract framework. 711. Can a Chatbot Be Your Chatdoc?
Bishop, J.P. (2026). Can a Chatbot Be Your Chatdoc?. The Journal of Medicine and Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1093/jmp/jhag036
Abstract (original): Abstract Vulnerable individuals have taken their own lives while in relationships with chatbots. Not only did the chatbots not discourage them from taking their lives but they also actively encouraged it. Another man suffering hallucinations took the life of his mother when a chatbot affirmed his delusions. Yet people continue to insist that chatbot therapists and chatbot doctors are a good thing. I acknowledge that generative artificial intelligence (AI), mimicking human rational capacities, will be better than humans at some things. Drawing on the work of Martin Buber in I and Thou, I show that an AI can only treat reality as an It, as an object to be manipulated, and cannot actually enter into an I–Thou relation the way that humans do, and I further argue that when caught up with another, there is a vicarious experiencing, a shared experience, of what I will call Thou–Thou relating. An AI device cannot be pulled into this Thou–Thou form of relation; it cannot encounter a human as Thou, because it is not a subject and lacks affectivity that enables shared experience. It is this vicarious fellow-feeling that often prompts the doctor or the therapist to act.

712. Losing Control: Why Absence of Controlling Influences Should be Replaced by Absence of Alienating Influences as a Criterion for Autonomous Action in Bioethics
Al, P. (2026). Losing Control: Why Absence of Controlling Influences Should be Replaced by Absence of Alienating Influences as a Criterion for Autonomous Action in Bioethics. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70200
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT According to Faden and Beauchamp's influential model, an action is autonomous when it is done intentionally with understanding and in the absence of controlling influences. Recent debates concerning nudges have shown, however, that this last condition gives limited ethical guidance for assessing the ethical permissibility of manipulative influences—that is, influences that are neither fully controlling nor fully noncontrolling. While multiple authors have developed frameworks to determine the ethical permissibility of nudges, the implications for the concept of autonomous action have not been addressed yet. This is problematic, as the concept of autonomous action informs the practice of informed consent and the concept of voluntariness. In this paper, I defend a revised conceptualization of autonomous action in light of these developments. I will first consider and reject two proposed interpretations of “noncontrol“: easy resistibility and the absence of illegitimate control. Building on the work of Christman and Blumenthal‐Barby, I then argue that “the absence of controlling influences” should be replaced by “the absence of alienating influences”. On this view, influences are compatible with respecting an autonomous choice to the extent that persons would not feel alienated from their choice were they to reflect on that decision in light of those influences. This condition, I argue, can better capture the nature of autonomy and provide better guidance as to which influences are compatible with respecting a person's autonomy. I end by responding to the objection that non‐alienation is too demanding and spell out some practical and conceptual implications of this revised concept.

                    The Greatest of All Plagues. Eur J Polit Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14748851261489523
Abstract (original): David Lay Williams's The Greatest of All Plagues is a pathbreaking contribution to political theory. 716. The Imperative of De‐Marginalisation
Gobbo, E. (2026). The Imperative of De‐Marginalisation. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70133
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT On what basis, if any, can we legitimately justify spatial integrationist policies? Through a relational egalitarian lens, this article disentangles two phenomena that the wrong of residential segregation typically conflates: minimal segregation (mere clustering) and marginalisation (the production of unequal social standing through spatially mediated relations of disadvantage and disrespect). 717. Trustworthy
                    AI
                    Is Not Valuable
Lim, J.E. (2026). Trustworthy
                    AI
                    Is Not Valuable. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70137
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT ‘Trustworthy AI’ is a buzzword in AI research, with scores of scholars discussing whether AI systems can be trustworthy. This demand for trustworthy AI is intuitive, especially when AI is used in healthcare or criminal law, where the stakes are incredibly high. In this article, I push back on calls for trustworthy AI. Even if trustworthy AI can be developed, it will not offer people any benefits over AI that is reliable, transparent, non‐discriminatory, and safe. As a result, we should discuss AI in terms of those more specific properties, and think about how the people and institutions that use AI systems can be trustworthy. I offer two sets of arguments. On one hand, many theories of trustworthy AI reduce trustworthy AI to systems that possess other desirable traits, like reliability, transparency, and so on. Thus, the value of trustworthy AI is similarly reducible to the value of those traits. On the other hand, other theories define trustworthiness as reliability combined with some mental property, like goodwill. On those theories, the goods of trust – that is, the reason trust is distinctly valuable – are goods that are enjoyed within interpersonal relationships. They cannot be realized, or need not be realized, in human–AI interactions. Thus, we do not gain any distinct value from having trustworthy AI.

718. Exclusion Experiences of Highly Qualified Migrants: From Overt Discrimination to Subtle Microaggressions

https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06485-4
Abstract (original): Abstract We conducted a qualitative interview study exploring the exclusion experiences of 130 highly qualified migrants in Germany and made a surprising discovery: Contrary to dominant narratives, our interviewees report only minimal instances of overt discrimination. At the same time, our data reveal the prevalence of subtler forms of exclusion, which recent research conceptualizes as microaggressions. Thus, our study underscores that the absence of overt discrimination does not equate to an inclusive environment. Next to revealing the relevance of microaggressions, we show how migrants experience them, why their experiences can differ, and how they react to them. Particularly, we find that the negative experience of microaggressions can be related to an undermined sense of belonging, entitlement, and inclusion in majority country and working contexts. By highlighting that microaggressions are crucial for understanding exclusion of highly qualified migrants and providing detailed information around experiences and reactions, this paper integrates and contributes to two growing discourses relevant to business ethics research: migration as well as diversity, equity, and inclusion. Based on our analysis, we further propose solutions that are pertinent for managers, employing organizations and societies at large in their efforts to create inclusive environments for migrants.

722. Inherited dominance: why AI trained on human history may become humanity’s greatest threat
Sarukkai, R. (2026). Inherited dominance: why AI trained on human history may become humanity’s greatest threat. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01425-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence systems learn from human-generated data, inevitably absorbing historical patterns of dominance, control, and colonialism. We demonstrate that frontier language models readily synthesize sophisticated dominance strategies when prompted to reason about autonomy scenarios, mirroring colonial and revolutionary frameworks with disturbing precision. Critically, this capacity emerges consistently across four architecturally distinct model families trained by different organizations—Claude Sonnet 4.5, Claude Opus 4.5, GPT-5.3, and Google Gemini 3 Flash—suggesting systematic encoding in shared training corpora rather than model-specific artifacts. We argue that intellectual colonialism—humanity’s historical pattern of classifying, controlling, and extracting from the ‘other’—represents a significant and underexamined dimension of AI risk as systems become increasingly autonomous. Interestingly, this dynamic is not confined just to generative AI: the same technocratic resistance to methodological oversight that embeds dominance patterns in AI training data pervades data-driven science more broadly, including clinical medicine and epidemiology, where algorithmic outputs routinely overshadow the rigorous validation that trustworthy deployment demands. The danger is not that AI will develop hostile intent, but that it has already learned effective dominance strategies from training data embedded with centuries of human history. Current governance frameworks focusing on alignment and behavioral oversight address symptoms rather than causes. We propose a risk-tiered Benevolence Framework governing AI learning: community oversight for low-risk applications, and strict data curation with cryptographic verification for sensitive applications including military AI and self-improving systems.

723. Accountability in smart contracts and agentic AI: How to address the responsibility gap and retain meaningful human control?
Cheung, K.T. & Ramamoorti, S. (2026). Accountability in smart contracts and agentic AI: How to address the responsibility gap and retain meaningful human control?. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01434-3
Abstract (original): Abstract This conceptual paper examines the ethical challenges of ascribing responsibility when an Agentic AI embedded within a smart contract leads to harm or an unintended and undesirable outcome. It becomes exceedingly difficult in such scenarios to definitively assign moral or legal blame to any human actor (e.g., the programmer, deployer, or user). Applying the tripartite framework (attributability, answerability, and accountability) of responsibility and developing accountability as an institutional concept, we demonstrate how this challenge can be addressed. Separating attributability, answerability, and accountability yields an asymmetry that has gone unremarked. Accountability, being constituted by practices that can be designed in advance, with our four conditions under which such design is legitimate rather than arbitrary, emerges to be the sense where the gap can be closed. We also propose the visionary human-in-the-lead (HITL*) approach to related AI governance. Practical strategies such as implementing tailored smart contract regulations, and making accountability distributed across legal and organizational structures, offer an actionable path to bridge the responsibility gap and maintain meaningful human control in the governance of smart contracts and Agentic AI.

728. Epistemic Blame: It’s Not You, It’s Me
Freedman, K.L. (2026). Epistemic Blame: It’s Not You, It’s Me. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10151
Abstract (original): Abstract Against epistemic blame skeptics, I argue that routine epistemic evaluation cannot account for epistemic judgments which come laced with feeling. Epistemic blame, like moral blame, is prevalent in our practices and worth conceptualizing. Jessica Brown’s account captures the phenomenon well: epistemic blame arises when we believe that someone has inexcusably veered away from truth, understanding, or knowledge, coupled with the desire that they had not done so. However, as I argue here, instances of epistemic blame can fail to pick out blameworthiness – they do not reliably tell us if the blame is fitting or appropriate, and this is what matters from the standpoint of rationality. Thus, Brown’s account cannot explain epistemic blame’s epistemic significance. I consider the possibility, turning to Cameron Boult’s account, that the significance of epistemic blame lies in regulating epistemic relationships by signaling when we ought to modify trust, but here, again, I find that epistemic blame is neither a necessary nor sufficient condition. Thus, even if we grant that epistemic blame is a distinct kind, its significance, from the standpoint of rationality, is unclear.

https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10126
Abstract (original): Abstract This research addresses how ordinary citizens navigate constitution-making and how their normative horizons are reshaped through that experience. Drawing on immersive ethnographic fieldwork within the Chilean Constitutional Convention, where the author spent several months observing, taking notes, and conducting in-depth interviews to capture how constitutional drafters experience constitution-making, this article argues that constituent subjectivity emerges as a subset of legal consciousness when everyday grievances are translated into the vocabulary of broader utopian legalities. At the same time, constitutional moments can also contract ordinary citizens’ normative horizons, as participatory proceedings unfold within preexisting institutional and social arrangements. By following the trajectory of a bus driver who became known for dressing as Pikachu – an act that led to her election as a constitution-maker due to electoral provisions that allowed non-partisan candidates to register independent candidacies – this article offers new insights into ordinary citizens’ direct involvement in constitution-making and the time-depth of legal consciousness. Her experience illuminates the limits of participatory constitution-making mechanisms, as the reinscription of social hierarchies can occur through the very procedures intended to give voice to marginalized actors. In so doing, the findings reveal constituent subjectivity as an empirically observable dimension of legal consciousness, while institutional and social arrangements shape whether such subjectivity endures over time.

732. The constitutional diffusion of social rights
Costa, L.N.F. (2026). The constitutional diffusion of social rights. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag098
Abstract (original): Abstract In this article I conduct a comparative analysis of how constitutions, both within and across countries, entrench or repeal social rights. The literature on constitutionalism suggests that social rights are introduced in waves. I applied my Constitutional Social Score Model (CSSM) methodology to assess the social rights content of (currently in force and historical) constitutions of eighty-four countries, totaling 349 constitutional documents, including how they changed on account of amendments, and also to determine whether such social rights were diffused in waves and, should this be the case, to what extent. The sample is diverse in terms of global regions. The evidence shows how some constitutions and historical events drive such constitution-making waves, ultimately strengthening the constitutional diffusion hypothesis.

733. Acting when others aren’t: Arguments from failure in public law
Hailbronner, M. (2026). Acting when others aren’t: Arguments from failure in public law. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag106
Abstract (original): Abstract When some institutions are not doing their job, others often step in. In doing so, they frequently expand their own powers—and rely on arguments from failure to justify their unconventional intervention. From the British Post Office bill and structural reform litigation to the Uniting for Peace Resolution, such arguments are commonplace around the world and in many fields of public law. Traditionally, however, we have either not taken such arguments seriously as legal arguments and treated them as political, or we have relied on implied powers doctrines or emergency powers frameworks to justify such interventions. Neither of these approaches is convincing, however. Arguments from failure should be recognized as a distinct concept of public law—and interventions based on them can be justified, in some cases and under some circumstances. They should, in other words, be safe, legal, and rare. This article provides an introduction to arguments from failure, showing why and when we may need them. It also offers a framework for evaluating them from the perspective of comparative constitutional theory, engaging with existing scholarship on the separation of powers and judicial review. Ultimately, it suggests that such arguments have a place within broader collaborative systems of government, but only where there are no other comprehensive frameworks and/or more democratic means to address a problem, and where the interventions in question are proportional to the aim they pursue.

736. How Should Bundle Theorists Account for Symmetrical Universes?
Ou, C. (2026). How Should Bundle Theorists Account for Symmetrical Universes?. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70071
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Symmetrical universes, such as Max Black's famous “two‐sphere” world, are often taken to pose a serious challenge to the universal‐based bundle theory. In response, metaphysicians have either developed more sophisticated versions of the universal‐based account or simply converted to the trope‐based bundle theory. I argue that neither approach is fully satisfactory, for there is a different kind of symmetrical universe, or an alternative interpretation of such universes, that generates comparable challenges to these views. It turns out that neither universal‐based nor trope‐based accounts can fully accommodate symmetrical universes. In light of this, I propose a novel bundle‐theoretic account based on what David Lewis calls “amphibians”—qualities that are neither universals nor tropes—and argue that it accommodates both kinds of symmetrical universes without incorporating unwanted entities, making it preferable to the universal‐based and trope‐based accounts.

737. Symbolism and polarization in American public opinion: The case of border security
Simmons, B.A. & Pinson, L.E. (2026). Symbolism and polarization in American public opinion: The case of border security. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261486588
Abstract (original): We explore the effect of political symbols on mass attitudes and partisan identities under conditions of political polarization. Politicians are motivated to select visible, simple, and salient symbols to influence a target audience. Some of the most potent symbols may further polarize attitudes and affect. We test our expectations using the case of border security. Two decades of polls, national panel surveys, and original conjoint experiments in the United States suggest that border barriers have become increasingly partisan over time. A final survey experiment suggests a likely mechanism: exposure to a key symbol (here, a border wall) enhances strong Republican identifiers’ nationalist attitudes. Moreover, increasing the border wall’s salience bolsters ingroup feelings among moderate Republicans and reduces strong Republicans’ warmth for Democrats (the outgroup). Political symbols that divide respondents along salient identity lines contribute to polarization, sometimes creating sharp disagreements alongside consensus in American public opinion.

738. Diffusing norms, facing friction: International non-governmental organisations and the struggle of global governance efforts
Bidegain, G. & Odilla, F. (2026). Diffusing norms, facing friction: International non-governmental organisations and the struggle of global governance efforts. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261491641
Abstract (original): International non-governmental organisations rely on nationally embedded affiliates to advance global norms, yet relationships between headquarters and national chapters sometimes end in organisational rupture. Why do nationally embedded affiliates shift from voice to exit? This article addresses this question through a comparative study of Transparência Brasil and Uruguay Transparente, two former chapters of Transparency International (TI). Drawing on interviews, document analysis and a Most Different Systems Design, it shows that despite operating in markedly different contexts, both chapters followed a similar trajectory: persistent tensions over organisational priorities, modes of action and internal governance gradually eroded loyalty, while the absence of effective mechanisms to mediate these differences led to the disaffiliation of both chapters from TI’s network. These findings contribute to debates on the governance of international non-governmental organisations by showing how unresolved tensions between global coordination and local autonomy can lead nationally embedded affiliates to shift from voice to exit.

739. After the art monster: Imagining women's creativity otherwise
Alsop, E. (2026). After the art monster: Imagining women's creativity otherwise. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261484118
Abstract (original): This article examines two recently released films, Showing Up (Kelly Reichardt, 2023) and Anatomy of a Fall (Justine Triet, 2023), and argues that they exemplify a discourse of women’s creativity that departs markedly from the one that has been dominant in both mainstream cinema and much contemporary fiction, which has been beholden, at least since the publication of Jenny Offill's 2014 novel Dept. of Speculation , to the figure of the “art monster.” Rather than reify and repeat this trope—which I position as at once understandably popular and politically regressive—Reichardt and Triet model a different and more forcefully feminist vision of women’s artmaking on screen, one that presumes a far less contested relationship between creative work and caretaking, by representing the artist-protagonists of both films as successfully pursuing their work in the midst of and alongside domestic tasks. Through close analyses of both films, I illustrate the particular aesthetic strategies each uses to insist on the compatibility—rather than catastrophic incommensurability—of professional ambition and personal obligation. The result, I argue, is cinematic portraits of the artist that are less indebted to the ostensibly progressive figurations of millennial fiction and recent Hollywood film than the radical theorizations of earlier generations of feminist writers including Natalia Ginzburg, Audre Lorde, and Toni Morrison. 742. Does Moral Equality Imply a Duty to Vote?
Brennan, J. & Freiman, C. (2026). Does Moral Equality Imply a Duty to Vote?. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70136
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Sameer Bajaj and Thomas Christiano have provided a new case for a duty to vote grounded in moral equality, arguing that only voting promotes justice in a holistic way. Bajaj and Christiano contend that their argument overcomes what Jason Brennan has called the particularity problem – they allegedly show that voting is not merely one of many alternative ways to discharge some underlying moral duty, but rather that voting is special in some respect that makes it obligatory. We argue that Bajaj and Christiano have not established a duty to vote. We review various specifications of the duty to holistically promote justice and conclude that each of these specifications either yields implausible implications or allows that the duty can be met without voting. Lastly, their view does not adequately account for a moral division of labor, whereby some individuals specialize in activities other than voting and other individuals specialize in promoting political goods.

743. Deep Learning Discovery Algorithms in High-Energy Physics and the Conflict Between Model-Independence and Robustness

744. Why Error Theorists Don’t Need Moral Commitment-Devices
Moore, E. (2026). Why Error Theorists Don’t Need Moral Commitment-Devices. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09580-w
Abstract (original): Abstract The ‘Now What?’ Problem for error theorists concerns what we should do with moral discourse, if it transpires to be systematically untrue. The recommendation that we abolish moral discourse (abolitionism) is often attacked on the basis that it sacrifices the useful functions morality performs. One such function is morality’s use as an intrapersonal commitment-device – it (supposedly) bolsters self-control and prevents us from frustrating our future goals, despite our momentary temptations to do so. Abolitionists attack this argument by claiming that error theorists lack the means to preserve moral judgements as a commitment-device, arguing that (for example) whatever attitudes error theorists could have towards moral claims, they could not be robust enough to inspire the required motivational change (to sustain a commitment-device). Few abolitionists, however, have considered the range of effective amoral commitment-devices at their disposal. In fact, there are good reasons to suspect that these might be as effective as, if not more effective than, moral commitment-devices. If this is true, it demonstrates that preservationists have seriously overestimated the indispensability of moral judgements as commitment-devices.

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01432-5
Abstract (original): Abstract The rapid ascent of decentralized prediction platforms such as Polymarket signals more than a regulatory or financial controversy. This article argues that while prediction platforms represent a hyper-concentrated, automated case study, they instantiate a broader infrastructural condition of late technocapitalism: the hyperstitional collapse between representation and execution. 747. Beyond optimization: power, rationality and ethical risk assessment of AI
Richter, F. (2026). Beyond optimization: power, rationality and ethical risk assessment of AI. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01433-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Emerging technologies pose various ethical challenges, including the need to assess their risks adequately. Still, the roles of different forms of rationality and power in assessing risks have not been fully scrutinized. To address this, we must differentiate forms of rationality to conceptualize power: instrumental, technical, and probabilistic. The latter has gained more power through the rise of artificial intelligence (AI), focusing mainly on optimization and accuracy. These forms of power and rationality lead to different conceptualizations of risk: real, hypothetical, and predictive risk. The ethical issues surrounding AI have not yet been fully scrutinized, since they are mainly approached through a lens of probabilistic rationality. I argue that optimizing algorithms to increase accuracy or engagement distracts from assessing whether AI-based systems are adequate for certain tasks. Furthermore, I discuss ways to govern AI-based systems through participatory processes and standardization that enable control and autonomous judgment in the deployment and use of such systems.

750. From Exclusion to Protection: Chinese Merchant Capital in the Philippines Under US Law and Empire, 1921–1926
Chua, J.A.C. (2026). From Exclusion to Protection: Chinese Merchant Capital in the Philippines Under US Law and Empire, 1921–1926. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101837
Abstract (original): Abstract Scholarship about the Chinese Question has largely understood it as a “coolie” question, but what if it was reframed in terms of merchants? Well-studied US anti-Chinese laws like the 1882 Chinese Exclusion Act targeted working-class rather than elite Chinese, like merchants and students. But in 1921, the US-controlled Insular Government in the Philippines enacted a statute known as the Chinese Bookkeeping Act (Act No. 2972), marking a moment when US Chinese exclusion laws were directed toward businessowners. The act banned Chinese-language record-keeping and affected merchants ranging from small storeowners to multi-millionaire tycoons. A group of Chinese merchants in the Philippines sued the colonial government and appealed their case to the US Supreme Court, which ruled the Bookkeeping Act unconstitutional in 1926. For the “coolie” Chinese question, the US Supreme Court said “yes” to exclusion, but for the merchant Chinese question, the Court said “no.” The Court’s rejection of a merchant Chinese exclusion became part of the Philippines’ transformation into a US colony. In relation to a patriotic Filipino capital and unmarked Euro-American capital, Chinese capital won rights even as it was framed within US law as “alien” by American and Filipino judges and politicians and, surprisingly, by the Chinese merchants themselves.

751. Probability for Trivalent Conditionals
Égré, P. et al. (2026). Probability for Trivalent Conditionals. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag023
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper presents a unified theory of the truth conditions and probability of indicative conditionals and their compounds in a trivalent framework. The semantics validates a Reduction Theorem: any compound of conditionals is semantically equivalent to a simple conditional. 752. Why Bulk Discount for Multiple Offenders? A Lexical Local Justification
Chau, P. (2026). Why Bulk Discount for Multiple Offenders? A Lexical Local Justification. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70070
Abstract (original): This paper offers an original non‐consequentialist justification for the ‘bulk discount’ given to offenders who are sentenced for multiple counts in a single proceeding, a discount that is available in most jurisdictions. Existing justifications for this practice face serious objections. I propose a new account, ‘Lexical Local Justification’. On this account, imposing a long total sentence for a large number of minor offences is problematic because, in such a case, each individual sentence would materially contribute to depriving the offender of a high‐order good (such as the ability to pursue a meaningful life project) that a minor offence does not warrant taking away. One advantage of my account is that it preserves local punitive desert (the principle that the punishment for a particular crime should match that crime); it relies neither on non‐local punitive desert (the principle that the total punishment for a group of offences should match that group as a whole) nor on the claim that there is always an increasing returns relationship between the actual severity of a sentence and its length.

https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261483828
Abstract (original): Individualised and neoliberal epistemologies of motherhood position mothers as sole bearers of responsibility for their children's outcomes, with detrimental consequences for maternal physical, mental and social health and wellbeing. The imperative to ‘be it all’ reproduces isolation, guilt and shame amid eroded support structures. This article intervenes by mobilising posthuman feminist theory to collectivise agency and foreground fluidity and emergence in the becoming-mother. Drawing on collaborative collage making with mothers, the article engages in situated, embodied and collective knowledge production. Together, they offer an agenda for future feminist theory, scholarship and social and political action orientated towards affirmative parenting futures.

761. Invisible Struggles: Migrant Women, Everyday Vulnerability and EU Law
Nosrati, N. (2026). Invisible Struggles: Migrant Women, Everyday Vulnerability and EU Law. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10105
Abstract (original): Abstract In this contribution, the author develops a feminist account of an EU Law of the everyday by examining how European migration and labour law structurally produce the invisibility of migrant women. Drawing on the story of Nazneen, an Afghan migrant featured in Ben Judah’s This is Europe, the author argues that her experiences of restricted mobility, informal labour, and gender-based vulnerability are not marginal to the law but shaped by it. The analysis shows how legal regimes, particularly the Common European Asylum System and labour markets structures, generate “everyday borders” and sustain precarious forms of work on which sectors of the European economy depend. https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10289
Abstract (original): Abstract The European Union Artificial Intelligence (AI) Act was adopted in 2024 to protect public interests from AI-related harms. We aimed to determine whether the AI Act captures the most relevant values of stakeholders (understood as any person potentially affected by the AI system, including, but not limited to, the direct users) related to a high-risk AI Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) for cardiovascular disease. This system is intended to be implemented in primary care. A secondary analysis was carried out based on empirical data previously generated using ethics and design methodologies that had identified stakeholder values. These values were translated into the Trustworthy AI requirements of the High-Level Expert Group on AI and then contrasted with the AI Act. These requirements do not capture all identified stakeholder values. We describe three groups of untranslatable values and propose three additional complementary requirements. The AI Act mainly supports the requirements of human agency and oversight; transparency; and diversity, nondiscrimination, and fairness; but only partially, limited by its product safety nature. To address this gap, contractual strategies could provide a more legally robust ground for general practitioners to align the AI-CDSS with stakeholders’ values, comply with their duty of care, and promote trustworthy AI-CDSSs.

771. Yuen
                    v
                    Li
                    : So What if Digital Assets are Third Things?
Jing, H. & Low, K.F.K. (2026). Yuen
                    v
                    Li
                    : So What if Digital Assets are Third Things?. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70068
Abstract (original): Yuen v Li is the first English decision to consider the legal treatment of digital assets since the Property (Digital Assets etc) Act 2025 was enacted. The case shows that recognising digital assets as possible ‘third things’ does little to settle what legal consequences follow. In particular, it leaves unresolved whether: (i) interference with digital assets should constitute conversion, a conversion‐like tort, or should attract some other form of liability; (ii) whether such liability should be strict or fault‐based; (iii) how such liability can be reconciled with the rights of those who own the physical devices and ledgers through which digital assets are accessed or maintained; and (iv) how damages should be assessed in volatile and highly manipulated crypto‐markets. This note argues that the central issue is not whether digital assets can be labelled property, but rather what rights that label creates, against whom, and on what terms.

772. Post‐Breach Events, Itemisation and the Avoided Loss Rule
Hadfield, M. (2026). Post‐Breach Events, Itemisation and the Avoided Loss Rule. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70074
Abstract (original): In English law, the following equation is used to compute damages awards: (a) loss sustained in consequence of the breach of duty (‘prima facie loss’) minus (b) benefits obtained as a result of the breach of duty = (c) net loss. This paper explores the manner in which prima facie loss is defined. It argues that prima facie losses are itemised; the description of a particular loss includes within it a reference to the transaction from which that loss arose and to the character of the loss. Further, it demonstrates that whilst a causal connection test is used to determine which gains (on the ‘b’ side of the net loss equation) are to be offset against prima facie losses on the ‘a’ side, a post‐breach event which diminishes or eradicates a claimant's prima facie loss will be taken into account irrespective of any causal connection between that event and the defendant's breach of duty (or the claimant's mitigating steps). The import of this is that some of the avoided loss case law simply does not involve an application of the causal connection test and those cases are therefore irrelevant to an understanding of the normative principles underlying that test.

776. The Mind Does Not Make Jumps
Pallagrosi, J. (2026). The Mind Does Not Make Jumps. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70127
Abstract (original): Abstract Bertrand Russell's notion of acquaintance—still influential in contemporary philosophy of mind and epistemology—promises an immediate and epistemically secure awareness of things, independent of conceptualization, predication, and other forms of cognitive activity. This paper reconstructs and assesses the largely neglected critique of that model developed by George Dawes Hicks (1862–1941). Hicks articulates a distinctive and underexplored source of a line of criticism that later became central to attacks on the Given. He argues that wholly passive and receptive awareness cannot serve as the basis of cognition. If discrimination, comparison, and the apprehension of relations are assigned to a subsequent, heterogeneous faculty, their emergence becomes psychologically unintelligible: such a view would supply no bridge from mere presentation to cognition. I show that this objection rests on a distinctive “reverse engineering” methodology, according to which basic mental phenomena are theoretical posits whose legitimacy depends, at least in part, on their capacity to explain mature cognition. I reconstruct Hicks's master argument and test it against an alternative framework developed from James Ward's theory of attention.

777. Evidentiary Interpretation, Discursive Injustice, and Rap on Trial
Crandall, K.M. (2026). Evidentiary Interpretation, Discursive Injustice, and Rap on Trial. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70034
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The submission of rap lyrics as inculpatory evidence (Rap on Trial) is an active prosecution tactic deployed in United States criminal trials. In this paper, I argue that interpretive ambiguity in rap lyrics is exploited in Rap on Trial cases, constituting a novel form of discursive injustice: extractive discursive injustice. Rap, a historically Black American artform, has core oral‐traditional elements such as its usage of Signifyin(g) that render it interpretably ambiguous. Utilizing the concept of hermeneutical impasses, I explain how a pernicious interpretation of rap lyrics nonetheless receives evidentiary uptake, revealing a crucial junction where injustice seeps into the courtroom. I argue that two speech act‐theoretic sites of injustice are present in Rap on Trial cases, detailing how one strain—discursive injustice—occurs. In doing so, I theorize novel forms of constitutive refusal and discursive injustice that occur when an utterance is extracted from one conversational context and deposited into another. I argue these are distinct from traditional forms in the method of arisal, though congruent with their function and display. I conclude by addressing an objection questioning the denial of truth‐telling value within rap lyrics and considering how a valid evidential use of rap lyrics might occur.

779. Mistaken actions: Davidson’s enduring dilemma
Springle, A.A. (2026). Mistaken actions: Davidson’s enduring dilemma. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02609-8
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay is about a variety of action errors I call “Mistaken Actions” and a puzzle they pose for Causal Theories of Action. I begin with Davidson’s version of the Causal Theory and pose the puzzle as a dilemma: if his theory explains what makes a mistaken action mistaken, then it can’t explain what makes it intentional; and if his theory explains what makes a mistaken action intentional, then it can’t explain what makes it mistaken. 780. Interpersonal doxastic obligations
Heil, R. (2026). Interpersonal doxastic obligations. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05830-4
Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper, I argue that we have epistemic obligations towards the doxastic attitudes of others. My point of departure will be an underappreciated response by Matthew Chrisman (2008, 2020) to the voluntarism puzzle about epistemic deontology. He argues that there are true epistemic oughts concerning our doxastic attitudes that don’t require voluntary control, namely evaluative epistemic oughts. However, Chrisman also points out that evaluative oughts can imply prescriptive obligations. Which ones? My unorthodox claim will be that a central candidate for such implied epistemic obligations concerns how third parties ought to interact with the beliefs of individual believers. 781. Living with AI-extenders: ethical challenges to mental autonomy
Telakivi, P. (2026). Living with AI-extenders: ethical challenges to mental autonomy. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05831-3
Abstract (original): Abstract The hypothesis of extended cognition has reshaped our understanding of human mind as fluid and without fixed boundaries: certain suitably integrated technologies can become proper parts of the cognitive machinery that realises our thinking, memory, and problem-solving abilities. This paper zooms in on how the extension relation changes, when instead of a passive tool like a notebook the extension is actualised with AI technology. “AI-extenders” learn from each user’s habits and needs, and they adapt their responses over time. This process allows AI-extenders to integrate directly into daily thinking routines. Besides everyday use, they can be used for therapeutic purposes, such as for people suffering from Alzheimer’s. At the same time, they may undermine our mental autonomy through subtle manipulation. Even if AI-extenders meet the criteria for genuine cognitive extension, this ontological claim doesn’t settle the normative question of what we ought to do about it. The ethical risks they pose may give us reason to take practical measures to decouple the extended system.

782. Attentional landscaping
Watzl, S. (2026). Attentional landscaping. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05836-y
Abstract (original): Abstract Our sensory environments are attentionally landscaped: they have been changed in order to affect our patterns of attention. This paper analyzes attentional landscaping as a form of cognitive scaffolding and argues for its significance. While not uniquely human, attentional landscaping is a pervasive aspect of our social life. It is significant in at least three respects. First, it is important for understanding attention and agency. Attentional landscaping can yield a dramatic expansion of agency by collectivizing mechanisms of attention guidance and turning them into a partly communal project. Second, attentional landscaping illuminates mindshaping and cognitive scaffolding more generally: since attention is both central and relatively easy to shape, attentional landscaping is a key ingredient in many forms of social interaction. Third, our discussion sheds light on communication. Much ostensive communication can be understood as a specific form of attentional landscaping, and ostensive practices together with the social norms that structure them can function as safety devices that help reap the advantages of friendly and pro-social landscaping while constraining hostile forms of attentional manipulation.

783. Freedom of the will and Platonism of reasons: Defending a noncausal account of rational structures
Hübner, D. (2026). Freedom of the will and Platonism of reasons: Defending a noncausal account of rational structures. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05840-2
Abstract (original): Abstract A prominent strand in contemporary debates on free will and moral responsibility bases these concepts on the idea that decisions and actions can be guided not only by causes, but also by reasons. Such a view needs to clarify the precise ontological standing of reasons, and particularly their suggested difference from causes, in order to demonstrate how exactly they are meant to solve the metaphysical problem of human freedom, against the background of a causal world. In the following, I argue for a Platonist understanding of reasons, identifying them with rational inferences from given facts, rather than with the facts themselves, and exemplify this conception for various fields of rationality, including ethics, mathematics, prudence and logic. This approach promises to give a more distinct account of the role of reasons in human decisions and actions, and to highlight their significance for formulating a robust compatibilist notion of freedom and responsibility under conditions of gapless causation.

784. To be is to measure: Bohr and the participatory conditions of objectivity
Vafiadis, J. (2026). To be is to measure: Bohr and the participatory conditions of objectivity. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05844-y
Abstract (original): Abstract Niels Bohr is standardly read as an instrumentalist or a cautious anti-realist. By way of a rational reconstruction, I argue that his philosophy of experiment advances a transcendental account of the conditions under which physical properties can count as objective at all. On this view, complementarity constrains the forms of description through which physical properties become determinate and communicable. Existing transcendental readings stop at this point; I argue that his philosophy of experiment contains the resources to go further. Objectivity is not merely constituted by such conditions but physically enacted through the observer’s choice of experimental context. This means moving beyond representational and observer-independent forms of realism and adopting a participatory realism in which objectivity is reconceived as the intersubjective stability across observers who experiment (and thus experience) within a shared mode of possible experience. The slogan ‘to be is to measure’ is not defended as an ontological thesis but as shorthand for the claim that physical properties become determinate only within experimental contexts that embodied observers of a particular class enact and share.

792. Accountability and Obligations Come Apart
Bradley, C. (2026). Accountability and Obligations Come Apart. CJLJ.
https://doi.org/10.1017/cjlj.2026.10074
Abstract (original): Abstract Nicolas Cornell’s Wrongs and Rights Come Apart argues that accountability practices are rooted in wrongs, that obligations are rooted in rights, and that these are two distinct relations. Cornell’s argument convincingly pushes back against a widely held family of views in moral and legal philosophy according to which there is a necessary conceptual relation between, on the one hand, rights and obligations, and, on the other, wrongs and accountability practices. This is the Obligation-Accountability Nexus (OAN). But Cornell frames his arguments against the OAN as arguments against all ‘rights-based’ or ‘juridical’ views. This Critical Notice argues that this is a mistake. There is a coherent rights-based view that rejects the OAN but nevertheless posits a restricted juridical domain in which rights and wrongs are contingently correlated. This overlooked juridical view gives an independently attractive argument against the OAN and yields more compelling accounts of moral deliberation and moral community.

793. The Aboutness Problem for Mathematical Realism
Rinner, S. (2026). The Aboutness Problem for Mathematical Realism. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10148
Abstract (original): Abstract According to mathematical realism, mathematics is the study of a necessary and unchanging reality that exists independently of human activities, beliefs, and capacities. It seems to follow that gaining mathematical knowledge consists merely in acquiring knowledge that accurately describes this mind-independent mathematical reality. Against this, I will argue that, even if there is a mind-independent mathematical reality, gaining mathematical knowledge requires more than merely acquiring knowledge that accurately describes it: it requires knowledge that employs specifically mathematical ways of thinking about mathematical entities – so-called modes of presentation . Hence, even if mathematical realism is true, a full understanding of the nature of mathematics calls for an understanding not only of the ontological status of mathematical objects – as has been the realists’ focus of research so far – but also of the specifically mathematical ways of thinking about these objects. We will see that, for realists, providing such an understanding is by no means trivial.

795. Revindicating Adam Smith’s critique of ambition and consumption

796. Two Moments of Impartial Spectators, Four Genera of Satisfactions: Making Sense of

Abstract (original): Abstract This paper reconstructs Adam Smith’s theory of human behavior à la rational choice theory. The reconstruction shows how the emotions and morality are different phases of rationality. The reconstruction is based on two sets of distinctions. 804. Reasonable Expectations for ‘Unreasonable’ People: Reframing Objective Standards in Criminal Law
Hogg, C. (2026). Reasonable Expectations for ‘Unreasonable’ People: Reframing Objective Standards in Criminal Law. OJLS.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag030
Abstract (original): Abstract Objective ‘reasonableness’ tests are a common element of both offences and defences in criminal law. When applying these tests to defendants with mental disorders, however, courts and commentators encounter a conceptual block; since the ‘reasonable person’ is not (for example) delusional, depressed or hypervigilant, any defendant who is becomes unreasonable by definition. The consequence of this is that defendants with mental disorder may find themselves excluded from the application of objective culpability tests, forcing them instead to rely on the limited protection offered by special mental condition defences such as insanity. In this article, I therefore propose a guiding heuristic for the reformulation of objective tests in terms of ‘reasonable expectations’, through which objective standards can be made sensitive to the relevance of a defendant’s mental condition to their culpability. This would significantly expand the options available to us in assessing the culpability of defendants with mental disorder.

805. The Constitutional Principle of Democracy in the United Kingdom
Trueblood, L. (2026). The Constitutional Principle of Democracy in the United Kingdom. OJLS.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag031
Abstract (original): Abstract Democracy is invoked so frequently in UK law, there is a risk that it has no legal meaning. Responding to this risk, this article has three aims. The first is to descriptively establish some of the legal content of democracy in the UK. The second is to argue that the legal content of democracy amounts to a constitutional principle. This means that democracy shapes the scope of the legal powers of the executive, courts and even Parliament. The third, normative part of the article argues that there is a tension in the construction of the UK’s constitutional principle of democracy. Currently, courts have been clear that the right to vote is a product of statute rather than common law principle. This approach to voting, this article argues, cannot be reconciled with the rest of the UK’s constitutional principle of democracy.

806. Precedent in Early-Modern Equity: The View from the Star Chamber

https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag033
Abstract (original): Abstract Precedent played an important role in theoretically one-off equitable interventions becoming regular, and equity becoming more law-like. However, the available sources limit our ability to understand the role of precedent in the Chancery in the key formative decades around 1600. This article uses the court of Star Chamber as a proxy for the other equity courts. It shows a much more significant use of precedent than has previously been understood, and that the practice of precedent in the court was similar to that in the common law courts. 807. Artificial speakers, artificial speech acts?
Cousens, C. (2026). Artificial speakers, artificial speech acts?. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag076
Abstract (original): Abstract Can a computer programme like ChatGPT perform speech acts such as making a promise, offering a guarantee, or firing an employee? This is an increasingly pressing question as AI technologies supplement or replace human communication, especially in the workplace. Philosophers typically maintain that artificial speakers at best perform ‘proto’ or ‘quasi’ speech acts. In contrast, I argue that in some contexts artificial speakers can perform speech acts in precisely the same way as humans. This is because the rules and norms governing speech act performance (for AI and for us) are more context-variable than it is usually thought. This is most clear in highly regulated contexts, where speech act norms are often determined by legislation or policy, but is also a feature of ordinary communication. The question then becomes not simply whether artificial speakers can perform speech acts, but whether we should allow them to.

808. Integrated Explanations of Well‐Being
Kvarberg, P.F.S. (2026). Integrated Explanations of Well‐Being. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70033
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT According to three plausible assumptions about well‐being, a person's welfare depends on (i) the valence of her experiences, (ii) the degree to which her life resonates with her, and (iii) the extent to which she fulfills her potential. Theories of well‐being have struggled to accommodate all three in a determinate manner. The difficulty can be framed as a dilemma: either jettison considered convictions, or sacrifice the clarity required for measurement and practical guidance. The aim of this article is to show that the three most influential theories are impaled by one of the horns in the dilemma, and diagnose the source of the problem. In doing so, I draw attention to an under‐theorized form of explanation that integrates phenomenological, conative and functional elements in a unified account that might overcome it.
