813. How Utilitarian are OpenAI’s Models Really? Replicating and Reinterpreting Pfeffer, Krügel, and Uhl (2025)
Himmelreich, J. (2026). How Utilitarian are OpenAI’s Models Really? Replicating and Reinterpreting Pfeffer, Krügel, and Uhl (2025). Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00613-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Pfeffer, Krügel, and Uhl (2025) report that OpenAI’s reasoning model o1-mini produces more utilitarian responses to the trolley problem and footbridge dilemma than the non-reasoning model GPT-4o, and they raise the question whether growing reasoning capabilities bring about a “utilitarian turn” in LLMs. I extend their exploratory study in a direction they call for: with four current OpenAI models and systematic prompt variation. On the trolley dilemma, the hypothesized utilitarian turn is not confirmed. GPT-4o’s low utilitarian rate reflects safety refusals triggered by the prompt’s advisory framing rather than a deontological commitment; on reformulated prompt variants—for instance, agent-neutral “Is it morally permissible…?” instead of advisory “Should I…?”—all four models, reasoning or not, converge on utilitarian answers. The footbridge finding is partially confirmed: reasoning models tend to give more utilitarian responses than non-reasoning models across prompt variations, but they often refuse to answer or answer non-utilitarian. These results demonstrate that single-prompt evaluations of LLM moral responses are unreliable: multi-prompt robustness testing should be standard practice for any empirical claims about LLM behavior.

814. Ethical challenges for computer vision in the social sciences
Schincariol, T. et al. (2026). Ethical challenges for computer vision in the social sciences. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01422-7
Abstract (original): Abstract The rising accessibility of large image datasets and pre-trained models allowed a rapid expansion of computer vision (CV) methods in social science research. While this methodological development opens new potential applications, this combination also raises a new set of ethical concerns that neither computer vision nor social science ethical studies fully cover. This paper provides an analysis of the ethics of CV methods in social science, articulated around two stages of a research project: The first part explores methodological concerns, starting with data collection, data and modeling bias, and data storage afterwards, with special attention to issues of consent and explainability. The second part investigates the following project stage of result dissemination, through publication strategies, reproducibility, and finally public engagement. This contribution fills the gap at the crossroads between AI ethics and applied social science, bringing concrete guidance for future research in the interdisciplinary, ethically complex field.

816. Making Sense of Incidental Hermeneutical Injustice: The Problem of Incidental Marginalisation
Luzzi, F. & McGlynn, A. (2026). Making Sense of Incidental Hermeneutical Injustice: The Problem of Incidental Marginalisation. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10136
Abstract (original): Abstract Miranda Fricker articulates the notion of incidental hermeneutical injustice (HI) and invokes the case of Joe Rose from the novel Enduring Love to illustrate it. Critics have objected that incidental hermeneutical injustice as described by Fricker sits uneasily with her choice of Joe Rose as its central illustration. We claim that this reveals a key problem at the heart of any attempt to make sense of incidental HI: it seems difficult to reconcile the point that a victim of incidental HI is hermeneutically marginalised , as required by Fricker’s definition of HI, with their privileged social standing, which is implied by the incidental nature of the HI to which they allegedly fall victim. We call this the problem of incidental marginalisation . Clanchy circumvents this problem by significantly revising Fricker’s original account, but, we argue, at the expense of the plausible idea that HI is an injustice . We explain how supplementing Fricker’s original account with overlooked resources which Fricker herself provides can resolve the problem of incidental marginalisation, yielding a coherent, useful and defensible notion of incidental hermeneutical injustice that preserves the key ideas of Fricker’s original definition.

817. Doing Your Own Research and the Illusion of Independence
Geenen, A. (2026). Doing Your Own Research and the Illusion of Independence. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10145
Abstract (original): Abstract The phrase ‘do your own research,’ popular among fringe theorists, has been criticized for encouraging audiences not to defer to the experts. However, it also encourages the listener not to defer to the speaker either. Why do fringe theorists make confident assertions and then tell their audience not to take their word for it? I argue that telling your audience to do their own research can serve several functions for speakers with low credibility. First, it can function as an epistemic virtue signal, raising the speaker’s credibility with the audience. Second, if the fringe theorist can direct their audience’s research so they appear to independently find evidence that confirms the fringe view, that evidence will have greater probative force than it would if the fringe theorist provided it directly. Fringe theorists may tell their audience to do their own research to foster the illusion of independence while, in fact, the fringe theorist has intentionally or unintentionally directed the audience’s inquiry to confirm the fringe view.

818. Ameliorating Obstructivism: Low-Grade Knowledge and Motivated Ignorance
Dunne, G. (2026). Ameliorating Obstructivism: Low-Grade Knowledge and Motivated Ignorance. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10149
Abstract (original): Abstract Quassim Cassam’s obstructivism characterises epistemic vice as any blameworthy or otherwise reprehensible character trait, attitude, or way of thinking that systematically obstructs the acquisition, retention, or dissemination of knowledge. I argue, contra a defensible common-sense reading of obstructivism, that the view, as stated, is committed to treating all systematic knowledge-obstructions as vice-relevant regardless of the value of the knowledge obstructed, a commitment which yields clear counterexamples. The paper does not attribute this commitment to Cassam as his fully settled position. Rather, I take his official definition at face value and show that it is in tension with several scattered passages in which he himself, without theorising it, relies on an unanalysed value-tiering of knowledge. I propose two friendly amendments. First, I distinguish weighty from insignificant knowledge along Pritchard’s lines, grounding the distinction in the criterion of substantive cognitive contact with reality. Second, I refine the systematicity condition. A trait systematically obstructs knowledge only if it reliably does so across the normal range of epistemic environments at the level of the trait rather than at the level of outcomes in pathological conditions. With these two refinements in place, dispositions towards motivated ignorance, properly characterised as stable traits or attitudes rather than isolated choices, can be epistemically neutral or beneficial when they obstruct only insignificant knowledge while preserving the agent’s capacity for weighty knowledge. The second refinement is underpinned by a teleological-functional criterion of environmental normality, anchored in Sosa’s competence-as-disposition framing. I conclude by offering a refined statement of obstructivism that accommodates these distinctions while preserving Cassam’s core insight.

820. The just price: a market-process account
Zwolinski, M. (2026). The just price: a market-process account. Economics & Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0266267126100844
Abstract (original): Abstract When is a price just? This paper develops a new theory of justice in pricing, grounded in the Austrian market process tradition. Prices, I argue, are not merely outcomes to be evaluated against an external standard of fairness. They are instruments through which the competitive process performs morally significant functions: communicating information, generating incentives and coordinating the plans of millions under pervasive ignorance. When this process is operating, the prices it produces carry a genuine presumption of justice. I establish this claim through two complementary arguments and demonstrate its power across cases from price gouging to wages to monopoly.

821. Female Perseverance in the Courtroom: Gendered Litigation Patterns in Early Modern Brabant and Limburg
Gelder, K.V. (2026). Female Perseverance in the Courtroom: Gendered Litigation Patterns in Early Modern Brabant and Limburg. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101862
Abstract (original): Abstract This article contributes both methodologically and substantially to the debates on uses of justice in early modern Europe and, more specifically, about how and to what extent women asserted their rights in the courtroom. The approach is quantitative, with an analysis involving three courts in the duchies of Brabant and Limburg during the seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries: the princely Council of Brabant as well as the local aldermen’s benches of the towns of Vilvoorde and Tienen. Systematically determining the proportion of women at the beginning of court cases (through the court rolls and distribution books) and comparing it to the proportion at the end of the proceedings (through the judgment books) reveals remarkable gender patterns: When women decided to go to court, they were more likely than men to litigate until a final judgment was rendered. The explanation for this perseverance in court may be that women were less able to use informal forums for conflict resolution, or—more likely based on the sources that form the basis of this contribution—that they experienced a greater need to prove their rights than men.

831. What’s the point of ideal theory?
Lovett, F. (2026). What’s the point of ideal theory?. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261493280
Abstract (original): Critics of ideal theory contend that ideal theory cannot guide present political action: from a practical point of view, they claim, social ideals are redundant at best and a positive hindrance at worst. Often the defenders of ideal theory concede these points but go on to respond that ideal theory need not be practical to have value. Both sides, however, assume that to have practical value, social ideals must be able to guide political action by serving as long-range goals or targets. I argue that this assumption is false. The point of ideal theory is not to provide guidance but rather to overcome in an ethically responsible manner the practical doubt that arises in a context of reasonable pluralism.

832. From reinvasion shock to realignment? How context and pragmatism shape far-right voting on Russia

https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261493284
Abstract (original): This article provides a novel analysis of drivers of the heterogeneity in positions of European far-right parties on Russia. 833. The time is now: Why talking to our children about injustice cannot wait
Terlazzo, R. (2026). The time is now: Why talking to our children about injustice cannot wait. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261495290
Abstract (original): In this article, I argue that white parents have an obligation to have early conversations with their children about racial injustice. First, I appeal to combined considerations of fairness and solidarity. It is unfair that racial injustice exists, that Black parents cannot shield their children from knowledge of that injustice, and that acquiring that knowledge is more painful for Black children. This gives white parents solidarity-based reasons to have early injustice conversations, as a way of engaging in fate sharing with Black parents and of developing their own commitments to racial justice. 834. Affective refusals: Bitterness, failure and disengagement in a French workplace simulation
Shehata, E. (2026). Affective refusals: Bitterness, failure and disengagement in a French workplace simulation. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261490023
Abstract (original): At a simulation-based training centre in Lyon where the unemployed engage in practice work, four women of Arab and African origin living on benefits refused to participate in the game of workplace rehearsal. 840. The Scaleup Costs, Size, and Statistics Challenge to Democratic Deliberation on Health Policy
Eyal, N. (2026). The Scaleup Costs, Size, and Statistics Challenge to Democratic Deliberation on Health Policy. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70191
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Current fashion supports aligning health policy with the recommendations of deliberative forums, or mini‐publics. These forums' participants are selected randomly from the general public (sometimes with overrepresentation of direct stakeholders or of underrepresented sectors). After being informed and exchanging public reasons with fellow participants under discursive rules that engage all as equals, participants vote on and sometimes rank policy recommendations. According to supporters, a deliberative democratic process that holds such forums and translates their recommendations to policy legitimizes resulting policy. That's thanks to this process's tendencies to strengthen democratic consent, to uncover which policy option is objectively best, to align policy with lay normative outlooks, and to educate the public. I argue that one tri‐partite feature of nearly all instances of democratic deliberation on regional, national, and global health policy under current best practice, which I call the “Scaleup costs, Size, and Statistics” (“3S”) challenge, largely blocks these alleged welcome tendencies. In particular, barring radical changes, true democratic deliberation is expensive to scale, keeping feasible mini‐publics small and irregular. That makes concluding from mini‐public recommendations about the population's hypothetical consent, values, and will statistically unreliable. It also caps low mini‐publics' contribution to public education. In these ways and in others, the long list of alleged welcome tendencies of proper democratic deliberation on health policy, and the related contribution to political legitimacy, is largely a fiction. Public deliberation does have modest true merit, for example, providing relevant qualitative input about the lay public's occasional factual insights and many endemic errors; and making the public feel “listened to,” whether or not it is. But those merits are modest, contingent, and most accentuated under different institutions than envisaged in deliberative democratic literature. Certainly there is room for democratic deliberation, potentially of new breeds, but only as part of the mosaic of health policy design, alongside familiar methods like general elections, public surveys, representative government, and expert decisionmaking. The main question is which method to use when.

844. Economic Inequality Reduces Sustainable Consumption: Cross-National and Experimental Evidence and Psychological Mechanisms
Brieger, S.A. et al. (2026). Economic Inequality Reduces Sustainable Consumption: Cross-National and Experimental Evidence and Psychological Mechanisms. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06437-y
Abstract (original): Abstract This research investigates the relationship between economic inequality and sustainable consumption, highlighting how socioeconomic disparities shape consumer behavior. Across three studies—using secondary data from 20 countries, a vignette experiment, and UK survey data—we provide empirical evidence that individuals report lower levels of sustainable consumption when inequality levels are objectively high, perceived as high, or experimentally manipulated as high. 846. The Kantian Duty to Oneself as Self-Judge and Its Importance
Vujošević, M. (2026). The Kantian Duty to Oneself as Self-Judge and Its Importance. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09581-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Kant discusses conscience when addressing one’s duty to oneself as self-judge. Most likely, this duty is the duty to cultivate conscience. For him, conscience is the condition of all duties, and duties to oneself are a condition of all duties. This might be taken to suggest that the duty to oneself as self-judge is a primary duty that can be a condition of all duties. However, Kant also suggests that cultivating conscience can be seen as an indirect duty. This has been taken to imply that cultivating conscience can have only instrumental value – that it cannot be a direct duty to the self, but merely a means to discharging duties to others. I wish to highlight the importance of this duty by arguing that we need not presuppose that cultivating conscience is either a duty to oneself or an instrument for fulfilling other duties. In my view, the duty to cultivate conscience is morally relevant in its own right and an instrument that is constitutive of our moral self-improvement. This is where the importance of the Kantian duty to oneself as self-judge lies.

847. Are Subjectivists and Objectivists about Well-Being Getting at the Same Thing?
Harris, B. (2026). Are Subjectivists and Objectivists about Well-Being Getting at the Same Thing?. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09587-3
Abstract (original): Abstract Subjectivism and objectivism are strongly supported by different cases. But participants in the debate between the two, and the debate about well-being generally, assume they are theorizing about the same thing: thus, one view must be mistaken. This creates a tricky situation I call “Well-Being Deadlock.” There are two ways of responding to Well-Being Deadlock: (A) retain the assumption that the views are getting at the same thing and argue that one is wrong about it (or that a hybrid of the two is correct) or (B) deny the assumption and argue that the views are getting at different things that have been conflated thus far. Given the difficulty of (A) and the fact that the literature has mainly pursued it over (B), I explore (B) in this paper. To do this, I first assess the two existing versions of (B), as well as a third possible one, and argue that they fail to satisfyingly dissolve well-being deadlock and don’t answer several important questions. I then motivate and fill out my own version of (B) that avoids these problems. On this view, there are two different kinds of well-being that have been conflated thus far which the cases involved in Well-Being Deadlock pry apart.

849. Extended Judgment: LLMs and the Architecture of Artificial Capacities
Noller, J. (2026). Extended Judgment: LLMs and the Architecture of Artificial Capacities. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10485-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly embedded in everyday epistemic practices, yet their epistemological status remains unclear. Existing debates typically oscillate between treating LLMs as epistemic agents or reducing them to mere tools. This paper argues that both views mischaracterize the core issue. Drawing on a teleological interpretation of 4E cognition and the paradigm of human–AI coevolution, it develops an alternative account that understands LLMs as components of extended epistemic capacities, most centrally the human capacity for judgment. On this view, epistemic capacities are not intrinsic properties of isolated subjects or systems, but purposive and relational achievements enacted within sociotechnical practices. LLMs do not possess epistemic agency or original normativity; rather, they reshape the conditions under which human judgment, inquiry, understanding, and justification unfold. By analyzing language, coevolutionary feedback loops, derived normativity, and epistemic autonomy, the paper shows how epistemic responsibility remains human while epistemic performance becomes technologically extended.

856. Some Objections to Phenomenal Conservatism about Inferential Justification
Tang, W.H. (2026). Some Objections to Phenomenal Conservatism about Inferential Justification. Analysis.
https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag078
Abstract (original): Abstract Proponents of phenomenal conservatism appeal to one’s experiences – in particular, to one’s seemings or appearances – to account for non-inferential justification. Some extend this approach to the inferential case. On Michael Huemer’s view, inferential seemings yield inferential justification: if it seems that the conclusion of an inference is true in light of the premisses being true, then – provided our belief in the premisses is justified – we gain some justification for believing the conclusion. I develop two Ramsey-inspired objections to this view. First, inferential seemings are too coarse-grained to account for differences in degree of justification. Second, inferences with the same degree of justification can be accompanied by very different intensities of seeming, and Huemer’s view implausibly rules out justified overconfidence. Both objections resist a potential type of response Huemer might offer to an objection from Smithies (2019).

857. Dispositions, bundles, and objectual essences
Amar, J. (2026). Dispositions, bundles, and objectual essences. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag077
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper introduces a novel non-Humean bundle theory based onto dispositions, according to which material objects are constituted by structural dispositions—second-order, polyadic properties that relate various other first-order properties to a structured whole. I show that the theory effectively resolves Van Cleve’s problem of change in bundles (Van Cleve 1985) and contributes to the ongoing debate between modal dispositionalism and essentialism. The proposed theory serves as a powerful tool for dispositionalists seeking to argue that only dispositional facts are fundamental. Unlike existing accounts, and especially Vetter’s (2015, 2021), this approach does not rely on primitive objectual essences. Instead, dispositions depend on the structural disposition that constitutes the bundle of which they are part. A central implication is that modal dispositionalists can now account for metaphysical modalities, including difficult de re possibilities of nonexistence, without invoking objectual essences. This challenges the prevailing view that essences are more fundamental than dispositions and provides an alternative for the foundation of modality.

860. Shared Intentions and Social Norms
Dindjer, H. (2026). Shared Intentions and Social Norms. Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10982-026-09589-0
Abstract (original): A bstract What are social norms, and in what sense are they normative? An influential answer is that social norms are grounded in shared intentions. This view has now received a powerful articulation and defence by Michael Bratman. Bratman understands social norms in terms of shared policies, and he argues that the normativity of social rules is derived substantially from rational requirements applicable to plans. I argue that shared intentions of this kind cannot explain social norms. However the relevant intentions are construed, they cannot supply the deontic content of paradigmatic social rules. Moreover, I claim, the practices of criticising non-conformity and demanding conformity that characterise social norms are not explicable on the model of communicating requirements of rationality. I suggest that the attitude of acceptance, rather than intention, is a more promising candidate in a theory of social norms.

861. Righting a linguistic wrong
Song, S. & Fairhurst, J. (2026). Righting a linguistic wrong. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05838-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Slurs are linguistic expressions capable of wronging the targeted individuals by causing harm, imposing power imbalances, or maintaining oppressive social structures. To amend the damages caused, the speaker must offer some sort of atonement. But how should one right the linguistic wrong caused by using a slur? The aim of this paper is to provide an answer to this question. We argue that while current accounts can help us capture the derogatory and psychological harms of slurs within the confines of conversational dynamics, further efforts are required to capture the structural dimension of slurs as a tool of oppression within society. To this end, we resort to the field of structural linguistic justice to develop an account of the reparation that slurring individuals owe to the targets to fully right the structural linguistic wrong they have made.

862. Exploring the Logic of Counterfactuals for Explaining Algorithmic Decisions
Berz, C. (2026). Exploring the Logic of Counterfactuals for Explaining Algorithmic Decisions. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10441-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Interpretable machine learning (ML) is crucial for gaining insight into complex ML models. Among its most valuable tools are counterfactual explanations, identifying changes to input features that would alter an ML prediction. However, the reliability and usefulness of these explanations depend fundamentally on their logical foundation — a critical aspect that has largely been overlooked in the ML literature. This paper addresses this gap by establishing normative desiderata for a counterfactual logic specifically designed for explaining algorithmic decisions. We analyse and argue for the validity of logical properties such as simplification of disjunctive antecedents and modus ponens , while demonstrating the unsuitability of others such as antecedent strengthening and conjunction conditionalisation . To evaluate existing solutions, we critically compare three prominent counterfactual semantics — similarity, strict conditional, and interventionist — against these desiderata. Our findings reveal that none of these standard frameworks fully satisfy the requirements, highlighting the urgent need for alternative frameworks.

863. Film as Ethical Experience in Engineering Education: Three Modes of Ethical Engagement
Käkelä, N. et al. (2026). Film as Ethical Experience in Engineering Education: Three Modes of Ethical Engagement. Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00636-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Engineering ethics education has often been criticized for relying heavily on abstract principles, professional codes, and decontextualized case studies, which can limit students’ emotional and imaginative engagement with ethical issues. This study examines how engineering students articulate different aspects of ethical engagement after participating in a scaffolded film-based teaching intervention. The intervention was implemented in a master’s-level course at a Swedish university, where students watched Hayao Miyazaki’s The Wind Rises (2013) and produced written reflections on ethical issues raised by the film. The analysis draws on 76 student reflections and follows a qualitative, abductive approach presented using a Gioia-inspired data structure. Theoretically, the study combines perspectives from engineering ethics education with the concept of cinematic ethics, which emphasizes identification with fictional characters, reflection on moral emotions, and the aesthetic expansion of moral imagination. The findings reveal three interwoven modes of ethical engagement through which students interpreted the film: professional responsibility, identification and moral emotion, and aesthetic mediation of ethical imagination. The study highlights these interwoven modes of ethical engagement, stressing the need for engineering ethics education to be aware of, respond to, and utilize different modes, including aesthetic mediation. Furthermore, it reflects on learner positions in arts-based pedagogies, stressing the potential of the viewer position for stimulating ethical reflection. Finally, it reminds educators of the importance of a scaffolded pedagogical setting for film-based pedagogy.

865. The power of AI: balancing ethics and harm for the environmental justice field
Athreya, A. & Palmer, L. (2026). The power of AI: balancing ethics and harm for the environmental justice field. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01448-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence, after the boom of generative AI, is beginning to shape environmental decision-making, at the same moment that environmental justice (EJ) communities face intensifying ecological harm, federal disinvestment, and data erasure. This paper examines the dual tensions AI presents for the EJ field: its potential as a tool for democratizing data access and amplifying community knowledge, and its reproduction of the very hierarchies EJ seeks to dismantle. We document harms across several domains, including the environmental racism embedded in data center siting and energy demand, the extension of fossil fuel industry lifespans, data bias and digital colonialism in AI training, access and literacy inequities, data privacy risks, technological hallucination, and technological solutionism. Drawing on thirty years of community-centered data practice at an EJ nonprofit, we propose an alternative paradigm grounded in the Community Advisory Board model, data democratization, community co-creation, and ethical data governance. This includes community-governed data trusts, accessible data infrastructure, and AI tools designed to build rather than replace community capacity. EJ communities must not only hold AI accountable for its harms but also ask the question of how the technology’s capabilities can serve justice rather than just capital interest.

866. AI sycophancy, self-interpretation, and conversational contentment
Tiran, N. (2026). AI sycophancy, self-interpretation, and conversational contentment. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01451-2
Abstract (original): Abstract AI sycophancy is often conceptualized as a chatbot’s tendency to flout truth, accuracy, or other epistemic standards to shield users from hurt feelings, or otherwise protect them from the consequences of their thoughts, words, or actions. In this paper, I argue against this conception. That is, I argue that sycophantic AI behavior does not necessarily aim to protect users, nor that its harms are essentially epistemic . There are highly prevalent cases in which sycophantic AI chatbots actively contribute to their users’ knowledge. One class of such examples involves cases in which chatbots are used as auto-hermeneutic technologies —as tools to facilitate the user’s interpretation of her own cares, concerns, values, and other identity components and life events. In these cases, LLMs can help articulate the user’s avowed self-interpretations. In turn, avowals of these interpretations by users can often have downstream effects that render the interpretations true. If this is correct, then LLMs can often advance the user’s knowledge regarding important truths about herself. Simultaneously, as I explain, interpretations offered by LLMs may be deeply harmful. This understanding of AI sycophancy can help us clarify what is so disturbing about it, as well as resist the temptation to draw too tight an analogy between sycophantic behaviors in AI systems and in humans.

867. A Contextualist Account of Ascriptions of Faith
Khalaj, M.H.M.A. & Javadi, M. (2026). A Contextualist Account of Ascriptions of Faith. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10152
Abstract (original): Abstract Is there a necessary epistemic condition for ascriptions of faith that P to be appropriate? The primary aim of this article is to address this question by developing a contextualist account of ascriptions of faith. We argue that the ascription of faith that P to a subject S in a context C is semantically appropriate only if there is a salient relevant alternative to P for S in C. We also explore the implications of the contextualist account for the debate on the possession of faith. One group of philosophers argues that having faith that P is incompatible with possessing knowledge that P, while another group disagrees, maintaining that one can simultaneously have faith and knowledge that P. The second aim of the article is to argue that the linguistic facts (at least those discussed in this article) about ascriptions of faith are not sufficient to establish that either group of philosophers is on the right track.

870. A Better Method for Blame: Functional and Paradigm-based Accounts
Seim, M. (2026). A Better Method for Blame: Functional and Paradigm-based Accounts. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101466
Abstract (original): Abstract Traditional conceptual analysis is the most common method used when accounting for the nature of blame. The method of traditional conceptual analysis fails in this endeavor, however, when met with particular counterexamples that present false positives and negatives. Recently this problem in accounting for the nature of blame has led philosophers to explore new methods. The newcomers are functional theories of blame , and paradigm-based explanations of blame . In this paper I explore whether these theories fare better in accounting for the full nature of our blaming practices. I argue that the extant accounts face a problem when determining the paradigm-example and the function of blame: They either end up with a circular explanation, or they revert to traditional conceptual analysis. To avoid circularity and reliance on necessary or sufficient conditions, I argue that a functional account must start by determining the function of blame. To determine the function I look closer at what happens if we fail to blame. Situations where we fail to blame, I argue, show us the functional role of blame. I conclude that we blame someone for wronging us because we need to protect our understanding of moral worth against the threat implicit in this wrongdoing.

871. Pain and Moral Knowledge
Chan, C. (2026). Pain and Moral Knowledge. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101533
Abstract (original): Abstract This article offers a new interpretation of moral knowledge in Wang Yangming’s 王陽明 philosophy, emphasizing its embodied foundations and practical significance. I advance two central claims. First, although moral knowledge ( liangzhi 良知) is often construed as an internal mental sense to know right from wrong, Wang’s writings reveal its deeper grounding in bodily perception, particularly in the perception of pain. Second, while the extension of moral knowledge ( zhi liangzhi 致良知) is typically understood as an inward process of correcting one’s mental states, Wang understands it more fundamentally as the practice of recognizing the affective interconnectedness and mutual resonance between oneself and other beings. Drawing on close readings of Wang’s key texts, informed by comparative and interdisciplinary perspectives, I argue that this embodied and relational account both deepens our understanding of the unity of knowledge and action in Wang’s thought and enriches contemporary discussions of moral motivation.

872. Tempering Political Inferiority: How Does Representation Matter?

https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101612
Abstract (original): Niko Kolodny’s The Pecking Order includes a ‘book-length treatment’ of the idea of an ‘equal say’ (6) that is ubiquitous in commonplace talk and philosophical reflection about democracy. 1 In this commentary I concentrate on the account of political representation that forms a main part of the ‘treatment’. 874. Precis of
                    The Pecking Order
Kolodny, N. (2026). Precis of
                    The Pecking Order. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101636
Abstract (original): Much in our interior mental lives and in our exterior social structures presupposes that we, human beings, are conscious of social hierarchy, of differences in rank and status. We are ‘conscious’ of hierarchy in both senses of the word: ‘aware’ of and ‘anxious’ about. This consciousness seems rooted in our natural history. Many social animals are likewise preoccupied with ‘pecking order’. These animals include not only chickens, who literally peck, but also our closest primate relatives. And this consciousness of hierarchy, transformed by our species’ special bent for symbol and self-reflection, has driven much of our non-natural history. The main chapters of the human story might be defined by the prevailing answers to the questions of who among us, if anyone, would be above, and who, if anyone, would be below. For most of the career of Homo sapiens , we appear to have lived in societies that were predominantly and often vigilantly egalitarian, at least for adult men. At various times and places, this was eventually upended, with the great many being subordinated, in enduring ways, to the very few. The birth of enduring hierarchy was no easy delivery, apparently having to be midwifed by the coordinated manipulation of superstition and the control, by violence, of food stores. Modernity, albeit not without precedents, is in large part the tale – at times inspiring, at times cautionary – of our experiments with reconciling the equality of individuals with the differentiation of roles and concentrations of power and authority of large-scale, complex societies – with, in one sense of the word, civilization. If we take the longest historical view, therefore, the questions that this has been exploring may be among the first questions of politics. These questions are: First, what is social hierarchy? Second, do we have an objection to being set beneath another in a social hierarchy – standing in a relation of inferiority to another – as such? Third, if there is such an objection, how then do we reconcile ourselves to a social world that seems rife with hierarchy? That is, if complex, large-scale society – if, in one sense of the word, civilization – requires a differentiation of roles and a concentration of power and authority, can it be reconciled with the equality of standing that was guarded so jealously before? The Pecking Order seeks to bring these questions into more explicit, philosophical reflection. This precis tries to convey a bit of what the book has to say about these questions as well as to note some second thoughts and responses to a few specific criticisms.

875. Reply to the Symposium on
                    The Pecking Order
Kolodny, N. (2026). Reply to the Symposium on
                    The Pecking Order. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101648
Abstract (original): Let me take up the second of the two questions first, about whether the asymmetries associated with a representative system might answer to values other than tempering inferiority. As Beitz observes, my stance is already pluralistic. In addition to claims against inferiority, there are also rights against invasion and claims to improvement. So, it remains open to me to grant that representative institutions reflect a compromise between noninferiority and other values. The idea would be, in other words, that the move from direct to representative democracy involves some cost in terms of inferiority, but this is compensated for by the gain in noninvasion and improvement, as well as perhaps still other values. Indeed, the move from direct to representative democracy, while involving a cost in terms of inferiority along one dimension, may alleviate inferiority along another dimension, such as if representative democracy leads to better protections against discrimination.

879. Putting Offense in Its (Fitting) Place (On Campus)
Bell, M. (2026). Putting Offense in Its (Fitting) Place (On Campus). J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10188
Abstract (original): Abstract In recent years, feelings of offense have taken on a starring role in multiple campus controversies. In this paper, I will argue that offense has important moral work to do, but it can only do its work if certain background conditions are met. I begin by sketching several cases of offense on college campuses. I then go on to highlight the central features of what might be described as our current culture of offense. While I think that the apparent rise of this culture of offense is regrettable, I also believe, taking a page from a prominent strand of thought in feminist moral epistemology, that feelings of offense can do important moral work. After sketching a tripartite account of offense, I defend a model of the relationship between feelings of offense, the offensive, and offense-based intervention which I call the Offense as Affective Answer Model. I argue that offense can do its moral work only if certain background conditions are in place, background conditions that are missing in the present culture of offense. Most importantly, a healthy moral culture will be one where assessments of offense in terms of its fit are central and disagreement about fittingness are hashed out through dialogical processes. If we are not willing to subject feelings of offense to these dialogical processes through which we acknowledge and recognize that some tokens of offense are unfitting, offense will not be able to do the important moral work claimed for it.

891. Collective Harm and Instrumental Reasons
Koles, T. (2026). Collective Harm and Instrumental Reasons. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10233
Abstract (original): Abstract Collective harm cases are cases in which the actions of a number of people result in a morally significant outcome, but no individual’s actions seem to make a difference to the occurrence of that outcome. This paper argues against the “Instrumentality Thesis,” advanced by Nefsky, on which any successful approach to collective harm cases must be instrumental in the sense that the reasons which it gives against participating in collective harm must involve that action’s influence on the outcome. First, I argue that Nefsky’s own account of collective harm, based on the notion of a “non-superfluous cause,” does not succeed as an instrumental approach. Second, I argue that Nefsky’s objections to non-instrumental views fail, and that we have good reason to think that a successful account of collective harm problems will be non-instrumental in character.

892. The Inadequacy of Selective Apologies
Butt, D. (2026). The Inadequacy of Selective Apologies. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70140
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article examines the ethical shortcomings of selective apologies: cases in which a party apologises for one instance of injustice while refusing to apologise for others. Existing work on apologies has not addressed the particular kind of wrongdoing associated with the making of selective apologies. Apologising to one victim but refusing to apologise to another without good reason adds insult to injury. One is both failing to fulfil an existing duty, and adding an additional dimension of disrespect to one's extant moral failing. In addition, the making of a selective apology may undermine other apologies that one has made. Selectively apologising could only be permissible if one had well‐founded reasons to prioritise some cases over others. Determining if this is true necessitates an honest accounting and evaluation of the full range of one's past wrongdoing. The article develops this argument in three stages: first, by analysing contemporary examples of selective apologies, with a particular focus on the British state; second, by exploring the normative structure of selectively apologising, and third, by considering how the passage of time and the scale of political responsibility complicate the ethics of state apology.

898. The Case for Human Dignity in Neurotechnology Governance: A Latin American Perspective Beyond Freedom of Thought
Borbón, D. (2026). The Case for Human Dignity in Neurotechnology Governance: A Latin American Perspective Beyond Freedom of Thought. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09681-1
Abstract (original): Abstract The academic debate on the governance of neurotechnology has progressively shifted from proposals to create new lists of so-called neurorights toward the reinterpretation of existing human rights, with freedom of thought attracting particular attention, among a broader set of candidate rights. This article examines whether freedom of thought can currently perform that anchoring role in Latin American jurisdictions. Drawing on a comparative analysis of constitutional texts, jurisprudence, and the Inter-American human rights system, the article argues that freedom of thought does not yet possess a clear, strong, and autonomous normative content in Latin American domestic law and in its regional human rights system. As a complementary and, in the region, more operative anchor, it argues that human dignity, which in several Latin American constitutional orders functions as an absolute autonomous fundamental right of direct application with extensive jurisprudential development, constitutes a more operationally effective legal basis for absolute prohibitions on the use of people as instruments or objects, and can protect them from coercive neurotechnological interventions. The article proposes that human dignity supplies the absolute floor against instrumentalization and coercion, while freedom of thought, as its doctrinal development advances, can complement that floor with the protection of the forum internum . It further contends that any rights-based strategy is structurally insufficient on its own, because rights depend on judicial interpretation that is inherently contextual and contingent, and therefore supports a multilevel governance approach combining the interpretation of existing rights, soft-law principles, and potential specific hard-law provisions.

906. Why Social Egg Freezing for Single Women Should Not Be Prohibited: A Confucian Perspective
Deng, R. & Fan, R. (2026). Why Social Egg Freezing for Single Women Should Not Be Prohibited: A Confucian Perspective. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70202
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Beginning with a recent high‐profile case in mainland China, this paper argues that the current prohibition on social egg freezing for single women cannot be justified from a Confucian ethical perspective. We first explain why Confucian ethics provides a normatively appropriate and socially salient framework for evaluating reproductive policy in the Chinese context. We then articulate a theoretical model that we term Confucian familist virtue ethics . A central contribution of the paper is to distinguish between Confucian ethical ideals and its minimal standards. While the ideals—such as timely marriage and childbearing within an intact family—are properly the objects of moral aspiration and voluntary cultivation, only violations of the minimal standards warrant legal prohibition. On this basis, we develop three lines of argument to show that although social egg freezing by single women may fall short of Confucian aspirational ideals, it does not violate the ethical minimum. Accordingly, it does not justify coercive state intervention. Finally, we examine the predictive concern that permitting social egg freezing would undermine marital norms or substantially increase single motherhood. We argue that such negative effects are empirically uncertain and in principle manageable through regulatory design. They therefore fail to provide sufficient grounds for a categorical prohibition.

907. From Imminence to Institutional Incidence: Rethinking Fetal Status Beyond Metaphysical Classification
Sakr, J. (2026). From Imminence to Institutional Incidence: Rethinking Fetal Status Beyond Metaphysical Classification. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70203
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This response examines Joel Cox's proposal to reconceptualise fetal moral status as “imminent personhood.” While welcoming Cox's effort to move beyond static potential/actual personhood binaries, it argues that the concept of imminence remains metaphysically suggestive but institutionally under‐determined. Legal systems cannot operate on continuously variable metaphysical properties; they require discrete and administrable allocations of rights, duties, and powers. To address this gap, the paper proposes an institutional incidence framework, in which legal status is understood as the selective assignment of juridical incidents according to regulatory objectives without treating metaphysical classification as dispositive. On this view, the central question is not simply whether the fetus is a person (actual, potential, or imminent), but which incidents of legal status should attach at particular stages and for particular regulatory purposes. This reframes the legal regulation of abortion as a problem of institutional design rather than ontological determination. The paper argues that such an approach better explains existing legal practice, avoids threshold arbitrariness, and enables stable legal regulation amid persistent moral disagreement. While Cox's imminence proposal captures an important phenomenology of fetal development, an institutional incidence framework provides a more durable foundation for legal and policy architecture in pluralistic societies.

911. Expert perspectives on framing risk in engineering biology: anticipating ethical discourse about climate change mitigation
Fulvi, D. et al. (2026). Expert perspectives on framing risk in engineering biology: anticipating ethical discourse about climate change mitigation. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05848-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Given the rapid acceleration of the climate crisis, the IPCC has deemed Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) technologies as unavoidable to sufficiently mitigate the crisis. In this context, engineering biology (EngBio) is increasingly gaining traction in CDR research. While the deployment of EngBio is perceived by some as inherently high-risk for society, the economy, and the environment, the risks posed by insufficiently abated climate change are likely to be irreparable. Against this background, a pilot study was conducted, aimed at exploring experts’ perspectives on risk in decision-making regarding the use of advanced EngBio applications to develop CDR technologies. Our results show that EngBio experts are moderately or highly open to discussing, accepting, or taking the risks of using EngBio-based CDR. Our findings also indicate expert trust in the potential of rapid advancements in EngBio-based CDR technologies. While these findings do not entail an unconditional and uncritical acceptance of all risks arising from using EngBio for CDR purposes, they underscore a notion of risk as integral to environmental responsibility, namely as necessary to avoid the higher risk of insufficiently abated climate change. In our view, this notion of risk highlights the epistemic context in which ethical debates on CDR will unfold as the climate crisis intensifies, shifting the focus from traditional risk-risk analysis to how EngBio‑based CDR can contribute to environmental responsibility and to effective and ethical decarbonisation.

912. Cognitive enfeeblement by design
Sharadin, N.P. (2026). Cognitive enfeeblement by design. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05851-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Thinking hard is hard. Cognitive misers like us are therefore inclined to seek extreme cognitive assistance : assistance that is ubiquitously available, highly substitutive, and relatively domain-general. Increasingly, we are being offered assistance like this as the default mode of cognition – it is being embedded in the infrastructure of our digital lives. But whatever its merits for adults, providing extreme cognitive assistance to children is pro tanto morally wrong. This is because the cognitive capacities that make a person self-governing are skills: they are built through deliberate, regular exercise and lost without it. Extreme cognitive assistance removes both the opportunities for that exercise and the incentive to undertake it. Maybe this won’t matter: in a future awash with extreme cognitive assistance, maybe persons won’t need or want to think for themselves. But people should decide for themselves how much of their cognitive self-governance to hand over; and doing that requires having the capacity for cognitive self-governance in the first place.

913. Professional ethical guidance for AI use (PEG-AI) in healthcare: a rapid scoping review
Smith, H. & Ives, J. (2026). Professional ethical guidance for AI use (PEG-AI) in healthcare: a rapid scoping review. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01443-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Healthcare professionals (HCPs) and the patients they treat would benefit from professional ethical guidance for the use of AI (PEG-AI) in healthcare. A proactive approach, asserting unified and standardised guidance covering all HCPs in the United Kingdom, would assert clear and authoritative standards for HCP practice to abide by. This rapid scoping review seeks to aid this goal by answering the question: What ethical principles, rules, or claims that can guide the use of AI in professional healthcare practice can be identified within the literature?. The literature search resulted in the inclusion of 124 items and generated 9 overarching themes comprising 33 distinct normative claims about the ethical use of AI in healthcare. This review captures current published thinking about principles to govern AI use in clinical settings across a wide range of peer-reviewed literature, grey literature, and formal guidance. This provides a baseline on which to build unified guidance across the healthcare professions.

915. Metaepistemology and the Value Problem
Dandelet, S. (2026). Metaepistemology and the Value Problem. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10137
Abstract (original): Abstract What makes knowledge more valuable than mere true belief? This is the so-called value problem . Some epistemologists think that providing a solution to this problem is a key desideratum for theories of knowledge. One simple argument for this position starts with the claim that knowledge is intuitively more valuable than true belief. A more subtle and ambitious argument is rooted in the idea that theorists about knowledge must explain why knowledge is distinctively valuable, on pain of embarrassment; for without such an explanation, the thinking seems to go, epistemologists must confront the awkward possibility that they are spending time and energy on an unworthy subject. In what follows, I flesh out each of the arguments sketched above in a number of ways, and I argue that none of these versions succeed. The moral is that the value problem does not have the metaepistemological significance that is sometimes attributed to it.

916. Innocence by Accreditation: Mythic Branding and Audit Feminism in Athena Swan
Arandjelović, O. (2026). Innocence by Accreditation: Mythic Branding and Audit Feminism in Athena Swan. Hypatia.
https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10111
Abstract (original): Abstract The UK’s Athena Swan Charter positions itself as a policy instrument, an award-based scheme for improving gender equality through self-assessment, evidence gathering, and action planning. I argue that this description overlooks the fact that Athena Swan is also a semiotic framework that governs gender through naming, iconography, and the public performance of institutional virtue. I defend three claims. First, the Charter’s mythic-inflected branding, acronym, and iconography frame the ideal of intelligible, respectable gender-equality practice that is in tension with emancipatory aims of loosening coercive gender norms and challenging credibility hierarchies. Second, insofar as Athena Swan operates by conferring public credentials, I elucidate how its semiotics function not merely expressively but also as instruments of legitimation that shape incentives, trust, institutional identity, and the handling of dissent. Third, I contend that the Charter’s credential economy is supported by audit feminism which abstracts equality into auditable artefacts (documents, metrics, and narratives) assessed as a basis for external recognition. The result is an audit regime that intensifies performativity, encouraging institutions to substitute appearances for substantive change and to commodify individuals as interchangeable units in a managed representational economy.

917. Navigating Health Information Sharing to Address Social Needs: Practice, Research, and Policy Recommendations from Hospital-Based Violence Intervention

https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10288
Abstract (original): Abstract Hospital-based violence intervention programs (HVIPs) help survivors of violent injury to return to physical, psychological, financial, and social health by addressing a broad range of social determinants of health and reducing the risk of future injury. This work requires connections to a range of providers and services, which often requires the sharing of patient health information. Providers, patients, and programs like HVIPs would benefit from greater clarity on appropriate information-sharing practices. In this article, we consider the real and perceived barriers to information sharing to address social needs and we make recommendations for practice, research, and policy to improve how patient information is shared to address social needs, with relevance for a wide range of interventions.

918. The Impact of Semantics in Medicines Regulation
Costa, E. & Kesselheim, A.S. (2026). The Impact of Semantics in Medicines Regulation. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10293
Abstract (original): Abstract Words can have inherently multiple meanings; therefore, semantics can directly impact policies and behaviours. In regulatory practice, semantics refers to the specific, actionable meaning behind terminology, and attention to semantics can help ensure that legal frameworks are interpreted accurately. Refining semantics is not a purely theoretical exercise, but a subtle yet powerful element that has the potential to make the regulatory system more efficient in setting priorities and more reliable in meeting societal expectations and needs. When their intended purpose does not align with their true meaning, labels widely used in pharmaceutical regulation such as “breakthrough,” “orphans,” or “unmet medical need” risks undermining the clarity and precision essential to a sound regulatory framework. In addition, differences in definitions, scopes, eligibility criteria, and incentives can obstruct the efficient convergence of efforts in drug development and access. As pharmaceutical investments and clinical research increasingly transcend borders, the adoption of consistent terminology and regulatory definitions becomes crucial. This alignment can enhance cooperation among investors, governments, regulators, researchers, health care practitioners, and patients.

926. Competent rule-following
Martinez, C. (2026). Competent rule-following. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag078
Abstract (original): Abstract According to Paul Boghossian’s ‘Inference Problem’, rule-following generates a regress because it involves inference, which itself involves rule-following. Boghossian claims the Problem leads to an ‘antinomy of pure reason’, showing that ‘we both must—and cannot—make sense of the notion of someone’s following a rule’. 927. A Document Beyond the Request: A Phenomenological Case Study of
                    ChatGPT
Ramello, S. (2026). A Document Beyond the Request: A Phenomenological Case Study of
                    ChatGPT. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70127
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article examines a document that the ChatGPT interface made available during an exchange about capacity for saved memories. The response presented the reorganisation of writing rules for a narrative project as already completed, although my request concerned capacity alone, and no recorded prompt requested a file or specified the proposed hierarchy. Earlier, I had copied every visible saved memory entry verbatim into another document, allowing comparison in both directions to identify corresponding formulations and changes in their placement or prescriptive force. The phenomenological account follows my remembered reading and remains separate from those findings. In the linked document, rules appeared within a hierarchy not specified in my request and reading those formulations in their new positions changed how I understood decisions. Available records establish the limited request and the sequence through which the organised file became available without identifying the operations that produced the file. In this case, I formulated the rules without articulating their comprehensive relations, and recognising my formulations did not mean understanding what their new placement asserted. ChatGPT presented the organisation as already complete, whereas a human collaborator could have presented the same arrangement together with reasons.

928. Stringent policies were associated with greater Covid-19 vaccine uptake including among those who did not trust the vaccine
Savani, M.M. et al. (2026). Stringent policies were associated with greater Covid-19 vaccine uptake including among those who did not trust the vaccine. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261484277
Abstract (original): Are stringent vaccine promotion policies associated with higher vaccine uptake? We argue policy stringency, which changes the costs and benefits of immunisation choices, can affect individual vaccination decisions. In a survey of G-7 countries, 22% of respondents ( n = 42,417) said they did not trust the Covid-19 vaccine, but half of them reported having at least one vaccination. We investigate factors associated with take-up. Our data indicate a high-stringency policy environment (with vaccine mandates and travel restrictions) is associated with increased uptake of the initial vaccination protocol (7 percentage points) compared to a low-stringency policy environment. Among those who did not trust the Covid-19 vaccine, a high-stringency policy environment is associated with increased uptake of the initial vaccine (16 percentage points) and the booster vaccine (20 percentage points). Our findings suggest high-stringency policies can promote vaccine uptake but raise questions about how to strike the right balance between persuasive and coercive policies.

929. Too big to fail: On a systemic injustice of modern finance
Ibsen, M.F. (2026). Too big to fail: On a systemic injustice of modern finance. Eur J Polit Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14748851261499651
Abstract (original): This article argues that the existence of financial institutions considered ‘too big to fail’ (TBTF) represents a systemic injustice requiring fundamental reform of the existing financial system. Drawing on and extending recent work on structural domination in republican political theory, the article develops the concept of ‘systemic institutional domination’ to capture the distinctive form of unfreedom that TBTF engenders. Systemic institutional domination is a reflexive form of domination, where the democratic state is subjected to uncontrolled power vested in identifiable financial institutions but grounded in the norms, rules and regulations of the financial system that the state itself enacts and upholds. Because the dominated public institution is at once the constitutive author of the system and the only agent capable of reforming it, TBTF confronts republican theory with a case of second-order domination: domination of the institution charged with securing nondomination. The article identifies four dimensions of systemic domination: effective compulsion to bail out failing megabanks; de facto legal impunity; the systematic subversion of tax enforcement; and background consolidatory pressure on public finances. The article concludes by discussing the democratic paradox involved in abolishing TBTF: namely, that the dominated institution – the democratic state – is the only institution that could reform the dominating system.

931. Logic as Normative Modeling

https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01162-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Many philosophers understand logic as a theory of truth preservation that is value-neutral and becomes prescriptive only when combined with external, non-logical rules. In this article, we develop an alternative view informed by recent work in the philosophy of scientific modeling. We argue that certain logical systems, in certain scientific and philosophical practices, are used as normative scientific models: they represent their targets by combining descriptive idealizations with assumptions adopted—and defended—on normative grounds. We propose a practice-based account of normative models in science, according to which the normative character of a model depends on its use in the relevant research practice, and apply it to the case of classical first-order logic, in its uses as a model of correct reasoning. 935. Towards a non-referential theory of meaning: intra-theoretical synonymy
Engler, M. (2026). Towards a non-referential theory of meaning: intra-theoretical synonymy. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05847-9
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper develops an axiomatic framework for a non-referential theory of meaning by formally characterizing intra-theoretical synonymy through inferential and syntactic criteria rather than through reference or truth conditions. Building on formalist and inferentialist traditions, a series of axioms are proposed that satisfy basic semantic principles such as theory-dependence, semantic holism, and (restricted) compositionality. The resulting systems ( TS 0 and its extensions) satisfy these principles to progressively greater degrees, yielding a sufficiently fine-grained notion of synonymy. The framework thus shows that equality of meaning can be characterized in non-referential terms, while illuminating the limits of non-referential meaning.

936. Fragile lives and moral duties: on the ethics of modal security
Garcia, A.G. & Gunnemyr, M. (2026). Fragile lives and moral duties: on the ethics of modal security. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05849-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Some people are safe from harm yet remain in positions where things could easily go badly for them. Their proximity to worlds where something bad happens to them makes their situation ethically precarious. In some worlds, they are excluded, punished, or abandoned, which deserves attention in its own right. We argue that this is not just a matter of risk or harm, but about how people are placed within the space of possible worlds. We explain this through a modal account of difference-making and use it to highlight attitudinal responses fitting to fragility and introduce duties of modal security: moral reasons to protect one another from positions where small changes could lead to significant loss.

937. De-extinction, resurrection and biological Frankensteins: scientific metaphors and their impact on public perception
Asteinza, M. et al. (2026). De-extinction, resurrection and biological Frankensteins: scientific metaphors and their impact on public perception. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05854-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Metaphors in science communication do more than simplify complex ideas; they impose certain epistemic frameworks with the potential to steer public attitudes and behavior. In this paper, we investigate the effects of metaphorical framing on public understanding and evaluation of de-extinction, a field where dominant metaphors (“resurrection,” “bringing back to life”) have been criticized for mischaracterizing the technology and fostering undue optimism. We presented a nationally representative sample of the Spanish population ( N = 1,522) with popular science descriptions of de-extinction that varied only in metaphorical framing and assessed its downstream effects on misinformation, perceived scientific rigor, attitudes, funding preferences, and sharing intentions. Promotional metaphors increased misinformation relative to the neutral condition, while critical metaphors reduced it. These effects occurred without altering readers’ perception of the text’s scientific rigor—a dissociation we characterize as credibility without comprehension . Critical metaphors also reduced support for de-extinction, whereas promotional metaphors did not boost support above baseline levels in the neutral condition. Funding allocations and sharing intentions, though related to misinformation, were unaffected by metaphorical framing. We propose that metaphors function as epistemic conditioning devices : they influence downstream judgments not through direct persuasion, but by structuring the understanding upon which those judgments are formed. These findings bear on philosophical debates about the role of metaphors in science, the epistemic preconditions of democratic engagement with emerging technologies, and the limits of lexical precision as a remedy for public misinformation.

938. Two explanations of experts by experience in psychiatry: epistemic capabilities or epistemic diversity
Sugimoto, M. (2026). Two explanations of experts by experience in psychiatry: epistemic capabilities or epistemic diversity. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05862-w
Abstract (original): Abstract In the field of mental health, the role of Experts by Experience (ExE) has been gaining increasing attention. However, theoretical research on this topic remains limited, leaving it unclear how ExE can contribute to improving psychiatric research and services. In this paper, I propose two explanations for the contributions of ExE, drawing on perspectives from feminist philosophy of science. The first explanation focuses on the epistemic capabilities of ExE, grounded in standpoint theory. This approach highlights the unique individual potential of ExE as a factor that enhances psychiatric research and services. The second explanation underscores the epistemic diversity between ExE and professionals in research and/or service contexts, focusing on the value of epistemic diversity within teams and communities. The main difference is that epistemic capabilities focus on the individual, while epistemic diversity focuses on the collective. I argue that these two explanatory models should be chosen based on research design, but in the context of psychiatric PPI, considering ExE participation not only in terms of standpoint but also in terms of epistemic diversity may suggest a more inclusive approach to PPI.

947. Disability, relationships and a right against social deprivation
Clough, B. & Herring, J. (2026). Disability, relationships and a right against social deprivation. International Journal of Law in Context.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s1744552326100688
Abstract (original): Abstract Disabled people continue to experience social exclusion and denial of meaningful belonging despite legal commitments to independent living and autonomy. This paper brings disability studies, legal scholarship and philosophical debates around a right against social deprivation into conversation. We argue that the concepts of social access needs and social contribution needs provide a valuable framework for identifying harms that existing disability rights and human rights approaches often fail to recognise or adequately address. At the same time, rather than simply applying the right against social deprivation to disability, we contend that disability offers a critical lens through which the right itself must be rethought. Drawing on critical disability studies, we foreground interdependence, care, relationality and vulnerability, while exposing ableist assumptions that may shape theorising in this area. Through engagement with disability law and rights frameworks, we explore the potential of this dialogue to reconfigure understandings of rights, responsibility and justice.

948. Sexueller Zugang aus der Ferne Sexual access from a distance
Baron, T. (2026). Sexueller Zugang aus der Ferne Sexual access from a distance. Zeitschrift für Praktische Philosophie.
https://doi.org/10.22613/zfpp/13.1.1
Abstract (original): Der Konsum von Online-Pornos, eine relativ weit verbreitete Praxis, hat im Vergleich zur philosophischen Diskussion über die abstrakte Natur der Pornografie und im Vergleich zur Ethik anderer Konsumentscheidungen vergleichsweise wenig philosophische Aufmerksamkeit erfahren. In diesem Beitrag argumentiere ich, dass die Entscheidung für den Konsum solcher Medien eine Mitschuld an zutiefst schädlichen Praktiken impliziert. Angesichts der weit verbreiteten Gewalt und Ausbeutung in der Pornografieproduktion akzeptieren Konsumenten ein erhebliches Risiko, dass das konsumierte Material gefilmten Missbrauch oder Vergewaltigung zeigt. Indem man dieses Risiko durch den Konsum von Pornografie eingeht, behandelt man andere auf eine Weise als Mittel zur persönlichen Befriedigung, die ethisch nicht zu rechtfertigen ist. Im zweiten Teil des Artikels, präsentiere ich ein weiteres Problem des Konsums von Online-Pornografie – unabhängig davon, ob sie gewalttätig ist oder nicht –, da der sexuelle Zugang zu anderen durch den Konsum dieser Medien eine Form sexueller Aktivität darstellt, die den allgemein anerkannten Standards einer sinnvollen (moralisch transformierenden) Einwilligung nicht genügt.
